RTO = how fast you must be back up. RPO = how much data you're allowed to have lost.
RPO — Recovery Point Objective
The maximum amount of data loss you can tolerate, measured in time. RPO = 1 hour means "if a disaster happens, we accept losing the last hour of data." RPO = 0 means "we cannot lose any committed data" — requires synchronous replication. RPO drives backup frequency and replication lag.
RTO — Recovery Time Objective
The maximum acceptable downtime after a disaster, measured in time. RTO = 4 hours means "we must be serving traffic again within 4 hours." RTO drives infrastructure choice (cold vs warm vs hot site) and runbook automation.

11.3 The four DR site types

Different RTO/RPO targets demand different DR infrastructure. The classic taxonomy from a decade of industry practice:

DR site typeRTORPOCostWhat it is
Cold site days last backup (hours-days) $ Just backups stored offsite. Recovery means provisioning new infra and restoring.
Pilot Light hours minutes-hours $$ Minimal always-on infrastructure (DBs replicated, app stopped). Scale up on disaster.
Warm standby minutes minutes $$$ Full infra running but at reduced capacity. Scale and switch traffic on disaster.
Hot site / Active-Active seconds ≈ 0 $$$$ Full mirror running at full capacity, serving traffic. Failover is just routing change.

Cold site

The cheapest DR option. You take backups (database snapshots, S3 cross-region replication, infrastructure-as-code in Git). When disaster happens, you provision fresh infrastructure from scratch — VMs, networks, databases — and restore data. RTO measured in days. Suitable for non-critical systems where extended downtime is acceptable.

Pilot Light

The "practical default" for most regulated businesses. Infrastructure is pre-provisioned in the DR region but mostly idle: databases are continuously replicated and running, but app servers are scaled to zero or minimal. On disaster, you scale up the app tier and route traffic. RTO: under an hour. RPO: seconds to minutes (continuous replication).

Warm standby

Full DR infrastructure running at reduced capacity (e.g., 25% of production). Can serve real traffic immediately on failover, then scales up as load shifts. RTO: under 15 minutes. RPO: continuous. More expensive but faster than Pilot Light.

Hot site / Active-Active

Two (or more) regions running in parallel, serving live traffic in normal operation. A region failure removes one of N — the others continue. RTO: seconds (DNS or LB convergence time). RPO: ≈ 0 with synchronous cross-region replication. Most expensive but the only viable choice for systems that absolutely cannot tolerate downtime.

11.4 Pilot Light topology — the practical default

Because it's the most common choice for regulated businesses (Jahez fits this), let's look at a concrete topology:

Pilot Light: data tier always running and replicating (RPO ≈ seconds), app tier scales from zero on disaster (RTO ≈ 30-60 minutes).

11.5 Backup strategies — the foundation

Backups are the safety net for every DR strategy except active-active. The discipline:

Frequency drives RPO
Daily snapshots = RPO ≤ 24 hours. Continuous WAL archiving = RPO ≤ minutes. Streaming replication = RPO ≤ seconds. Pick based on tolerance for data loss.
3-2-1 rule
3 copies of data, on 2 different media, 1 offsite. Modern interpretation: production + cross-AZ replica + cross-region backup + immutable cold storage.
Test the restore, not the backup
A backup that can't be restored is no backup. Most organizations discover this during a real disaster. Test restoration quarterly: spin up a fresh DB from latest backup, verify it works, count how long it took.
Immutability
Backups must be protected from the same threats that take out production. Ransomware that encrypts your DB will encrypt mounted backups too. Use S3 Object Lock, Azure Immutable Blob, or air-gapped storage for the most critical backups.
Encryption at rest
Backups contain everything sensitive. Encrypt with keys stored in a separate KMS — and ensure the recovery process can access those keys when production is gone.

11.6 The DR runbook — what activates on disaster

For a Pilot Light topology like above, the "activate DR" runbook covers:

  1. Detect & declare. Confirm the primary region is genuinely unavailable (not a transient blip). Declare DR via incident process (§34).
  2. Promote DR database. Promote the Postgres replica in DR to primary. Confirm WAL is fully applied. Note the data position — anything written after this point in primary is lost.
  3. Scale app tier. Scale order-svc, payment-svc, etc. from 0 to production replica counts. Wait for readiness probes.
  4. Switch traffic. Update Route53 / DNS to point production hostnames at DR region. Wait for DNS TTL convergence (typically 60s).
  5. Verify business correctness. Synthetic checkout transaction. Confirm orders persist. Confirm payment provider integration works from DR network.
  6. Communicate. Status page, customer-facing messaging, executive briefing.
  7. Failback planning. Once primary returns, plan a controlled failback. Often deferred to a maintenance window — DR keeps serving in the meantime.

11.7 Game days — the most important DR investment

A DR plan that has never been tested is folklore, not engineering. The single most valuable activity in DR is the game day: a scheduled exercise where the team executes the DR runbook, ideally as if a real disaster has occurred.

  • Quarterly minimum. Less frequent and the runbook drifts from reality.
  • Rotate participants. The on-call rotation should ALL have practiced. The senior engineer who knows everything will not be the one paged at 3am.
  • Test in stages. Start with tabletop exercises (talk through scenarios). Progress to controlled failovers (real but during maintenance windows). Eventually: chaos engineering style randomized failures.
  • Track time. Measure RTO actuals. If your stated RTO is 1 hour and game days take 4 hours, your real RTO is 4 hours — admit it.
  • Update the runbook every time. Things change. Cloud provider APIs change. Your tools change. Your team changes. Each game day surfaces drift.
The dirty truth. Most organizations have a DR plan that's never been fully tested. When a real disaster happens, the runbook is half-broken — credentials are stale, the new database engine doesn't restore the same way, the network configuration in DR is missing a route. The real RTO is 5x what was promised. Game days fix this.

11.8 Synthesis — HA · FT · DR as layered defense

Now that we've covered Availability, Resilience/Fault Tolerance, and Recoverability/DR separately, the synthesis: they are not alternatives — they are layers. A serious production system uses all three, sized appropriately to the component.

The three concentric rings of resilience. Each layer handles the failures the inner layer can't.

A Jahez-shaped layered example

ComponentHAFTDR
order-svc 5 replicas across 3 AZs (N+2) — Pilot Light in me-central-2
Postgres orders DB Multi-AZ deployment Synchronous replica in same region Cross-region streaming replica + 5-min backups
Kafka events 3 brokers, RF=3, min.insync=2 Synchronous quorum writes MirrorMaker to DR region
recommendation-svc 2 replicas (N+1) — Cold backup of model artifacts only
internal admin tool 1 replica — —

Notice: not every component gets all three layers. The internal admin tool has neither HA nor DR — it's acceptable to be down during a disaster. The recommendation-svc has HA but no FT and minimal DR — it's not on the critical path. The orders DB has all three at maximum because order data loss is unrecoverable.

11.9 The connection to the rest of Part III

This three-quality framing (Availability, Resilience, Recoverability) is the architectural lens. The rest of Part III implements it:

  • Availability is measured via §28 SLI/SLO and tracked through error budgets.
  • Resilience is engineered through patterns covered in §30 Handle Phase, §31 Auto-Recovery, and §33 Cascading Failures.
  • Recoverability is operationalized via runbooks (§32 Prevent), incident response (§34), and the troubleshooting playbook (§52).

Understanding these distinctions allows us to make deliberate architectural choices, aligning our investments in resilience with the actual business impact of downtime and data loss. That's the lens to carry into the failure taxonomy and matrix in §35 and §36 — for each failure type, you can now ask not just "which phase handles this?" but "which strategy does this component need?"

The HA · FT · DR distinction in §§23–11 draws heavily on Mr Ben Abdallah's article Unpacking System Resilience: High Availability, Fault Tolerance, and Disaster Recovery (Medium, Aug 2025), which lays out the three-strategy framework with unusual clarity. Recommended reading.

§12 · PerformancePerformance / Latency

A system can be 100% available and still painfully slow. Performance is the speed quality — how fast the system responds and how much work it does per unit time. Two metrics dominate: latency (how long a single request takes) and throughput (how many requests per second). Most production incidents at scale are not "the system is down" but "the system is slow enough that users are giving up." This section covers the math, the measurement discipline, and the levers.

12.1 The two fundamental metrics

Latency — time per operation
How long does a single request take from initiation to completion? Measured in milliseconds (web), microseconds (low-latency trading), or seconds (batch jobs). User-perceived latency includes everything: network, queue wait, service time, downstream calls, response serialization.
Throughput — operations per unit time
How many requests does the system handle per second (RPS / QPS / TPS)? Measured at peak and sustained. The two metrics interact via Little's Law (§23.2) — you can't optimize one in isolation.

12.2 Little's Law — the most useful formula in capacity planning

For any stable system over the long run:

L = λ × W
L · average number of requests in the system at any moment
In-flight + waiting. The "concurrent" requests.
λ · average arrival rate (RPS)
How fast requests come in.
W · average time a request spends in the system (latency)
Queue wait + service time.

Three useful rearrangements:

You knowYou computePractical use
RPS & latencyConcurrency = λ × W"100 RPS at 200ms → 20 in flight per pod"
Concurrency & latencyCapacity = L / W"Pool size 50, requests 250ms → 200 RPS max"
RPS & concurrencyLatency = L / λ"100 in flight, 50 RPS → 2s avg latency"
The Jahez sanity check. If order-svc handles 500 RPS at p50 = 80ms, you have on average 500 × 0.080 = 40 concurrent requests in flight per pod. If your DB connection pool size is 30, you're already starving on average — let alone at peak. Little's Law tells you the pool was the bottleneck before any latency spike happened.

12.3 Percentiles, not averages — measuring what users feel

The single most important rule: never trust averages for latency. Averages hide the tail. If 99 users see 50ms and 1 user sees 5,000ms, the average is 100ms — fine on a dashboard, terrible for the affected user.

Measure percentiles instead:

PercentileMeaningWhat it tells you
p50 (median)Half users faster, half slowerTypical experience. Boring number.
p955% of users see slowerCommon-but-noticeable slow path.
p991% of users see slowerThe unlucky users — usually because of GC, cache miss, lock contention.
p99.90.1% of usersAt 1M RPS, 1,000 users/s. Real number for serious services.
maxSlowest single observationUseful for finding bugs (timeout = 30s? someone hit it).

The fan-out problem

If a page loads 10 backend calls and each is at p99 = 500ms, the probability the page sees at least one 500ms call is:

P(at least one slow) = 1 − (0.99)¹⁰ = 9.6%

Nearly 1 in 10 page loads is slow, even though each individual call is "fine 99% of the time." This is why p99 of an aggregate request matters far more than p99 of any single component, and why teams that set per-service SLOs without considering fan-out get blindsided.

12.4 The hockey stick — why p99 explodes near saturation

Latency does not grow linearly with utilization. It grows hyperbolically. The classic M/M/1 queue formula:

average wait time ≈ service_time × ρ / (1 − ρ)
                  where ρ = utilization = arrival_rate / service_rate
Utilization (ρ)Wait time multiplierSensation
50%1× service timeHealthy. Latency stable.
70%2.3× service timeStill fine. p99 only ~2× p50.
80%4× service timep99 starts pulling away from p50.
90%9× service timep99 unstable. Small load increases = big jumps.
95%19× service timeCliff. Spikes cause minute-long queues.
99%99× service timeDegenerate. System effectively broken.
The hockey stick: latency stays roughly flat until utilization crosses ~70%, then climbs hyperbolically. The 70% rule is "stay left of the cliff." See §26 for capacity planning that respects this curve.

This is why "my CPU is only at 75% why is everything slow" is asked every week somewhere on Earth. The answer: queues, not CPU. Once you cross ~70% utilization on any single resource (CPU, connection pool, thread pool, queue), wait time explodes faster than linear.

12.5 The latency budget — accounting for every millisecond

For any user-facing transaction, set a total latency budget and divide it across components. Otherwise components individually look fine but the total breaks.

Example: Jahez checkout has a 500ms p99 latency SLO. Decompose:

Total budget: 500ms
  ├── Network (mobile RTT)        50ms   # nothing to do here
  ├── API gateway routing          5ms   # bounded
  ├── auth-svc                    20ms   # JWT validation
  ├── order-svc business logic    80ms   # includes own DB query
  ├── payment-svc                 250ms  # external mada call
  ├── persistence write           40ms   # DB insert + WAL flush
  ├── audit log emit               5ms   # async
  └── response serialization      10ms
                                   ───
                          total:  460ms  # 40ms safety margin

This budget is the contract. If payment-svc starts taking 350ms, you've blown the SLO — even if no individual service "broke." Performance work is now: do you optimize payment-svc, parallelize the auth-svc call, or negotiate a higher latency target?

12.6 The classic optimization patterns

PatternReducesCost
CachingService time, downstream callsCache invalidation complexity
ParallelizationTotal latency (when calls are independent)More concurrent connections
Connection poolingConnection setup timeMemory for idle connections
Indexing (DB)Query timeWrite overhead, storage
Pagination / limitsService time, payload sizeUX complexity
CompressionNetwork timeCPU for compress/decompress
HTTP/2 / gRPCConnection overhead, head-of-line blockingOperational complexity
CDN / edge cachingNetwork distanceCache invalidation, cost
Async processingPerceived latencyEventual consistency (§12)
Hedged requestsTail latency p992× the load on downstreams

Hedging — the tail-latency trick

The clever pattern from Google's "The Tail at Scale" (Dean & Barroso): for tail-latency-sensitive requests, send the same request to TWO replicas after a short delay (say, p95 latency). Whichever replies first wins; cancel the other. This dramatically cuts p99 because you're no longer at the mercy of a single slow replica.

// Pseudocode
async function hedgedCall(req) {
  const primary = backend.call(req);

  // Wait p95 latency before hedging
  const hedgeTimer = sleep(100);  // 100ms = our p95

  const result = await Promise.race([primary, hedgeTimer]);
  if (result === hedgeTimer) {
    // primary is slow — race a hedge
    const hedge = backend.call(req, { replica: 'different' });
    return Promise.race([primary, hedge]);
  }
  return result;
}

Cost: doubles the load on backends for slow-path requests. Only use for high-value tail-sensitive paths, never for everything.

12.7 The latency SLI definition

Define your latency SLI precisely. The standard formulation:

# Prometheus — fraction of requests served under threshold
sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{
  app="order-svc", le="0.5"
}[5m]))
/
sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{
  app="order-svc"
}[5m]))

This gives you "% of requests faster than 500ms" — a number you can SLO directly (target 99%, alert on burn rate per §21).

12.8 Performance vs the other qualities

Performance trades against everything else:

  • Performance vs Durability — synchronous replication (§22) adds latency. Async is faster but loses recent writes on failure.
  • Performance vs Consistency — strong consistency (§12) requires coordination, which costs latency. Eventual consistency is faster.
  • Performance vs Cost — more replicas, faster instances, hotter caches all cost money. The 70% rule means you pay for 30% idle headroom (§19).
  • Performance vs Maintainability — clever optimizations are often hard to maintain. Knuth: "premature optimization is the root of all evil."

Performance work is fundamentally about deciding which axis to give up to gain speed. The next sections (§24 Scalability, §25 Elasticity, §26 Capacity) cover how to scale capacity to meet demand without killing latency in the process.

§13 · PerformanceScalability

Scalability is the ability to handle growing load by adding resources. It's a property — not a thing you do, but a way the system responds to investment. A scalable system handles 10× the load by costing roughly 10× more (and not 100×). A non-scalable one starts melting at 2× and no amount of money fixes it. The skill is recognizing which kind you have, and what you can do about it.

13.1 The two dimensions — vertical vs horizontal

Vertical scaling has a hard ceiling and rapidly increasing cost. Horizontal scaling has a softer ceiling and roughly linear cost — but only if your system architecture supports it.

13.2 Vertical scaling — when it makes sense

  • Stateful single-master databases (classic Postgres primary, Redis primary). You can't trivially split them; bigger machine is the easiest path.
  • Memory-bound workloads (large in-memory caches, ML model serving). One big machine with 1 TB RAM is simpler than 10 machines coordinating 100 GB each.
  • Latency-sensitive workloads where adding a network hop hurts more than running on a beefier box.

The catch: hardware has limits. You hit them. AWS's biggest instance (u7-class) is impressive but finite. And cost climbs super-linearly — a 4xlarge isn't 4× the cost of an xlarge, it's 4-5× depending on the family.

13.3 Horizontal scaling — the path most software takes

Add more instances behind a load balancer. Each handles a fraction of total load. This works beautifully for stateless services because any instance can serve any request.

The stateless requirement

Horizontal scaling demands that no instance hold session state another instance might need. Patterns that make services stateless:

  • JWT-based auth instead of server-side sessions — token carries identity
  • External session stores (Redis, Memcached) — any instance reads from shared store
  • Database-backed state — session in Postgres or DynamoDB
  • Event sourcing — state derived from event log readable by any instance

The K8s implementation

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata: { name: order-svc, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  replicas: 10                  # scale this number → scale the service
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: order-svc }
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: { app: order-svc }
    spec:
      topologySpreadConstraints:
      - maxSkew: 1
        topologyKey: topology.kubernetes.io/zone
        whenUnsatisfiable: DoNotSchedule
        labelSelector:
          matchLabels: { app: order-svc }
      containers:
      - name: app
        image: ghcr.io/jahez/order-svc:v2.15.0
        resources:
          requests: { cpu: "500m", memory: "512Mi" }
          limits:   { cpu: "1000m", memory: "1Gi" }

The Service in front round-robins across all 10 pods. Doubling load = doubling replicas. Linear scaling, in theory.

13.4 Universal Scalability Law — why "linear" is a lie

Real systems don't actually scale linearly. Neil Gunther's Universal Scalability Law (USL) models how throughput scales with concurrency:

throughput(N) = N × λ / (1 + α(N − 1) + β × N × (N − 1))

α = contention coefficient   (waiting for shared resources)
β = coherency coefficient    (cost of keeping replicas consistent)
N = number of workers/replicas
α — contention
How much workers fight over shared resources (a single DB, a global lock). Linear scaling penalty. Doubling replicas with high α gives less than 2× throughput.
β — coherency
How much workers must coordinate to stay consistent (cache invalidation, distributed consensus, replication). Quadratic penalty. Past a certain N, adding workers reduces total throughput.
Three scaling curves: ideal linear (theory), contention-limited (Amdahl-like), and coherency-limited (USL). Most real systems exhibit both penalties, peaking and then declining as N grows.

The practical takeaways

  • If your bottleneck is the database, adding more app pods does almost nothing — you've saturated the shared resource (high α).
  • If your service uses distributed locks or strong consistency, scaling out has a ceiling — and beyond it, performance degrades (high β).
  • The fix isn't always "scale wider" — sometimes it's "shard the bottleneck" (per-tenant DB, per-region cache) so contention drops.

13.5 The shared-bottleneck problem

You can have 100 app servers, but if all of them write to one Postgres primary, your write throughput is bounded by what one Postgres can do. Adding more app servers helps reads (you can cache) but does nothing for writes.

Common shared bottlenecks:

ResourceWhere it bottlenecksHow to scale past it
Single DB primaryWrite throughputSharding, multi-master DB (CockroachDB, Spanner)
Single RedisRead+write throughputRedis Cluster (sharded), or replicas for reads
Distributed lockCoordination throughputShard the lock; use lease-based instead of mutex
Message queue (single partition)Producer/consumer rateMore partitions; consumer groups
External API rate limitThroughput capNegotiate higher limits; cache; batch
Network bandwidthCross-AZ transferLocal caching; data locality
Synchronous downstream callCombined response timeMake async; batch; cache

13.6 Sharding — splitting the bottleneck

The fundamental scaling pattern when a single resource can't grow more: sharding. Split the data (or work) into independent partitions; each partition lives on a different machine.

shard_id = hash(user_id) % NUM_SHARDS

  user u_88421 → shard 3 → postgres-3
  user u_91022 → shard 7 → postgres-7
  user u_88422 → shard 4 → postgres-4

Each shard handles 1/N of total load. Independent. No contention.

Strategies:

  • Hash sharding — hash a key, modulo shard count. Even distribution but resharding is painful.
  • Range sharding — split by key range (orders A-F → shard 1, G-M → shard 2). Cleaner ranges, but hot ranges create hot shards.
  • Consistent hashing — hash with a ring topology. Resharding moves only ~1/N keys.
  • Geographic sharding — data lives where users live. Riyadh users on me-central-1, Jeddah on me-central-2. Latency wins, but cross-region queries are expensive.

13.7 Read replicas vs sharding — different problems

 Read replicasSharding
SolvesRead throughputWrite throughput AND data size
ComplexityLow — just point reads at replicasHigh — application must know which shard
ConsistencyReplication lag (eventual)Per-shard strong, cross-shard hard
When to useRead-heavy workloadsWhen write rate exceeds single-master capacity

Most applications can scale a long way on read replicas alone. Sharding is the move when your writes exceed what a single master can do — which for modern Postgres is somewhere around 10K-100K transactions per second depending on workload.

13.8 The Jahez scaling story

Realistic scaling progression for a fast-growing service:

  1. Day 1: 1 monolith app + 1 Postgres. 100 RPS easy.
  2. Year 1, 1K RPS: 5 app instances + 1 Postgres + Redis cache. App is stateless; caching reduces DB load.
  3. Year 2, 5K RPS: 20 app instances + 1 Postgres primary + 3 read replicas + Redis Cluster. Reads now scale horizontally.
  4. Year 3, 20K RPS: Microservices split (orders / payments / catalog), each with own DB. 100+ instances. Services scale independently.
  5. Year 5, 100K RPS: Order DB sharded by city. Riyadh/Jeddah/Dammam shards independent. Cross-shard analytics via Kafka → data warehouse.

Notice: each step is a different pattern, triggered by a different bottleneck. The skill of scalability engineering is recognizing which bottleneck you're hitting and applying the right pattern, not blindly "adding more replicas."

13.9 Scalability vs Elasticity — they're different

People conflate these. They're related but distinct:

  • Scalability (this section) — CAN you handle more load by adding resources? Yes/No question. A property of architecture.
  • Elasticity (§25) — DO resources adjust automatically as load changes? A property of the operational system.

A system can be scalable but not elastic — you can add capacity, but you have to do it manually. A system can be elastic only because it's scalable — auto-scaling presupposes the architecture can absorb the new instances. Section §25 covers the operational mechanics of doing it automatically.

§14 · PerformanceElasticity

Elasticity is the ability of a system to automatically adjust resources up AND down as demand changes. Static "always 20 replicas" is scalable but not elastic — you pay for peak capacity 24/7. An elastic system runs 5 replicas at 3 AM and 50 replicas at lunch peak, scales smoothly between those, and never wakes anyone up to do it. Done well, elasticity makes the cost-vs-availability tradeoff much friendlier.

14.1 The elasticity dimensions

What scalesHow fastK8s mechanism
Replicas (horizontal)Seconds to ~1 minuteHorizontalPodAutoscaler (HPA)
Pod resources (vertical)Pod restart requiredVerticalPodAutoscaler (VPA)
Cluster nodesMinutes (cloud provisioning)Cluster Autoscaler / Karpenter

A complete elastic system uses all three: HPA scales pod count for the workload's normal range, VPA right-sizes individual pods, and cluster-autoscaler adds/removes nodes to make room.

14.2 HorizontalPodAutoscaler — the workhorse

HPA watches a metric (CPU, memory, custom) and adjusts replica count to keep the metric near a target. The classic example:

apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: order-svc, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: order-svc
  minReplicas: 5                    # floor — never below this
  maxReplicas: 50                   # ceiling — protect downstream
  metrics:
  - type: Resource
    resource:
      name: cpu
      target:
        type: Utilization
        averageUtilization: 70      # target 70% — the magic number (§26)
  behavior:
    scaleUp:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 0   # scale up fast
      policies:
      - type: Percent
        value: 100                    # up to 100% increase per period
        periodSeconds: 30
    scaleDown:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 300 # scale down slow (5min cooldown)
      policies:
      - type: Percent
        value: 10                     # max 10% reduction per period
        periodSeconds: 60

The asymmetric scaling rule

Scale up fast. Scale down slow. The asymmetry above is intentional. Slow scale-up = users see overload before relief arrives. Slow scale-down = wasted money but stable system. Fast scale-down = you remove capacity right before the next spike. Always: aggressive scale-up policies, conservative scale-down policies.

14.3 The right metric to scale on

CPU utilization is the default but often wrong. The metric should be the one that actually correlates with user load:

WorkloadBest metricWhy
Web API (CPU-bound)CPU utilizationDirect correlation with request load
Web API (IO-bound)RPS or in-flight requestsCPU stays low even under heavy load — wrong signal
Worker pulling from queueQueue depth (or lag)Queue grows = need more workers
WebSocket / long-poll serverActive connectionsConnections persist regardless of CPU
ML inferenceGPU utilization or batch fillGPU is the bottleneck, not CPU
Memory-heavy cacheWorking set sizeNeed more capacity = more cache space

Custom metrics example — scaling on Kafka lag

For a Kafka consumer service, scaling on CPU is wrong — the right signal is "how far behind we are":

apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: order-events-consumer }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: order-events-consumer
  minReplicas: 3
  maxReplicas: 30
  metrics:
  - type: External
    external:
      metric:
        name: kafka_consumergroup_lag
        selector:
          matchLabels:
            consumergroup: order-processors
            topic: order.events
      target:
        type: AverageValue
        averageValue: "1000"           # lag > 1000 msgs/consumer → scale up

Requires a metrics adapter (Prometheus Adapter, KEDA, Stackdriver) to expose external metrics. KEDA in particular has built-in scalers for Kafka, RabbitMQ, AWS SQS, and dozens of others.

14.4 VerticalPodAutoscaler — right-sizing pods

VPA watches actual resource usage and adjusts pod requests/limits. Useful when you don't know the right size or when workload characteristics change.

apiVersion: autoscaling.k8s.io/v1
kind: VerticalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: order-svc-vpa }
spec:
  targetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: order-svc
  updatePolicy:
    updateMode: "Off"           # start in recommendation-only mode
  resourcePolicy:
    containerPolicies:
    - containerName: "*"
      minAllowed:  { cpu: "100m", memory: "128Mi" }
      maxAllowed:  { cpu: "4",    memory: "4Gi"   }
Don't use VPA and HPA on the same metric. If both are tuning CPU usage, they fight: VPA grows the pod, which lowers CPU%, which causes HPA to scale down replicas. Scale ONE dimension automatically: HPA on CPU, or VPA on CPU, not both. The classic combination is HPA on RPS and VPA on memory — they don't conflict.

14.5 Cluster autoscaling — making room for the pods

HPA scaling up is useless if there are no nodes to schedule the new pods on. Cluster Autoscaler (CA) and the more modern Karpenter watch for unschedulable pods and provision new nodes:

The full elastic scale-up path. HPA reaction is fast; cluster provisioning is the slow link. For traffic patterns where you can't tolerate that gap, keep buffer capacity (higher minReplicas, warm node pools).

Karpenter vs Cluster Autoscaler

  • Cluster Autoscaler (legacy): works with predefined node groups. Slow to provision (~5 min). Limited instance type flexibility.
  • Karpenter (modern): direct EC2 / GCE provisioning. Picks the cheapest available instance type that fits the pending pods. ~60s provisioning. Spot-aware. Cost-aware bin-packing.

For new clusters, Karpenter is the right choice. It's significantly faster and tends to use 30-50% less compute through smarter packing.

14.6 The cold-start problem

Scaling out adds new instances, but new instances are slower than warm ones:

  • JIT compilation — first 100 requests through a JVM/CLR are 10× slower
  • Connection pools empty — each connection needs the TCP+TLS handshake
  • In-memory cache empty — cache misses force backend round-trips
  • JIT-compiled hot paths — only get hot after sustained traffic

If you scale from 5 to 15 pods in response to a load spike, those 10 new pods serve the spike at degraded performance. The warm 5 pods carry disproportionate load, may overload, and the new pods may timeout-and-restart in a death spiral.

Mitigations:

  • Higher minReplicas — keep enough warm capacity that scale-out is just additive, not the difference between "working" and "broken"
  • Pre-warming — startup probe drives synthetic traffic until JIT/caches are ready, before pod becomes Ready
  • Predictive scaling — for known load patterns (lunch peak, event releases), scale up before the spike (cron-based HPA or KEDA scheduled scalers)
  • Graceful ramp-up — service mesh or LB gradually increases traffic to new pods rather than slamming them

14.7 Predictable patterns vs reactive scaling

Most user traffic isn't truly unpredictable. Lunch peaks happen at lunch. Ramadan iftar spikes happen at sunset. Weekends look different from weekdays. Reactive scaling alone is insufficient for predictable patterns — you'll always be 1-3 minutes behind the curve.

Combine reactive (HPA) with scheduled scaling:

# KEDA cron scaler — pre-warm before lunch peak
apiVersion: keda.sh/v1alpha1
kind: ScaledObject
metadata: { name: order-svc-cron }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    name: order-svc
  minReplicaCount: 5
  maxReplicaCount: 50
  triggers:
  - type: cron
    metadata:
      timezone: Asia/Riyadh
      start: "0 11 * * *"           # 11:00 — pre-warm before lunch
      end:   "0 15 * * *"           # 15:00 — return to baseline
      desiredReplicas: "20"
  - type: cpu                       # standard reactive on top
    metricType: Utilization
    metadata:
      value: "70"

14.8 Spot instances and elasticity

Spot/preemptible instances are 60-80% cheaper than on-demand but can be reclaimed with ~2 minutes notice. Elastic systems with proper redundancy can absorb spot interruptions:

apiVersion: karpenter.k8s.aws/v1
kind: NodePool
metadata: { name: spot-pool }
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      requirements:
      - key: karpenter.sh/capacity-type
        operator: In
        values: [spot]                # prefer spot
      - key: kubernetes.io/arch
        operator: In
        values: [amd64, arm64]        # either CPU arch
      expireAfter: 720h                  # 30d — replace nodes for security
  disruption:
    consolidationPolicy: WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized
    consolidateAfter: 30s

Combine with PodDisruptionBudgets to guarantee availability during spot interruptions: "always keep at least N pods running, even if a node is going away."

14.9 The economics of elasticity

The dollar payoff of elasticity vs static provisioning:

ApproachCapacity 24/7Cost (relative)
Static for peak (no elasticity)50 replicas always1.00× (baseline)
Elastic, on-demand instances5-50 as needed~0.40× (60% savings)
Elastic + spot for stateless5-50 as needed, spot-priced~0.20× (80% savings)
Elastic + spot + savings plans for baseline5 reserved + spot for elastic~0.15× (85% savings)

For a service whose static-peak cost is SAR 200K/month, full elasticity discipline can drop it to SAR 30K — a SAR 170K/month saving with zero impact on availability if engineered well.

14.10 The trap — when elasticity hides problems

Auto-scaling is a powerful tool but it can paper over real engineering problems. The signs you're abusing it:

  • HPA constantly at maxReplicas. Not elasticity — your maxReplicas is set wrong, OR there's a real performance regression you're masking with capacity.
  • Constant scale flapping. Replica count oscillates every 30s. Symptoms of bad target metrics or insufficient stabilization windows.
  • Cost climbing despite no traffic growth. Auto-scaler is hiding a memory leak, slow query regression, or downstream slowdown that's making each request more expensive.
  • Mysterious latency spikes during scale-up. Cold-start problem (§25.6) — you're solving load with new pods that themselves degrade performance.

Treat auto-scaling as a comfort layer over a properly engineered system, not a substitute for proper engineering. The next section (§26 Capacity Planning) covers the discipline of knowing what your system can handle before auto-scaling has to react.

§15 · PerformanceCapacity / Capacity Planning

Capacity planning is the discipline of knowing what your system can handle before you need to know. Scalability (§24) tells you the system can grow; elasticity (§25) tells you it will grow automatically; capacity planning tells you how much you actually need, where the bottlenecks are, and when you'll hit the wall. Done well, you never get surprised. Done poorly, your auto-scaler hits maxReplicas at 11:30 AM during the lunch rush and the on-call is in a war room.

15.1 The four questions capacity planning answers

  1. What's our current capacity? — At what RPS does each service start to degrade? What's the actual breaking point (not the "feels OK" point)?
  2. What's our headroom? — How far is current load from breaking? Are we 50% utilized or 90%?
  3. How fast is demand growing? — At current trajectory, when do we hit the ceiling?
  4. What's the bottleneck? — When we hit the wall, which resource breaks first? CPU? Memory? Connection pool? Database?

Most teams can answer 1 and 2 reasonably well. 3 and 4 require deliberate practice and tooling.

15.2 The 70% rule — why headroom is non-negotiable

From the latency-vs-utilization curve in §23.4: latency stays roughly flat until utilization crosses ~70%, then climbs hyperbolically. The operational consequence: plan capacity so steady-state utilization stays below 70%.

Why 70% specifically? Little's Law math: to survive a 2× traffic spike at the knee, you need utilization ≤ 50%. To survive a 1.5× spike, ≤ 67%. 70% is a battle-tested compromise: enough headroom to absorb realistic spikes, not so much that you're paying for idle infrastructure. For traffic-spiky businesses (e.g., daily lunch peak in food delivery), use 50-60%. For predictable steady-state systems, 75-80% is fine.

15.3 Knee detection — finding where YOUR system breaks

Theory tells you 70%; finding YOUR system's actual knee requires measurement. The protocol:

  1. Load test with steadily increasing RPS. Don't ramp instantly — ramp over 5-10 minutes to let queues settle at each level.
  2. Plot RPS vs p99 latency. The knee is where p99 starts to detach sharply from the trend line.
  3. Set max sustained traffic at 70% of the knee. The other 30% is your spike absorption budget.
  4. Re-run quarterly — your system's knee shifts as code, dependencies, and data scale.
Find the knee through load testing, then set your capacity ceiling at 70% of it. Sized correctly, you can survive a 1.4× spike without latency degradation.

15.4 Capacity for what? — the per-resource view

"Capacity" isn't one number; it's the minimum across all resources. Whatever runs out first is your true capacity. Common bottlenecks:

ResourceSymptom when fullWhere to measure
CPUp99 climbs; throttling visible in cgroup metricscontainer_cpu_usage_seconds_total
MemoryOOMKilled; swap thrashing; GC death spiralcontainer_memory_working_set_bytes
DB connection pool"connection pool timeout" errorsApp-emitted pool metrics
Thread poolRequests pile up in queue; latency explodesApp-emitted thread pool metrics
File descriptors"too many open files"; new connections failprocess_open_fds
Network bandwidthCross-AZ packets dropped; nginx 502sCloud provider network metrics
External rate limit429s from third-party APIPer-vendor metrics
Disk IOPSfsync slow; write latency climbsnode_disk_io_time_seconds_total

The cardinal rule: measure utilization of every constrained resource, not just CPU. CPU is what's easiest to graph, not necessarily what runs out first.

15.5 Bound every queue — the universal capacity rule

Every queue, in every system, at every layer, MUST have a maximum size. The size depends on the workload, but some max is non-negotiable. Without a bound, you've turned latency growth into memory growth, which becomes an OOM, which becomes a cascading failure (§33).

Queue typeHow to bound
Java BlockingQueueUse ArrayBlockingQueue(capacity), never LinkedBlockingQueue() with no size
Go channelsBuffered channels with explicit size: make(chan T, 1000)
Node streamshighWaterMark option on Writable / Transform streams
RabbitMQ queuesx-max-length argument; configure overflow behavior (reject vs drop-head)
KafkaPartition count × retention sets effective bound; producer buffer.memory bounds in-flight
HTTP server thread poolsTomcat maxThreads, nginx worker_connections, libuv defaults
DB connection poolsAlways set maxConnections — don't rely on the DB's max as your client-side bound

15.6 Backpressure — the flow-control discipline

When a fast producer meets a slow consumer, something has to give. The wrong answer is "buffer it all" — that's how unbounded queues, unbounded latency, and OOM crashes are born. The right answer is backpressure: the consumer signals "I'm full," and the producer slows or rejects new work.

Three flow-control strategies for a full queue:

StrategyBehaviorWhen to use
Block (push backpressure)Producer waits until queue has spaceIn-process pipelines; producer can wait
Reject (load shedding)Return 429 / 503 immediatelyHTTP APIs, public ingress
Drop (lossy)Discard message silently or with metricTelemetry, low-priority events

The wrong answer: buffer indefinitely. That's not flow control — it's deferred failure.

HTTP-level backpressure — 429 and 503

// Express.js — load shedding with explicit backpressure
const activeRequests = new Map();
const MAX_CONCURRENT = 100;

app.use((req, res, next) => {
  if (activeRequests.size >= MAX_CONCURRENT) {
    metrics.increment('http.shed');
    res.set('Retry-After', '2');
    return res.status(503).json({ error: 'overloaded, retry' });
  }
  const id = req.id;
  activeRequests.set(id, Date.now());
  res.on('finish', () => activeRequests.delete(id));
  next();
});

15.7 Capacity forecasting — when will you hit the wall?

Knowing your current capacity isn't enough — you need to know when you'll outgrow it. The simplest approach: linear extrapolation.

# Prometheus — forecast when peak RPS will hit a threshold
predict_linear(http_requests_total{app="order-svc"}[7d], 30 * 86400)
# Returns predicted RPS in 30 days based on last 7 days of data

For more sophisticated planning:

  • Year-over-year growth. Take peak hour from same week last year, multiply by your business growth rate, that's projected peak.
  • Event-aware forecasting. Ramadan iftar / shopping events / product launches are NOT linear. Get historical data from prior events.
  • Multi-dimensional capacity. Forecast each resource separately — RPS might grow 30% but data volume grows 50% (each user has more orders); the bottleneck might shift.

15.8 N+M for capacity planning — design for the worst case

Capacity planning must assume failures during peak. The N+M rule from §10:

  • N+1 — survives single failure. Default for non-critical services.
  • N+2 — survives a failure during a deploy. Default for critical services.

Concretely: if peak load needs 10 pods, deploy 12 (N+2). At peak, with 2 instances down (one dead, one rolling), you still have 10 serving — barely. If you only had 11 (N+1), the deploy itself takes you over the cliff.

15.9 Asymmetric capacity — the hidden multiplier

The asymmetry rule from cascading failures (§33). A service healthy at 10K RPS that crashes at 11K RPS may need to drop to ~1K RPS to recover (10% of normal). This is because crashed/cold instances don't immediately serve at full capacity. Plan capacity for the recovery scenario, not just the steady state. If you have 10 pods normally, you may need headroom for 100 pods during recovery from a cascade.

15.10 Capacity reviews — the discipline

Mature SRE teams run quarterly capacity reviews. The agenda:

  1. Current state per service: peak RPS, peak utilization (CPU, memory, etc.), distance from knee.
  2. Forecast: projected peak in 3 months, 6 months, 12 months.
  3. Bottlenecks: which resource breaks first if traffic doubles? What's the migration path?
  4. Cost projection: at projected scale, what's the infrastructure spend?
  5. Action items: what work needs to start NOW to avoid hitting the wall in 6 months?

The output is a planning document that the on-call team and management both reference. Without it, the on-call team is surprised by capacity events; with it, they're prepared.

15.11 The Jahez calibration

A realistic capacity plan for an order-svc service:

CURRENT (Q4 2025)
  Peak RPS:           4,200  (during 13:00 lunch peak)
  Avg RPS:            1,800
  CPU at peak:        62%
  DB pool at peak:    78%       # bottleneck
  Knee from load test:5,800 RPS
  Headroom:           38% (4,200 / 5,800 = 72% of knee)

FORECAST (Q4 2026, +50% YoY)
  Peak RPS:           6,300
  Headroom:          NEGATIVE — exceeds knee by 9%

ACTIONS BY Q2 2026
  - Scale DB read replicas 3 → 5            (low risk, ~SAR 30K/mo)
  - Increase DB pool size 30 → 50           (DB also needs upgrade)
  - Add caching layer for hot read paths    (effort: 2 sprints)
  - Re-run load test post-changes; expect knee at ~9,000 RPS

Notice: the bottleneck is NOT app CPU (62%) — it's the DB connection pool (78%). Adding more app pods would do nothing for write throughput. Knowing the bottleneck IS the capacity plan.

15.12 Capacity in context

Capacity is the bridge between performance (§23), scalability (§24), and elasticity (§25). Performance tells you how the system behaves; scalability tells you it can grow; elasticity tells you it grows automatically; capacity planning tells you how much and when. Without it, the auto-scaler hits its ceiling at the worst possible moment, and you find out where the bottleneck was — in production, at 11:30 AM, while the on-call is in a war room.

§16 · DataDurability

Durability is the probability that committed data survives over time. Independent of availability — a system can be temporarily unreachable yet 100% durable, or always available yet lose data on every restart. Durability is what makes the difference between "we had an incident" and "we lost customer orders." Measured in "data 9s" the way availability is measured in uptime 9s, but the math is different and the engineering investments are different.

16.1 The crisp definition

Durability = the probability that data, once acknowledged as written, will still be readable at any point in the future.

Durability = 1 − P(data loss in a given period)

The standard reference: Amazon S3's Standard tier offers 99.999999999% (eleven 9s) annual durability. That means: if you store 10 million objects, statistically you'd expect to lose one object every 10,000 years.

Compare to a single hard drive: typical annual failure rate is 1-2%. Single-disk storage is at best 99% durable per year — losing roughly 1% of data annually if you have no redundancy. Eleven 9s versus two 9s is a billion-fold difference.

16.2 Durability vs Availability — different problems, different math

 AvailabilityDurability
QuestionIs the system responding right now?Will my data be there later?
Failure modeService down (recoverable)Data lost (unrecoverable)
Time scaleSeconds to hoursYears to decades
Engineering leverRedundant compute, failoverReplication, backups, integrity checks
Cost driverStandby capacityStorage volume × replication factor
Worst caseOutage — fixed when service returnsData loss — never recovered
The asymmetric tradeoff. An hour of downtime is recoverable — users wait, business resumes. A lost order is permanent — the customer is gone, the revenue is gone, possibly trust is gone. Durability investments deserve different priority than availability investments. Most experienced SREs prioritize durability investments first because the failure mode is unforgivable.

16.3 The data 9s

DurabilityAnnual data lossTypical achieved by
99% (two 9s)1% per yearSingle disk, no backup
99.9% (three 9s)0.1% per yearRAID-1 mirror
99.99% (four 9s)0.01% per yearRAID-6, single-region replication
99.999% (five 9s)0.001%Multi-AZ replication, daily backups
99.99999% (seven 9s)0.00001%Three-way replication + cross-region backup
99.999999999% (eleven 9s)≈ 1 in 10⁹/yearS3 Standard — multi-AZ, erasure-coded

Each additional durability 9 typically requires ~10× more engineering investment in independent failure domains. Two copies on the same rack = same failure domain (one outage kills both). Two copies in different regions = independent. The independence matters more than the count.

16.4 The engineering levers

Durability is engineered through four primary mechanisms, often combined:

① Replication — multiple copies

Store the data in N places. If the durability of one copy is D, the durability of N independent copies is roughly 1 − (1−D)^N. Three independent copies at 99% each gives 99.9999% combined.

Critical caveat: independence. Three replicas in the same rack is roughly the same as one replica — they share fate. Three replicas in three AZs are mostly independent. Three replicas in three regions are fully independent.

② Erasure coding — replication's smarter sibling

Instead of storing 3 full copies (200% storage overhead), erasure coding splits data into chunks plus parity, e.g., 6 data + 3 parity (50% overhead). Any 6 of 9 chunks reconstruct the data. Same durability as triple-replication but at half the storage cost.

Used in S3 internally, HDFS, Ceph, MinIO. The tradeoff: more CPU to read/write and slower partial-failure recovery.

③ Backups — point-in-time snapshots

Replicas protect against hardware failure. They don't protect against logical corruption — a bug deletes 100K rows and that delete replicates instantly. Backups are the safety net for "the data was correct yesterday and we need to get back there."

  • Full backups — complete snapshot. Slow to take, fast to restore.
  • Incremental backups — only changed blocks since last backup. Fast to take, requires the chain to restore.
  • Continuous WAL archiving — write-ahead log streamed to backup storage. RPO ≈ seconds. PostgreSQL standard.
  • Cross-region backup — backup lives in a different region than primary. Survives regional disaster.
  • Immutable backups — write-once-read-many storage that ransomware can't encrypt. S3 Object Lock, Azure Immutable Blob.

④ Integrity checking — detecting corruption

Data can rot silently. Bit flips happen. Checksums detect this:

  • Block-level checksums — every disk block has a CRC. Re-validated on read. ZFS, Btrfs do this natively.
  • End-to-end checksums — content hash stored alongside. Validated on every read by clients (S3 ETags work this way).
  • Periodic scrubbing — background process rereads all data, compares to checksums, reconstructs corruptions from replicas.

16.5 The 3-2-1 rule

The classic data protection rule, still relevant in 2026:

3 copies of your data
2 different storage media (or types)
1 offsite copy

Modern interpretation for cloud-native systems:

  • 3 copies — production primary + cross-AZ replica + cross-region backup
  • 2 different types — primary on EBS (block) + backup on S3 (object) gives independent failure modes
  • 1 offsite — backup in a different region (or a different cloud, for the truly paranoid)

Some shops add a fourth: 1 immutable copy on write-once storage that ransomware cannot encrypt. As ransomware attacks have grown, this has moved from paranoia to baseline.

16.6 Synchronous vs asynchronous replication

The single most consequential durability decision: when does the primary acknowledge a write?

Synchronous: replica has the data before client gets ack. Async: client gets ack first; replication catches up. The latency-vs-durability tradeoff in one picture.

When to choose which

WorkloadChoiceWhy
Financial transactions, ordersSynchronousLost order = lost revenue. Pay the latency cost.
User profile updatesAsync with monitoringSlight loss tolerable; speed matters for UX.
Analytics eventsAsync, batchedSome loss acceptable; throughput matters more.
Audit logs, complianceSynchronousRegulatory — cannot lose evidence.
Cache (no source of truth)Async or noneLoss is tolerable — rebuilt from primary.
Cross-region disaster recoveryAsync (continuous)Sync across regions = unacceptable latency.

16.7 PostgreSQL durability — the gold standard reference

Modern Postgres demonstrates the engineering levers concretely:

# postgresql.conf — the durability dials
synchronous_commit = on               # default: wait for WAL flush before ack
synchronous_standby_names = 'replica1,replica2'  # wait for replica too
wal_level = replica                   # enable WAL streaming
max_wal_senders = 10                  # number of replicas allowed
archive_mode = on                     # archive WAL for PITR
archive_command = 'aws s3 cp %p s3://jahez-wal-archive/%f'

# Recovery flexibility
full_page_writes = on                 # protect against partial-page writes
fsync = on                            # NEVER turn off in production
checkpoint_timeout = 5min            # bounded WAL recovery time

The settings represent durability decisions made deliberately:

  • synchronous_commit = on — durability over latency. Each commit waits for WAL fsync to local disk.
  • synchronous_standby_names — durability across failure domains. Wait for replica acks too. Latency cost: cross-AZ network roundtrip per commit.
  • archive_command — every WAL segment is shipped to S3 → cross-region durability + point-in-time recovery capability.
  • fsync = on — never disable. Some teams turn this off "for performance"; they're choosing to lose data on power failure. The choice is rarely worth it.

16.8 The Jahez durability calibration

DataDurability targetMechanism
Orders (Postgres)Eleven 9s effectiveSync replication multi-AZ + cross-region streaming + S3 WAL archive + 7-day backups
Payment recordsEleven 9s + immutableAbove + S3 Object Lock for compliance retention (regulator requirement)
User profilesNine 9sMulti-AZ replication, daily backup, no immutable
Session tokensNone (recreatable)Single-region Redis, no backup needed
Recommendation modelsEight 9sS3 Standard storage; can retrain from analytics if lost
Cache (Redis)None — ephemeral by designLost cache rebuilds on demand from source DB
Logs (operational)Six 9s, 30-day retentionLoki backed by S3; old logs auto-expire
Audit logsEleven 9s, 7-year retentionS3 Object Lock + cross-region; regulatory requirement

Key insight: not every dataset deserves the same durability investment. Audit logs need 11 9s for 7 years; session tokens need zero durability (they regenerate on login). Match the investment to the cost-of-loss.

16.9 Restore testing — the unspoken durability requirement

A backup that has never been restored is folklore, not protection. The dirty truth: most organizations don't discover their backups are broken until they need them. The discipline:

  • Quarterly restore drills — pull last night's backup, restore to a test instance, verify business correctness.
  • Track restore time — how long does it take? This is your real RTO for data restoration scenarios.
  • Test the chain — for incremental backups, restore from a chain that requires the last 7 days of incrementals.
  • Test cross-region restore — when DR activates, can you actually restore from the cross-region copy?
  • Audit the audit logs — confirm the immutable backups are actually immutable. Try to delete one in a test scenario; expect it to fail.

16.10 Where durability connects

Durability is not standalone. It interacts with:

  • §24 Consistency — sync replication enables strong consistency; async replication forces eventual consistency. The two qualities are mathematically intertwined (CAP theorem).
  • §11 Recoverability — DR strategy depends on backup durability. Cold sites rely on backup-restore; hot sites rely on continuous replication.
  • §12 Performance — sync replication adds latency. Performance work often pressures teams to weaken durability ("just turn off fsync"). Don't.
  • §18 Cost-efficiency — every replica costs storage. Erasure coding, tiered storage (hot/warm/cold), and lifecycle policies are the cost levers.

The next section (§24 Consistency) covers the second half of the data quality story: even when data is durable, is what each observer sees actually correct?

§17 · DataConsistency

Durability (§23) ensures data exists. Consistency ensures everyone sees the same data. It's the second half of the data quality story — and arguably the harder one in distributed systems. The CAP theorem proves you can't have all three of consistency, availability, and partition tolerance at once. The choice you make defines your architecture: ACID databases pick C+P (or C+A in non-partition cases), Cassandra picks A+P, Redis picks A+P. Understanding consistency models is the difference between a system that's "fast" and one that's actually correct.

17.1 The crisp definition

Consistency = the property that all observers of the system see the same state at the same time. The strict version (linearizability) requires that as soon as a write completes, every subsequent read returns that write or newer. Weaker versions allow various forms of staleness.

The word "consistency" is overloaded — three different meanings collide:

ContextMeaningExample
ACID transactionsEach transaction preserves invariants (foreign keys, constraints)"Transferring money preserves total balance"
CAP theoremAll replicas see the same value at the same time"Read after write returns the new value"
Eventual consistencyReplicas converge to the same value given enough time"DNS propagation"

This section focuses primarily on the second meaning — replica consistency in distributed systems — because that's where most production engineering decisions are made.

17.2 The CAP theorem — pick two

Eric Brewer's 2000 conjecture (later proven): in any distributed system you can have at most TWO of the following three:

CAP theorem: under network partition, you must choose between consistency and availability. The third corner — partition tolerance — is non-negotiable in any distributed system because partitions WILL happen.

The honest framing — PACELC

CAP only describes behavior under partition. The PACELC theorem (Daniel Abadi) extends it: even when there's no partition, you face a tradeoff between Latency and Consistency.

If Partition (P):  choose Availability (A) or Consistency (C)
Else (E):          choose Latency (L) or Consistency (C)

Most modern distributed databases pick PA/EL (favor availability + low latency, accept eventual consistency) or PC/EC (favor consistency, accept higher latency). Knowing which family yours is in helps predict its behavior.

17.3 Consistency models — the spectrum

Not just "strong" vs "eventual" — there are multiple useful intermediate guarantees, ordered from strongest to weakest:

ModelGuaranteeUsed by
LinearizabilityAs if there's only one copy; reads always return latest write. The strongest.Spanner, etcd, ZooKeeper
Sequential consistencyAll operations appear in same order to all clients (but might be slightly stale)Most multi-master DBs
Causal consistencyCausally-related operations seen in causal order; concurrent ops may be reorderedSome social systems, MongoDB causal
Read-your-writesA client sees its own writes immediately (but other clients may not)Session-aware caching
Monotonic readsYou don't go backwards in time; reads only advanceOften combined with read-your-writes
Eventual consistencyWithout further updates, all replicas convergeDNS, Cassandra, S3

The skill is choosing the WEAKEST model that's still correct for your use case. Stronger consistency costs latency; the right choice is "as weak as we can get away with."

17.4 ACID — what databases historically promised

Single-node relational databases offered ACID guarantees. Each letter:

Atomicity
All operations in a transaction succeed or all fail. No partial commits.
Consistency
Every transaction transitions the database from one valid state to another (preserves invariants — foreign keys, unique constraints, check constraints).
Isolation
Concurrent transactions don't see each other's intermediate state.
Durability
Once committed, the change persists across crashes (§23).

17.5 Isolation levels — the practical knobs

Even single-node databases let you weaken Isolation for performance. The four standard levels:

LevelAllowsPerformance
Read UncommittedDirty readsFastest (rarely used)
Read CommittedNon-repeatable reads, phantomsPostgres default
Repeatable ReadPhantoms onlyMySQL default
SerializableNothing — full isolationSlowest, deadlock-prone

Most production code uses Read Committed and pays attention to specific places where stronger isolation matters (e.g., balance updates use SELECT FOR UPDATE).

17.6 BASE — the eventually-consistent counterpart

For systems prioritizing scale over strict consistency, BASE is the alternative philosophy:

Basically Available
System guarantees availability per CAP, even if responses might be stale.
Soft state
State of the system may change over time without input — replicas converging.
Eventual consistency
Without new writes, the system will reach a consistent state.

BASE is the philosophical heart of NoSQL and most large-scale distributed databases. Whether you're explicit about it or not, you're choosing BASE when you choose Cassandra, DynamoDB, or any eventually-consistent system.

17.7 Replica consistency in practice

The mental model: a write goes to the primary; replicas catch up later. The questions become:

  1. How does the primary acknowledge the write? (Sync vs async — see §23.6)
  2. Where can clients read? (Primary only vs primary-or-replica)
  3. What guarantees do replica reads have? (Strong vs eventual vs read-your-writes)

The matrix:

Write modeRead targetConsistency observed
Sync (wait for replicas)Any replicaStrong (linearizable)
SyncPrimary onlyStrong (linearizable)
AsyncPrimary onlyStrong (latest)
AsyncRead-your-writes-aware replicaRead-your-writes only
AsyncAny replica, no awarenessEventual (may see stale)

The "read-your-writes-aware" trick: track a session's "last write timestamp" and route reads to a replica that has caught up to that timestamp. Postgres logical replication exposes the WAL position; clients can pin reads to a sufficient replica.

17.8 Message delivery semantics — consistency for queues

Distributed messaging has its own consistency model — the question is "did this message get delivered, and how many times?" Three guarantees:

GuaranteeMeaningConsequence
At-most-onceEach message delivered 0 or 1 timesSome messages lost. No duplicates.
At-least-onceEach message delivered 1 or more timesNo loss. Duplicates possible.
Effectively-onceEach message processed exactly once via dedupNo loss, no duplicate effects.
True "exactly-once" delivery does not exist on a network. The Two Generals Problem proves you cannot guarantee both delivery AND non-duplication using only an unreliable network. What systems mean when they say "exactly-once" is "at-least-once delivery + idempotent or deduplicated processing." The correct term is effectively-once. Anyone selling you "exactly-once" without idempotency is selling magic beans.

Idempotency — turning at-least-once into effectively-once

An operation is idempotent if applying it once and applying it many times produce the same result. Idempotency is what makes at-least-once delivery safe.

OperationIdempotent?How to fix if not
SET balance = 100✓ Yes—
UPDATE balance = balance + 10✗ NoUse idempotency key
INSERT order WHERE id = 'ord_551'✓ Yes (PK constraint dedup)—
POST /orders✗ NoIdempotency-Key header
PUT /orders/ord_551✓ Yes (full replacement)—
Send email "thank you"✗ NoTrack sent IDs; dedup before send
Charge credit card✗ NoIdempotency key, vendor dedup

The idempotency key pattern

# Client request includes Idempotency-Key
POST /v1/orders HTTP/1.1
Idempotency-Key: 8a7f4c12-e3d9-4f6b-b0a1-d8e2c5f6a9b3

{
  "user_id": "u_88421",
  "items": [...],
  "amount_sar": 87.50
}
// Server-side handler — atomic dedup
async function createOrder(req) {
  const key = req.headers['idempotency-key'];
  if (!key) throw new BadRequest('Idempotency-Key required');

  const existing = await db.idempotency.find(key);
  if (existing) return existing.response;  // dedup

  return await db.transaction(async (tx) => {
    const order = await tx.orders.create(req.body);
    await tx.idempotency.insert({
      key:        key,
      response:   order,
      expires_at: addDays(new Date(), 7)
    });
    return order;
  });
}

17.9 The outbox pattern — atomic state + event emission

Common scenario: in one transaction you want to (a) write to your DB AND (b) publish an event to a queue. You can't do both atomically across systems. Naive approach causes inconsistency:

// WRONG — both fail-open scenarios
await db.orders.insert(order);    // commits
await queue.publish(orderEvent);  // crashes! → DB committed, no event

The outbox pattern writes the event to the same database, in the same transaction, into an "outbox" table. A separate process polls the outbox and publishes to the real queue.

-- Schema
CREATE TABLE outbox (
  id           UUID PRIMARY KEY,
  event_type   TEXT NOT NULL,
  payload      JSONB NOT NULL,
  published_at TIMESTAMPTZ            -- NULL until relay sends it
);

-- App: ONE transaction writes both
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO orders (...) VALUES (...);
INSERT INTO outbox (id, event_type, payload)
  VALUES (gen_random_uuid(), 'order.placed', '...');
COMMIT;

-- Relay: separate process
SELECT * FROM outbox WHERE published_at IS NULL
  ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 100 FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED;
-- publish to Kafka, then UPDATE outbox SET published_at = NOW()

This is at-least-once (relay can republish on crash) — consumers must be idempotent. But state and event are now atomically consistent.

17.10 Consistency boundaries — picking what's strong vs eventual

The skill in real systems is identifying which transactions need strong consistency and which can tolerate eventual. The Jahez calibration:

OperationConsistency requiredWhy
Place order + decrement inventoryStrong (single transaction)Cannot oversell
Charge paymentStrong + idempotentCannot double-charge or lose
Send order confirmation emailEventual (async, retried)Slight delay acceptable
Update analytics dashboardEventual (minutes-late OK)Reporting tolerates lag
Show order history to userRead-your-writesUser must see own recent order immediately
Show recommendationsEventual (hours-late OK)ML refresh cadence anyway
Cache invalidationEventual (TTL fallback)Stale-cache acceptable for short windows

The pattern: strong consistency at write boundaries, eventual consistency for downstream propagation. The order itself is atomic; the email, analytics, and recommendations all happen async with at-least-once + idempotency.

17.11 Where consistency connects

Consistency interacts with every other quality:

  • §23 Durability — sync replication enables strong consistency. Loss of durability often means loss of consistency invariants.
  • §9 Availability — CAP forces the tradeoff. Stronger consistency = lower availability under partition.
  • §12 Performance — strong consistency = coordination = latency. Eventual = no coordination = fast.
  • §13 Scalability — strong consistency creates bottlenecks (single primary). Sharding + eventual scales further.

The data family ends here. The next group, the Operational family, covers how humans actually keep these systems alive — observability, maintainability, security, and cost-efficiency.

§18 · OperationalObservability

Observability is the meta-quality. Every other quality in this taxonomy depends on observability — you cannot improve availability if you can't measure it, you cannot find performance regressions you can't see, you cannot prove durability without instrumentation. Observability is the property that lets engineers understand a system's behavior from the outside. Part I of this guide is the deep dive on the discipline itself; this section frames it as a quality among the SRE family and covers what makes observability good, where it costs, and how to think about it as an investment.

18.1 The crisp definition

Originally a control-theory term: a system is observable if its internal state can be inferred from external outputs alone. Adapted to software: can engineers answer arbitrary questions about the system's behavior using only the data it emits?

This is different from monitoring. Monitoring watches predefined signals for predefined failures. Observability lets you investigate unknown unknowns — questions you didn't think to ask in advance. Both matter; observability is the larger discipline.

18.2 The three pillars (refresher)

Covered in depth in §3-§7. The shorthand:

PillarAnswersCost driver
Metrics"What's the trend?" "Is X healthy?"Cardinality (§23.4)
Logs"What happened in this specific case?"Volume × retention
Traces"Where is time being spent?"Span volume (§23.5)

A mature observability system uses all three with deliberate sampling and cardinality discipline. Naive "log everything, label everything, trace every request" becomes untenable past a few thousand RPS.

18.3 The five engineering qualities of good observability

  1. Comprehensive coverage — every service, every dependency, every pillar. Gaps become blind spots during incidents.
  2. Cross-pillar correlation — exemplars (metric→trace), trace IDs in logs, structured logs queryable by trace_id. The pillars must connect.
  3. Low query latency — ad-hoc queries return in seconds, not minutes. Slow tools mean engineers don't use them during incidents.
  4. High cardinality where it matters — for events/logs/traces, NOT metrics.
  5. Sustainable cost — sampling, retention, and aggregation strategies that scale sub-linearly with traffic.

18.4 Cardinality — the central discipline

Cardinality is the number of unique label combinations a metric has. Prometheus and most TSDBs store one time series per unique combination. The math runs away faster than people expect:

# Innocent-looking metric:
http_requests_total{
  app="order-svc",
  method="POST",
  status="200",
  endpoint="/orders",
  user_id="u_88421"      // ← THE problem
}

# 1 service × 4 methods × 5 status × 30 endpoints × 5,000,000 users
# = 3,000,000,000 unique time series
# = OOMKilled Prometheus inside 20 minutes

The cardinality budget

Real Prometheus deployments survive on the order of 1-10 million active series per replica. Past that, ingestion latency climbs, queries slow, and memory grows linearly until OOM.

TierActive seriesBehavior
Healthy< 1M per replicaSub-second queries, cheap storage
Caution1M – 5MSlower queries, larger memory
Pressure5M – 10MQueries time out; OOM risk
Broken> 10MFederate, shard, or scale-out backend

The cardinality rules

✓ Safe in metric labels

  • app · < 100 services
  • namespace · < 50
  • method · GET/POST/PUT/DELETE
  • status_class (2xx/4xx/5xx)
  • endpoint — template path, NOT actual
  • region, zone, cluster
  • provider · mada / visa
  • city · riyadh / jeddah

✗ Never in metric labels

  • user_id — millions of values
  • order_id, request_id
  • trace_id, span_id
  • email, phone (privacy too)
  • full URL path with IDs
  • error_message — free text
  • timestamp, session_id
  • any UUID, any user-supplied string

Templated paths — the canonical fix

// WRONG — cardinality explodes per-user
metrics.increment('http_requests_total', {
  endpoint: req.url        // "/orders/u_88421/items/i_12"
});

// RIGHT — templated path, low cardinality
metrics.increment('http_requests_total', {
  endpoint: req.route.path // "/orders/:userId/items/:itemId"
});

18.5 Where high-cardinality data goes — exemplars and wide events

You still need to know which user's request failed. Just don't put it in metric labels. Three options:

① Logs — structured logs handle high-cardinality natively
Loki indexes a small set of labels; the body holds high-cardinality fields. {app="order-svc"} | json | user_id="u_88421"
② Traces — every span carries arbitrary attributes
OTel span attributes have no cardinality limit. Tempo/Jaeger store one trace per request, indexed by trace_id.
③ Exemplars — the bridge from metric to trace
An exemplar is a single trace_id attached to a histogram bucket sample. When p99 spikes, click the metric and Grafana takes you to a trace from that exact moment. The metric→trace flow that makes the LGTM stack feel magical.
// Go: exemplar wiring
histogram.WithLabelValues("POST", "order").
    (prometheus.ExemplarObserver).ObserveWithExemplar(
        durationSeconds,
        prometheus.Labels{"trace_id": span.SpanContext().TraceID().String()},
    )

18.6 Sampling — paying for what you actually need

Storing every metric, log line, and trace span at full fidelity is unsustainable past a few thousand RPS. Four strategies:

StrategyHow it picksPros / Cons
Probabilistic (head)Random N% at request entrySimple. Loses rare events.
Rate-limitedCap N samples/sec per dimensionKeeps baseline of every kind.
Tail samplingWait for full trace, keep based on outcomeKeeps all errors + slow + critical. Higher collector cost.
AdaptiveSampling rate adjusts to load or budgetStays within fixed cost ceiling.

Tail-sampling — the standard pattern

tail_sampling:
  decision_wait: 10s
  policies:
    # Always keep errors
    - name: keep-errors
      type: status_code
      status_code: { status_codes: [ERROR] }
    # Always keep slow requests
    - name: keep-slow
      type: latency
      latency: { threshold_ms: 1000 }
    # Always keep critical services
    - name: keep-critical
      type: string_attribute
      string_attribute:
        key: service.name
        values: [payment-svc, order-svc]
    # Sample 10% of everything else
    - name: sample-baseline
      type: probabilistic
      probabilistic: { sampling_percentage: 10 }

Log sampling — the often-forgotten one

  • Drop healthchecks at the agent — single biggest free win
  • Sample successful requests — log 1 in 100 of 200 OK; log every 4xx/5xx
  • Reservoir sampling — keep N logs/min/service no matter how many emitted
  • Log levels in production — INFO and above by default; DEBUG behind a flag

18.7 Service mesh — observability for free

A service mesh (Istio, Linkerd) injects a sidecar proxy next to every pod, transparently intercepting all network traffic. The proxies form a uniform L7 plane handling routing, security, and observability. You get RED metrics, distributed tracing, and structured logs for every service-to-service call without instrumenting application code.

A service mesh gives consistent observability across every service-to-service call without app-level instrumentation.

What the mesh gives you for free

SignalWithout meshWith mesh
RED metricsManual instrumentation in every serviceAutomatic, uniform names
Per-route metricsCustom code in every serviceFree, sliced by HTTP path / gRPC method
Distributed tracingNeed OTel SDK in every serviceSidecar inserts trace headers; partial coverage even without app instrumentation
Access logs (L7)Each service writes own formatUniform JSON logs with full request context
Topology graphInferred from logs / manualAuto-generated from actual traffic (Kiali)

The cost

  • Latency overhead: 1-3 ms per hop. Adds up in deep call chains.
  • Resource overhead: ~100 MB RAM, 50-100m CPU per pod. Cluster-wide non-trivial.
  • Operational complexity: the mesh is itself a system to operate. Istio specifically has a steep learning curve.
  • Modern alternative: eBPF-based observability (Cilium, Pixie) skips sidecars entirely. Zero per-hop latency. Younger ecosystem.

18.8 Observability-Driven Development (ODD)

The discipline articulated by Charity Majors and the Honeycomb team: observability is not an operations afterthought — it is a development practice. If you cannot tell from production telemetry alone whether a feature is working, the feature is not done.

Old modelODD model
Code first, monitor laterInstrumentation is part of the code
Dashboards built post-launchQueries identified during design
"Looks good in staging" = ready"Telemetry confirms behavior in prod" = ready
Ops debugs the unknownDevs debug from telemetry, in prod
Test coverage = unit + integrationPlus "can I observe this in prod?"

Wide events over narrow metrics

A wide event is a single structured log/span carrying everything you know about a request. One event with 30 fields beats 30 separate metrics — at query time you can slice by any combination.

// Bad — many narrow metrics, can't combine
metrics.increment('order_created');
metrics.observe('order_amount', amount);
metrics.observe('order_duration', duration);

// Better — one wide event, query any combination
log.info({
  event:           'order.created',
  user_id:         user.id,
  user_tier:       user.tier,
  city:            order.city,
  payment_method:  'mada',
  amount_sar:      order.amount,
  item_count:      order.items.length,
  duration_ms:     duration,
  feature_flags:   activeFlags,
  deploy_version:  process.env.APP_VERSION,
  trace_id:        ctx.traceId,
  result:          'success'
}, 'order created');

// Now you can ask:
// "What's p99 of order.duration_ms for premium users in Riyadh paying with
//  mada on deploy v2.15.0 with feature flag X enabled?"

Cardinality is a feature, not a bug — for events

The classic TSDB advice ("keep cardinality low") applies to metrics. For events in columnar log stores or Honeycomb-style observability, high cardinality is exactly what you want. user_id, order_id, trace_id — all welcome. The whole point is to slice by them later.

This is why the modern observability stack has bifurcated:

  • Metrics (low-cardinality) — for dashboards, alerts, aggregate trends
  • Events / wide logs / traces (high-cardinality) — for debugging, "why is THIS user's order slow?"

Use both. They answer different questions.

18.9 The observability flywheel

The deepest principle: good observability → fast debugging → faster incident response → faster shipping → more deploys → more changes to debug → better observability invested in. It's a flywheel. Slow at first, fast once spinning, and the teams that get the wheel moving early outpace those that don't by an order of magnitude over a few years.

18.10 Connection to other qualities

  • Availability (§9), Performance (§12) — measured through observability. You can only manage what you can see.
  • Security (§25) — audit logging IS observability for security events. Same infrastructure, different retention/protection rules.
  • Cost-efficiency (§26) — observability is itself a major cost. The discipline of cardinality, sampling, and retention is FinOps applied to telemetry.
  • Maintainability (§24) — debuggable systems are maintainable systems. Observability investment compounds over years.

Observability sits at the foundation of operational excellence. Every other operational quality — maintainability, security, cost-efficiency — depends on it.

§19 · OperationalMaintainability

A system that works perfectly today but cannot be safely changed tomorrow has no future. Maintainability is the quality of being modifiable, debuggable, deployable, and operable over years — by engineers who weren't there when it was built. It's the quality that determines whether your system gets better or worse over time. Most production systems quietly fail this test: they accrete complexity until everyone is afraid to touch them. The DORA metrics give us a concrete way to measure it.

19.1 The crisp definition

Maintainability = the ease with which a system can be modified to fix faults, improve performance, adapt to changed requirements, or add new features — by engineers who weren't its original authors.

Maintainability has several dimensions, all of which compound:

  • Modifiability — can we change the code without breaking things?
  • Debuggability — can engineers figure out why it's broken?
  • Deployability — can we ship safely, often, and roll back fast?
  • Operability — can the on-call engineer keep it running?
  • Understandability — can a new engineer onboard quickly?

19.2 The DORA metrics — the industry-standard measurement
