  • Use names of roles, not people: "the on-call" not "Ahmed"
  • Focus on system contributors: missing alert, unclear runbook, race condition
  • Write action items that are specific, owned, and time-bound
  • Track action items to completion — review at the next monthly incident review
  • Share publicly across the engineering org — others learn too

✗ Don't

  • "Ahmed should have caught this in code review" — punitive, useless
  • "We need to be more careful" — not actionable
  • "Add monitoring" without specifying what metric, what alert, what threshold
  • Close action items as "WontFix — too much work" without escalation
  • Hide the postmortem from people outside the team

27.3 Chaos engineering — finding failures before they find you

You can't prevent what you don't anticipate. Chaos engineering is the practice of injecting failures in a controlled way to discover weaknesses before customers do. Pioneered by Netflix's Chaos Monkey; standardized by the Principles of Chaos Engineering (Basiri et al.).

The protocol:

  1. Define steady-state — what "healthy" looks like in metrics (RPS, p99, error rate).
  2. Form a hypothesis — "the system will continue to meet its SLO when I kill 30% of order-svc pods."
  3. Run the experiment — kill pods, slow a network, fail an AZ, exhaust CPU.
  4. Compare to steady-state. If it diverged, you found a weakness.
  5. Fix and re-run.

K8s-native tools: Chaos Mesh, LitmusChaos, chaoskube (random pod killer). Run in staging first; advanced teams run in production with limited blast radius (one cell, off-peak).

# Chaos Mesh — kill 30% of order-svc pods every 10 min
apiVersion: chaos-mesh.org/v1alpha1
kind: PodChaos
metadata:
  name: order-svc-pod-kill
  namespace: jahez-staging
spec:
  action: pod-kill
  mode: fixed-percent
  value: "30"
  selector:
    namespaces: [jahez-staging]
    labelSelectors: { app: order-svc }
  scheduler:
    cron: "@every 10m"

27.4 Error-budget policy — making prevention enforceable

The principle ties the room together: when you exceed your error budget, feature work pauses and reliability work takes priority. Codified, this is the most powerful prevention mechanism in SRE — it gives engineering leadership a non-political reason to slow down feature shipping when reliability is suffering.

Budget statePolicy
Healthy (> 50% remaining) Ship features at full speed. Take some risks.
Caution (10–50% remaining) Increase scrutiny. Mandatory canary deploys. No risky changes Friday afternoon.
Exhausted (0% remaining) Feature freeze. All work focuses on reliability action items. Lift only when budget recovers.
The discipline. Without enforced policy, "we should fix the reliability stuff" loses to "ship the feature for the demo." Error-budget policy is what makes prevention real, not aspirational.

§33 · Failure LifecycleCascading Failures

A cascading failure is the worst-case manifestation of everything in Part III: a failure that grows over time as a result of positive feedback. One node fails, increasing load on the rest, increasing the probability they fail, taking down the entire system. This section follows the canonical treatment in the Google SRE Book (Mike Ulrich, Ch. 22) — what causes them, how to design against them, what triggers them, and what to do when one is in progress.

27.A.1 The defining property — positive feedback

A cascading failure is not "many failures" — it is a self-amplifying loop. The output of the failure becomes its own input. Every cascading failure has a feedback structure that looks like this:

The structural signature of a cascading failure: failure → more load on survivors → more failure → loop tightens.

Every prevention strategy below is fundamentally about breaking that loop before it forms — by capping load, capping retries, isolating blast radii, or making degraded behavior cheap.

27.A.2 The Shakespeare scenario — the canonical example

The SRE Book uses this scenario throughout, and it's worth repeating because it shows how innocent the trigger looks:

  1. Cluster A handles 1,000 QPS; Cluster B handles 1,000 QPS. Both are healthy.
  2. Cluster B fails. Load balancer redirects all traffic to Cluster A.
  3. Cluster A is now seeing 2,000 QPS — far beyond its capacity.
  4. Cluster A's frontends run out of CPU/memory/threads. They start crashing or failing health checks.
  5. The load balancer sees fewer healthy tasks in A; it redistributes even more aggressively to whatever's left.
  6. Within minutes, the entire service is down — not because B's failure caused it directly, but because the response to B's failure overloaded A.

The trigger was small (one cluster died, which capacity planning should have absorbed). The cascade is what made it global.

27.A.3 The cause families

The SRE Book groups cascading-failure causes into three families. Almost every real cascade fits one of these.

① Server overload — the most common cause

Direct overload (traffic exceeds capacity) or indirect (capacity drops while traffic stays steady). The Shakespeare scenario above is the canonical case. Once the rate of successfully handled requests drops below the demand rate, the system has crossed into cascade territory.

② Resource exhaustion

The same as overload, viewed at the resource level. The SRE book's deep insight here: resource exhaustions feed each other. The classic chain (the "GC death spiral"):

  1. Java frontend has poorly tuned GC parameters.
  2. Under high (but expected) load, the frontend runs out of CPU due to GC.
  3. CPU exhaustion slows down request completion.
  4. The increased number of in-progress requests causes more RAM to be used.
  5. Memory pressure leaves less RAM available for caching.
  6. Reduced cache size means more cache misses.
  7. More cache misses mean more requests fall through to backends.
  8. Backends run out of CPU or threads.
  9. Health checks fail. Cascade begins.

Each step feeds the next. Even after you stop the load, several of these stay broken. Diagnosing this from the symptom (the dead backend) backwards to "GC tuning on the frontend" can be nearly impossible during the outage.

③ Service unavailability — the snowball

Once a few servers crash, the remaining ones get more load and crash too. The SRE book's most important quantitative insight here:

If a service was healthy at 10,000 QPS but cascaded due to crashes at 11,000 QPS, dropping to 9,000 QPS will almost certainly NOT stop the crashes. Why? Reduced demand, but capacity is now far smaller (most servers are crash-looping or just-restarted). The recovery threshold is much lower than the breaking threshold. To stabilize, you may need to drop to ~1,000 QPS — 10% of normal — and let the system rebuild.

This asymmetry — breaks at 110%, recovers only below 10% — is what makes cascades so vicious. Mitigation has to be aggressive, not gradual.

27.A.4 Designing against cascading failures

The SRE Book's prevention list, in priority order, framed as "what kills the feedback loop":

StrategyWhere it breaks the loop
Load test until failure (and beyond) You can't design against what you haven't measured. Find the breaking point and the recovery point. The recovery point is much lower — that's the dangerous knowledge.
Serve degraded results Cheaper-to-compute responses under load: subset search instead of full corpus, simpler ranking, no images. Reduces the per-request cost so capacity stretches further.
Reject requests when overloaded (load shedding) Fail fast at the door. An HTTP 503 is cheap; an OOM is catastrophic. The server protects itself rather than letting overload turn into crash.
Higher-level rate limiting Reject at the LB or API gateway, before the request consumes any backend resource. Selective ("drop background sync, keep interactive sessions") is better than indiscriminate.
Capacity planning with N+2 Survive the loss of major chunks of infrastructure. If each cluster breaks at 5,000 QPS and peak is 19,000 QPS, you need ~6 clusters (not 4) for N+2.

27.A.5 Queue management — small queues, fail fast

Counterintuitive principle: large queues hurt more than they help. A queue equal to 10× the thread pool, with 100 ms thread-time per request, means a queued request takes 1.1 seconds — most of it queue wait. Latency explodes. Queues don't add capacity; they just convert load into latency.

The SRE book's guidance:

  • Steady traffic — keep queue size small (≤ 50% of thread pool). Reject early when saturated.
  • Bursty traffic — larger queue may absorb spikes, but only if the burst is followed by recovery time.
  • Use LIFO or CoDel instead of FIFO — drop old requests rather than processing them. By the time a request has been queued 10 seconds, the user has refreshed the browser anyway. There's no point serving them. "You don't get credit for late assignments with RPCs."

27.A.6 Retries — the fastest way to amplify a cascade

The SRE Book's most actionable lesson. Naive retries don't recover from overload — they guarantee cascade:

  1. Backend limit: 10,000 QPS. Beyond, it rejects with 503.
  2. Frontend sends 10,100 QPS — 100 QPS rejected.
  3. Frontend retries those 100 → backend now sees 10,200 QPS → 200 rejected.
  4. Retries grow: 100 → 200 → 300 QPS of retries on top of baseline traffic.
  5. Backend melts under the volume of work it's doing on rejected requests.
  6. Crash redistributes load to remaining backends. Cascade.
// Naive retry — the cascade-amplifier
for attempts < 10 {
  resp, err := backend.Call(req)
  if err != nil {
    attempts++
    continue          // no backoff, no jitter, no budget — disaster
  }
  return resp
}

The SRE Book's retry rules — every single one matters:

Always use randomized exponential backoff
Without jitter, retries from many clients synchronize after a network blip — a "retry ripple" that hits the backend in a single huge wave. With jitter, retries spread randomly across the retry window. Dan Sandler's quote: "If at first you don't succeed, back off exponentially." Ade Oshineye's follow-up: "Why do people always forget that you need to add a little jitter?"
Limit retries per request
Don't retry indefinitely. 3–5 retries is typical. After that, fail and surface the error.
Server-wide retry budget
Cap the total retry rate across the process: e.g., max 60 retries/minute regardless of how many requests fail. Prevents the retry storm from consuming the entire budget. The difference between a contained capacity hiccup and a global cascade.
Never retry at multiple layers
If JS retries 3×, frontend retries 3×, backend retries 3×, a single user click can become 27 attempts on the database (3³). Pick ONE layer to own retries — usually the highest one — and disable retry at every other layer. Multi-layer retry amplification is the single most common cause of cascading database overloads in the SRE book's experience.
Distinguish retriable vs non-retriable errors
Don't retry 4xx (client errors will never succeed). Don't retry overload-rejected 503s without checking Retry-After. Use a specific status code (e.g., 429 with retry-after, or a custom OverloadException) so clients know to back off rather than retry immediately.

27.A.7 Deadlines — the resource-leak prevention

If servers spend resources processing requests that have already exceeded their client deadline, they're doing pure waste. The work is wasted, the user has given up — yet the resources are still being consumed.

Deadline propagation

Set the deadline once at the top of the call tree; propagate the remaining time down through every layer. Every server in the chain knows when the original request's window expires.

Server A: // 30s deadline set at frontend
  ↓ takes 7s, then calls B with 23s remaining
Server B: // 23s deadline propagated
  ↓ takes 4s, then calls C with 19s remaining
Server C: // 19s deadline propagated — knows when to give up

// Without propagation:
// B uses a hardcoded 20s deadline for C → C thinks it has 20s
// → does 15s of work after the original 30s already expired → pure waste

gRPC's context.Context with deadlines does this natively when used correctly. Many HTTP-only stacks need explicit propagation via headers like X-Deadline-Ms.

Cancellation propagation

Beyond passive deadline expiry: when a client gives up (or a higher-level deadline triggers), actively cancel all the in-flight RPCs spawned by that request. Hedged requests in particular need this — sending duplicate requests to multiple replicas to take the fastest, then cancelling the rest. Without cancellation, hedging is just amplifying load.

The bimodal latency trap

The SRE book gives a startling math example:

  • 10 frontend servers, 100 threads each = 1,000 thread capacity total.
  • Normal: 1,000 QPS at 100 ms = 100 threads in use. Plenty of headroom.
  • Now 5% of requests start hitting a 100s deadline (e.g., a Bigtable keyspace becomes unavailable).
  • 5% × 1,000 QPS = 50 QPS × 100s = 5,000 threads' worth of work.
  • You only have 1,000 threads. The frontend can now serve only 19.6% of requests successfully.

5% of requests hitting a 100s deadline causes 80% of requests to fail. The fix: fail fast on errors. If a backend is unavailable, return an error immediately rather than holding the thread for the full deadline. Use the RPC layer's fail-fast option. Don't set deadlines orders of magnitude longer than mean latency — they're traps for cascades.

27.A.8 Slow startup & cold caches — the recovery trap

Even after you've fixed the trigger, recovery itself can re-trigger the cascade. Why? Because just-restarted servers are slower:

  • Java/.NET JIT hasn't warmed up — first 1000 requests are 10× slower
  • Connection pools haven't been built — first requests pay the connection cost
  • In-memory caches are empty — every request goes to the cold backend

If your service depends on caches for capacity (not just latency), bringing it back from a cold start under full load is impossible — it'll cascade again immediately. The SRE Book's distinction:

Latency cache
Cache improves response time but the service can sustain expected load with an empty cache. Safe.
Capacity cache
Service cannot sustain expected load with an empty cache. Dangerous — cache is now a hard dependency. The SRE Book's warning: be careful when adding caches "for performance." You may have just turned them into a load-bearing structural component without realizing.

Recovery requires gradual ramp-up: slowly increase load on a cluster to let caches warm before going to full traffic.

27.A.9 Always go downward in the stack

Avoid intra-layer communication — services at the same layer talking to each other (peer A asks peer B to handle a request peer A couldn't). Three reasons:

  • Distributed deadlock — A's thread pool fills waiting on B; B's thread pool fills waiting on A.
  • Failure-mode amplification — when load increases, intra-layer proxying often increases too (e.g., re-routing for failed users), adding load on top of the original problem.
  • Bootstrap complexity — circular dependencies make initial startup of the system fragile.

If a frontend hits the wrong backend, the backend should tell the frontend to retry on the right backend, not proxy the request itself. Communication should always go up→down, never sideways.

27.A.10 The triggering conditions

What turns a system that's been "fine for months" into an active cascade? The SRE Book's list:

  • Process death — a Query of Death, an assertion failure, a few crashes. A small initial loss can be enough.
  • Process updates — pushing a new binary to too many tasks at once removes capacity during the rollout.
  • New rollouts — code or config changes that alter request profile, resource usage, or backend behavior.
  • Organic growth — usage grew without capacity adjustment. The most common cause that surprises people.
  • Planned drains / turndowns — capacity removal for maintenance reduces headroom.
  • Request profile changes — same RPS but more expensive requests on average. Usually invisible until you measure cost-per-request.
  • Resource limit changes — relying on overcommit / slack CPU as your safety margin. Slack CPU disappears when neighbor jobs change.

During an active incident, always check for recent changes first. ~80% of cascades correlate with a deploy or config change in the prior 30 minutes. If something was pushed, consider rollback before deeper investigation.

27.A.11 Testing for cascading failures

The hardest part of cascade prevention: you can't reason your way to "what will fail first." You have to measure it.

Test until failure, then beyond

Load test components past their breaking point. Find:

  • What resource exhausts first? (Often surprising — not what you assumed.)
  • Does the component reject excess load gracefully (good) or crash (cascade-prone)?
  • How far does load have to drop before the system recovers? (The asymmetry from §33.3.)
  • Does it recover automatically when load drops, or does it need human intervention?

Test both gradual ramp-up and impulse (instant load step) — they expose different failure modes due to caching effects.

Test popular clients

You don't always control your clients. Find out: do they back off exponentially? Do they queue and burst on reconnect? Do they have an external trigger (e.g., a software update push) that creates a synchronized retry storm? Stage failures with your largest internal clients to see how they behave.

Test noncritical backends

The trap: a "noncritical" backend (spelling suggestions, recommendations) blackholes — never returns. Your frontend waits the full deadline on every request. Suddenly the noncritical dependency has consumed all your frontend threads. Test that noncritical backends being unavailable AND being slow-but-not-failing both leave the critical path healthy.

27.A.12 Immediate response — when the cascade is happening NOW

Once you've identified an active cascade, the SRE Book's playbook:

ActionWhen to use it
Increase resources If you have idle capacity. Often insufficient if the system is already in a death spiral — adding tasks doesn't help if they crash on warmup.
Stop health-check kills If your scheduler keeps killing tasks for failing health checks while they're trying to start up, temporarily disable health-check-driven kills until the system stabilizes. Distinguish process health (is the binary alive?) from service health (can it serve requests?).
Restart servers For GC death spirals, deadlocks, wedged threads. Caution: don't restart if the cascade is cold-cache driven — restarting makes it worse.
Drop traffic — aggressively The big hammer. Per the asymmetry rule, drop to ~1% of normal load if the entire service is crash-looping. Let things stabilize, then ramp back up gradually.
Enter degraded mode If you've engineered for it. Disable expensive features, serve cached/simpler responses, drop low-priority requests.
Eliminate batch load Turn off non-critical jobs sharing the serving path: index updates, copies, statistics, prefetchers. Reclaim that capacity for user traffic.
Eliminate bad traffic If specific queries are causing crashes or amplification (queries of death), block them at the edge.
The most important rule: fix the trigger before recovering. If you ramp traffic back without fixing whatever caused the cascade (insufficient capacity, GC misconfiguration, runaway client), the cascade will simply restart the moment traffic returns. Stabilize first by dropping load; then add capacity / fix config / block the bad client; then ramp up.

27.A.13 Closing — the meta-lesson

The SRE Book closes the chapter with the principle that should be internalized over everything else:

"Without proper care, some system changes meant to reduce background errors or otherwise improve the steady state can expose the service to greater risk of a full outage. Retrying on failures, shifting load around from unhealthy servers, killing unhealthy servers, adding caches to improve performance or reduce latency: all of these might be implemented to improve the normal case, but can improve the chance of causing a large-scale failure. Be careful when evaluating changes to ensure that one outage is not being traded for another."

Every well-intentioned reliability improvement has a potential dark side under saturation. Retries help in the normal case, amplify in cascade. Caches help latency, become hard dependencies. Health-check-driven restarts help with stuck processes, kill recovering ones. Load balancing away from "unhealthy" servers reduces capacity precisely when it's needed most.

The discipline of cascade-resistant design isn't about avoiding any single feature — it's about understanding what each mechanism does at the saturation boundary, and breaking the feedback loops before they form. The patterns from §30 (resilience patterns), §31 (auto-recovery), and §15 (backpressure) are all ultimately defenses against the same enemy: the positive-feedback loop that turns a small failure into a global outage.

This section is a deep summary of "Addressing Cascading Failures" by Mike Ulrich, Chapter 22 of the Google SRE Book (O'Reilly, 2017, CC BY-NC-ND 4.0). The original chapter is the canonical reference and is essential reading. Available at sre.google/sre-book.

§34 · Process & PeopleIncident Response Process

Part III gave you the technical phases — Detect, Alert, Handle, Recover, Prevent. This section is the human side: who does what during an active incident, how to coordinate, how to communicate, what to write down. The technical patterns matter, but they fail in the hands of a chaotic team. A clear process is what turns a panicking room into a structured response.

38.1 Severity levels

Every incident response process starts with classification. Severity drives who gets paged, comms cadence, and post-incident treatment.

LevelDefinitionExamplesResponse
SEV-1 Critical. Major user impact, revenue, or compliance. Checkout completely down · payment processing failing · data breach · region outage All hands. Page on-call + leadership. Public status page. War room.
SEV-2 Major. Significant degradation; partial outage. Checkout slow (p99 5s+) · single payment provider down · 30%+ error rate on a service Page on-call. Status page if customer-facing. Slack incident channel.
SEV-3 Moderate. User-visible but workarounds exist. Search degraded · recommendations broken · slow but functional On-call investigates next business day. No public comms required.
SEV-4 / SEV-5 Minor. Internal-only or cosmetic. Internal dashboard slow · log shipping delayed · cosmetic UI bug Ticket. Fix in normal work cadence.
The classification rule of thumb: If a customer would call the company about it, it's at least SEV-2. If a customer would call the press about it, it's SEV-1.

38.2 The roles during an active incident

For SEV-1 and SEV-2, separate roles. One person should not play two roles — when the incident commander tries to debug, coordination collapses.

Incident Commander (IC)
Owns coordination, NOT debugging. Decides what to investigate next, assigns tasks, enforces timeboxes, calls in additional help, communicates externally. The IC's job is to not touch the keyboard. They can be a senior engineer or a dedicated SRE; what matters is calm, clear thinking and authority to decide.
Subject Matter Expert(s) (SME)
The engineer(s) actually doing the technical investigation and remediation. They have keyboards. They follow the IC's direction on what to investigate.
Communications Lead (Comms)
Writes status page updates, manages stakeholder messages, drafts customer-facing comms. Not the IC — comms is a real job during a SEV-1. Often filled by a manager, PM, or customer-success person.
Scribe
Records the timeline as it happens — actions taken, hypotheses tested, results. Often pulled from the IC's instructions or from Slack channel messages. The scribe makes the postmortem 10× easier.
Customer Liaison (large incidents)
For SEV-1s with major customers, a dedicated person handles the biggest customers' inquiries individually. Frees Comms to handle public messaging.

38.3 The incident lifecycle

The incident lifecycle. Mitigate before resolve; never delay mitigation to find root cause.

38.4 Mitigate before resolve — the critical principle

The single most important rule: stop user impact first, understand why later. A 10-minute mitigation that buys you breathing room to find the real fix is always better than a 60-minute investigation while users suffer.

Mitigation actions, in rough order of preference:

  1. Rollback the most recent change. ~80% of incidents correlate with a recent deploy.
  2. Disable the suspect feature flag. Even if you didn't deploy, maybe someone toggled.
  3. Failover to a healthy replica / region. The blast radius is smaller than fixing in place.
  4. Scale up if it's a load issue (often masking a slower regression).
  5. Restart the affected service. Rarely the right answer but often a fast partial mitigation.

38.5 The war room — how to run it

For SEV-1 / SEV-2, you need a synchronous coordination space. Modern best practice: a dedicated Slack channel + voice call. The Slack channel becomes the timeline.

# Slack channel naming convention
#inc-2026-04-30-order-svc-down

# Pinned message at top of channel:
🔴 SEV-1 · order-svc 5xx rate elevated
IC:    @ahmed
SMEs:  @leila (orders), @rashed (platform)
Comms: @sara
Scribe: @noor

📞 Voice room: https://meet.jahez.com/incident-room
📊 Dashboards: order-svc-red, cluster-overview
🚨 Status page: https://status.jahez.com/incidents/abc123

Started: 2026-04-30 14:38 UTC

Slack channel discipline:

  • One thread per investigation hypothesis ("checking DB pool" / "checking deploy at 14:25").
  • Status updates from IC every 15–20 min: "At 15:02 — error rate down to 3% after rollback. Investigating root cause. Next update 15:20."
  • Decisions in the main channel, not in DMs.
  • Drop screenshots of dashboards, queries, results — they'll be referenced later.

38.6 Status page communications

For customer-facing impact, every minute without comms erodes trust. The cadence:

TimeActionWhat to say
T + 5 min First post (acknowledge) "We're investigating reports of issues with [feature]. Updates coming."
T + 15 min Confirmation post "We've identified an issue with [system]. Customers may experience [impact]. Engineers are working on it."
Every 30 min Progress update "Investigation continues. Mitigations applied. We'll update again at [time]."
On mitigation Recovery post "Service has been restored. We're monitoring and will follow up with a postmortem."
Within 5 days Public postmortem What happened, what we did, what we'll change. Honest. No spin.

The communication principles

  • Acknowledge quickly, even if you don't yet know the cause. Silence implies "they don't know there's a problem."
  • Plain language. "Database connectivity issue" not "PgBouncer pool exhaustion in eu-south-1."
  • Specific impact. "Customers in KSA may see checkout errors" not "some users may be affected."
  • Set the next-update time. "Next update at 15:30" — even if you have nothing new, posting "no change yet, next at 16:00" is reassuring.
  • Don't speculate publicly. Internal hypotheses stay internal. Public statements are facts only.

38.7 The post-incident review (postmortem)

Already covered structurally in §32.1. The process angle:

  • Schedule within 5 business days. Memory fades fast.
  • Author = primary IC or SME, not management.
  • Review = blameless and broad — invite everyone who touched the system, plus a few outsiders for cross-pollination.
  • Action items get owners and dates, tracked to completion in your usual ticket tool, reviewed monthly.
  • Share widely — across the eng org, not just the team. Other teams have probably hit similar things.

38.8 Game days — practice the process when nothing is broken

You wouldn't run a fire drill the first time during a real fire. Same logic applies. Run a quarterly game day:

  1. An organizer designs a scenario — kill primary DB, simulate region outage, inject 50% packet loss between two services.
  2. Trigger it in a controlled environment (staging or production with limited blast radius).
  3. The team responds as if real — declare incident, assign roles, run the playbook.
  4. Debrief afterwards — what worked, what was confused, what's missing from the runbook.

The first game day at most companies reveals 10–15 process gaps that would have hurt during a real incident. Subsequent ones reveal fewer. Cumulative effect: the team that runs game days routinely responds to real incidents faster and calmer.

§35 · Failure ReferenceFailure Taxonomy — what can go wrong

A canonical inventory of failures organized by where they live (application / infrastructure / cluster) and when they happen in the lifecycle. For each: signals, root causes, how to handle, how to recover, how to prevent. Use this as a checklist for any service.

29.1 The organizing model

Three layers, organized by lifecycle within each. Application is split across its three temporal phases (startup → running → shutdown). Infrastructure is split by dependency type. Cluster/cloud covers the platform itself.

Each subsection below uses the same structure: signals → root causes → how to handle → how to recover → how to prevent. Read top-to-bottom for a complete walkthrough or jump to the failures relevant to a service you're building.

29.2 Application — Startup phase

Everything that can go wrong from "deploy initiated" to "pod is Ready and serving traffic." This is where K8s lifecycle primitives are the heaviest — every error has a corresponding K8s state.

The lifecycle: scheduling → image pull → volume mount → init containers → main container start → startup probe → readiness probe → endpoint registration.

29.2.1 Pod stuck Pending (no node available)

Signals
Pod phase = Pending; Event FailedScheduling; kube_pod_status_phase{phase="Pending"} sustained.
Root causes
Insufficient CPU/memory across all nodes · resource requests too high · all matching nodes tainted · required nodeSelector / affinity has no match · PVC binding pending · node group at autoscaler max.
Handle
This phase has no traffic impact — pod isn't serving yet. The "handle" is at the platform: Cluster Autoscaler / Karpenter should add capacity automatically. Existing pods of older revisions keep serving (rolling deploy).
Recover
Diagnose with kubectl describe pod <pod> — Events tell you exactly which constraint failed. If autoscaler can't add nodes, escalate quota or fix selectors. kubectl rollout undo if a recent change tightened constraints.
Prevent
Right-sized requests (use VPA recommendations); cluster headroom >= largest pod's request; quota-aware autoscaler limits; alert on Pending > 10min; pre-flight admission webhook to reject obviously unschedulable specs.

29.2.2 ImagePullBackOff / ErrImagePull

Signals
Container state shows ImagePullBackOff; Events Failed to pull image; kube_pod_container_status_waiting_reason metric.
Root causes
Image tag doesn't exist (typo, wrong version) · registry credentials expired or missing imagePullSecret · registry network unreachable · registry rate-limited (Docker Hub anonymous limits) · imagePullPolicy: Always with private registry that's down.
Handle
Old replicas still running on previous image keep serving. maxUnavailable: 0 in rollout strategy ensures no capacity loss during the failure.
Recover
Verify the image tag exists in the registry. Check imagePullSecret. Try a docker pull from a node. Fix the cause and let the kubelet retry, or roll back to the previous Deployment revision.
Prevent
Mirror your registry (private mirror or harbor) · pin to immutable tags + digests, never :latest · pre-pull DaemonSet for critical images · CI step that resolves the image before merge · progressDeadlineSeconds auto-fails the rollout instead of hanging forever.

29.2.3 CreateContainerConfigError / volume / config errors

Signals
Container state CreateContainerConfigError or CreateContainerError; Event explains the missing reference.
Root causes
Referenced ConfigMap or Secret doesn't exist in this namespace · key in ConfigMap doesn't exist · PVC stuck Pending (StorageClass missing, no capacity, wrong AZ) · file in volumeMounts.subPath doesn't exist · ServiceAccount missing.
Handle
Pod can't start; previous revision serves traffic. No request-level handler.
Recover
kubectl describe pod tells you exactly what's missing. Apply the missing object, then kubelet retries automatically.
Prevent
GitOps tool (ArgoCD / Flux) deploys all related objects together as one application · validate ConfigMap keys against schema in CI · admission webhook (Kyverno / OPA) that rejects pods referencing nonexistent objects.

29.2.4 Init containers fail

Signals
Pod phase stuck at PodInitializing; kubectl logs <pod> -c <init-container> shows the failure.
Root causes
DB migration script fails · expected dependency (DB, secret store) not yet reachable · permission denied on volume · missing config the init container needs.
Handle
K8s retries the init container per restartPolicy. Existing pods of previous revision keep serving traffic.
Recover
Read init logs. Fix the root cause (missing migration target table, broken migration script, network policy blocking egress to DB). For migration failures specifically: never block startup on schema migrations — run them as a separate Job instead.
Prevent
Init containers should be idempotent and fast. Don't run migrations in init — use a Job. Don't depend on services that themselves might be starting up. Set explicit timeouts inside the init script.

29.2.5 Slow startup (startup probe timeout)

Signals
Container restart loop with last_terminated_reason = startup probe failed; kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total climbing on a fresh pod; Event Unhealthy: startup probe failed.
Root causes
JIT warmup (JVM, .NET) takes longer than the startup probe allows · large in-memory cache being primed (e.g., loading menu catalog into memory) · slow downstream dependency check during boot · loading large model file or config from network.
Handle
Pod isn't yet serving — readiness probe hasn't passed — so no traffic is impacted. K8s itself loops trying to start it.
Recover
Generous startupProbe.failureThreshold × periodSeconds = total boot budget. Once startup passes, liveness takes over with normal periods. This is exactly what startup probes are for: don't bake long boot times into your liveness probe.
Prevent
Profile boot time and set startup budget to 2× that · lazy-load non-critical caches · async background warmup after Ready · use AOT compilation (GraalVM, .NET ReadyToRun) for JVM/.NET; pre-pulled container layer caches; smaller images.

29.2.6 Readiness never passes

Signals
Pod is Running but never Ready; kube_pod_status_ready{condition="false"}; Service endpoints don't list this pod.
Root causes
Readiness probe checks an unreachable dependency · DB credentials wrong · network policy blocks egress · port mismatch between probe and container · readiness checks too strict (e.g., requires every downstream healthy).
Handle
K8s correctly excludes the pod from Service endpoints — no broken responses get sent. This is the readiness probe doing its job. Other healthy pods absorb traffic.
Recover
Inspect what readiness checks. kubectl exec into the pod and curl /healthz/ready manually. Fix the dependency or the readiness logic.
Prevent
Readiness should check only critical hard dependencies. Recommendations service unhealthy? That's degradation, not unreadiness — keep serving with an empty recommendations list. Don't make readiness recursive.

29.2.7 Bad rollout / regression caught at startup

Signals
New ReplicaSet pods crash on start; old ReplicaSet still healthy; kubectl rollout status shows stalled.
Root causes
Bug in new version causes crash on boot · missing required env var added in new version · breaking config change · incompatible schema (new code expects new column).
Handle
If maxUnavailable: 0, capacity is preserved while rollout stalls. progressDeadlineSeconds + kubectl rollout undo on failure makes this an auto-recovery scenario.
Recover
kubectl rollout undo deployment/<name> brings back the prior revision instantly. Investigate the failed version offline.
Prevent
Canary deployments (Argo Rollouts / Flagger) — promote based on real metrics, not just "pods are Ready." Schema migrations as separate, backward-compatible steps. Required-env validation in CI. Smoke test against new image before applying to production.

29.3 Application — Running phase

The pod is Ready and serving. Now any failure has direct user impact, so handling and recovery matter most. This is where the resilience patterns from §30 earn their keep.

29.3.1 Memory pressure / leak

Signals
container_memory_working_set_bytes climbing without correlated traffic increase · OOMKill events (exit code 137) · pod last state Terminated with reason OOMKilled.
Root causes
Real leak (unclosed listeners, growing in-memory cache, retained references) · workload sized too small (legitimately needs more memory than the limit) · large response bodies held in memory (the menu/restaurants N+1 example from §52.2) · runtime overhead growth (JVM heap fragmentation).
Handle
Readiness probe pulls a stressed pod from rotation when it slows. The kernel OOM-killer kills the worst offender; remaining pods absorb traffic. Do NOT have a liveness probe that checks memory — if all pods are stressed at once, you'd cascade-restart them.
Recover
Short-term: bump memory limit, rolling restart. Capture a heap dump before destroying evidence: for JVM jmap -dump, for Node --inspect + Chrome devtools, for Go pprof. Long-term: fix the leak.
Prevent
Alert at 85% memory utilization (page before OOM, not after) · profiling in staging · pagination by default for endpoints that return lists · streaming responses for large payloads · VPA in recommendation mode to right-size limits.

29.3.2 CPU saturation / throttling

Signals
container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_total > 0 · request latency p99 climbing · CPU usage at limit · request queue depth growing inside app.
Root causes
Traffic spike beyond capacity · regression in a hot code path (JSON serialization, regex, encryption) · runaway loop · GC pressure consuming CPU · noisy neighbor on the node consuming shared resources.
Handle
HPA scales out replicas. Rate limiting at the edge sheds excess load. Queue with timeouts so requests fail fast rather than piling up.
Recover
Auto-scale via HPA on CPU or RPS. Profile to find the hot path (continuous profiling tools: Pyroscope, Polar Signals). If a regression: roll back. If natural growth: raise maxReplicas + node pool capacity.
Prevent
Load tests in CI with realistic traffic mix · performance budget (p99 must stay < X under N RPS) · CPU profiling in staging · HPA configured well in advance of expected peaks · pre-scale before known events (Black Friday, Ramadan iftar peak).

29.3.3 Traffic spike / overload

Signals
RPS far above baseline · downstream queue growing · latency growing across all endpoints · error rate climbing as resources exhaust.
Root causes
Marketing event (push notification campaign, viral promotion) · upstream retry storm · scraping bots / abuse · client app bug retrying aggressively · planned event without pre-scaling.
Handle
Rate limiting at the edge (ingress / API gateway) · load shedding — drop low-priority requests first · queue with bounded size + timeout · graceful degradation (disable recommendations, simplified responses).
Recover
HPA scales out. If pre-scaled, the event passes uneventfully. If not, the system stabilizes at degraded mode until peak passes.
Prevent
Pre-scaling for known events · CDN + edge caching for static / cacheable responses · per-user/per-IP rate limits · circuit breakers on upstream calls so retries don't compound · contracts with marketing/product to share large-event timing.

29.3.4 Slow response / latency spike

Signals
p99 latency above SLO · error rate may be flat (no errors, just slow) · upstream client timeouts increase.
Root causes
Slow downstream (DB query, external API, internal service) — see §35.5 · cache miss storm · GC pause · CPU throttling · cold start of a new replica handling traffic before warmup · noisy neighbor on shared infra.
Handle
Tighten timeouts so slow requests fail fast and free resources · circuit breaker on the slow downstream · hedge requests to multiple backends if cheap.
Recover
Trace-driven diagnosis: drill from the slow span to the root call. Often the fix is at the downstream layer (add an index, restart the dependency, fall back).
Prevent
Latency SLOs and burn-rate alerts catch this early · trace-everywhere coverage so you can drill on the first incident · per-dependency timeout tuning · load tests that exercise tail latency, not just averages.

29.3.5 Deadlock / stuck threads / hung pod

Signals
RPS on one pod drops to ~0 while others continue · pod is Running and Ready but not processing · liveness probe might still pass (if probe is too cheap) · thread/goroutine count plateaus.
Root causes
Mutual lock acquisition in wrong order (classic deadlock) · all worker threads blocked on a slow downstream call · channel send with no receiver · synchronous I/O blocking the event loop (Node.js anti-pattern) · thread pool exhausted by long-held tasks.
Handle
Liveness probe should be cheap and meaningful: it should fail when the app stops processing, not just respond from a static handler. Pattern: liveness handler increments a counter and a separate goroutine resets it — if it stops moving, app is stuck.
Recover
kubelet kills + restarts the pod via liveness probe. Capture a thread dump before kill if possible (JVM: jstack <pid>, Go: SIGQUIT, Node: --inspect). Fix the lock ordering / the unbounded blocking call.
Prevent
Race detector in CI (Go's -race, Java thread-sanitizer) · code reviews for lock-acquisition order · timeouts on every blocking operation · structured concurrency that bounds wait time · liveness probe that actually exercises the request path.

29.3.6 Connection / file-descriptor leak

Signals
process_open_fds climbing · "too many open files" / EMFILE errors · TCP connections in CLOSE_WAIT piling up.
Root causes
Unclosed HTTP responses · connection pool not returning connections (forgotten defer client.Close()) · file handles not closed on error paths · unbounded SSE / WebSocket connections accepted without limit.
Handle
Bump container ulimit as a bandage; readiness probe will fail when limit hit (since the process can't open new FDs to handle requests). Other replicas absorb load.
Recover
Rolling restart resets FDs. lsof -p <pid> in a leaking pod tells you which kind of resource is leaking. Fix in code.
Prevent
Linters (Go's bodyclose, eslint rules for unclosed promises) · resource-management primitives (try-with-resources, RAII, defer, finally) · max connection limits on servers · alert on FD count growth.

29.3.7 Cache stampede / thundering herd

Signals
Sudden DB QPS spike at predictable intervals (TTL boundary) · latency cliff right when cache expires · downstream service overload synchronized across pods.
Root causes
Many pods all see the cache miss simultaneously (same TTL) · all of them issue a fresh DB query · DB gets N concurrent queries for the same data instead of 1 · result is N inserts of the same value back into cache.
Handle
Single-flight pattern: only one in-flight refresh per cache key, others wait for the result · jittered TTLs: random ±20% so expirations don't align · probabilistic early refresh: as TTL approaches, occasional requests trigger a background refresh.
Recover
If it's already happening: temporarily disable the cache invalidation and serve stale, or rate-limit the upstream until it stabilizes.
Prevent
Designed-in patterns above · stress test cache-miss scenarios · circuit breaker on the cached downstream so a stampede can't take it down.

29.3.8 Unhandled exception / panic / crash

Signals
Process exits with non-zero · kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total increments · stack trace in logs · last state Terminated reason Error.
Root causes
Bug — null pointer, type error, panic on bad input · OOM at the language level (different from container OOM) · forced shutdown from a fatal error path that should have been handled.
Handle
kubelet auto-restarts (CrashLoopBackOff if frequent). Other replicas serve. Per-request error boundaries in your framework (express error middleware, .NET exception filters, Go recover()) — one bad request shouldn't crash the process.
Recover
K8s self-heals automatic restart. If it's a regression, rollback. If it's input-driven, find and fix the trigger condition.
Prevent
Universal panic/exception handlers · fuzz testing for input parsing · contract tests for API edges · structured error types (no untyped exceptions on hot paths).

29.3.9 Bad config push (live config change)

Signals
Error rate or latency change correlated to a non-deploy event · ConfigMap change in audit log · feature-flag toggle right before the spike.
Root causes
Wrong value pushed to ConfigMap / feature flag service · forgot environment-specific override · type mismatch (string vs int) · invalid regex / URL.
Handle
App should validate config on read and fall back to safe defaults · feature flags should default to "off" on parse error.
Recover
Revert the config change. This is why config rollback should be as easy as code rollback — Argo CD revert, feature-flag toggle, config service revert.
Prevent
Config validation in CI (schema check, regex parse, URL parse) · staged rollout for config (canary by user %) · audit trail with who/what/when · alerts annotated with recent config changes.

29.3.10 Business-logic failures (during running)

Signals
Anomaly on business KPIs (orders/min drops, conversion drops, GMV anomaly) · spike in 4xx errors or domain-specific decline reasons · customer reports.
Root causes
Coupon abuse · pricing bug · payment fraud spike · inventory race (double-booking) · authorization rule misfire · A/B experiment causing regression.
Handle
Domain-specific: rate limit per user · disable the affected feature flag · activate fraud rules · serialize writes through a queue or distributed lock for inventory.
Recover
Disable the feature, hotfix the bug, reconcile affected orders (refunds, manual review).
Prevent
Business KPIs as first-class signals on dashboards · anomaly detection (not threshold) for KPIs · feature flags for new flows · A/B testing with automatic rollback on KPI regression · contract tests on business invariants.

29.4 Application — Shutdown phase

The forgotten phase. Pods shut down constantly during normal operation: rollouts, scale-down, node drain, evictions, preemption. If shutdown is wrong, every deploy causes errors — and those errors are nearly invisible because they're brief.

The K8s shutdown sequence: pod marked Terminating → removed from Service endpoints → SIGTERM sent to PID 1 → grace period countdown → SIGKILL if still alive at terminationGracePeriodSeconds.

29.4.1 Lost in-flight requests on shutdown

Signals
5xx error rate spikes briefly during every deploy · users see "connection reset" errors · upstream load balancers log connection-failure metrics during rollouts.
Root causes
Pod removed from Service endpoints AT THE SAME TIME as SIGTERM is sent — but propagation of the endpoint update isn't instant. Brief window where load balancer still sends requests to a pod that has started shutting down. App ignores SIGTERM and exits abruptly.
Handle
preStop hook with a small sleep (10–20s) — gives time for endpoint update to propagate before the app starts shutting down. Graceful shutdown: on SIGTERM, stop accepting new connections, wait for in-flight requests to drain, then exit.
Recover
Errors stop after rollout completes. There's nothing to "recover" mid-incident — it's transient.
Prevent
Standard pattern in every Deployment:
spec:
  terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 60           # total budget
  containers:
  - name: app
    lifecycle:
      preStop:
        exec:
          command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "sleep 15"]
    # sleep 15s before SIGTERM gives endpoint update time to propagate
// In your app — handle SIGTERM gracefully (Node.js example)
let shuttingDown = false;

// Readiness fails immediately, so no new traffic arrives
app.get('/healthz/ready', (req, res) => {
  if (shuttingDown) return res.status(503).end();
  res.status(200).end();
});

process.on('SIGTERM', async () => {
  shuttingDown = true;
  log.info('SIGTERM received, draining...');

  // Stop accepting new requests
  server.close();

  // Wait for in-flight requests (with a hard cap)
  await waitFor(() => activeRequests === 0, { timeout: 30_000 });

  // Close downstream connections cleanly
  await db.end();
  await redis.quit();
  await kafka.disconnect();

  process.exit(0);
});

29.4.2 Database connections leaked at shutdown

Signals
Postgres pg_stat_activity shows abandoned connections from terminated pods · DB connection limit hit faster than expected after several rollouts · "remaining connection slots reserved" errors.
Root causes
App killed before pool.end() ran · SIGKILL hit before graceful shutdown finished · forgotten cleanup hook · terminationGracePeriodSeconds too short.
Handle
Postgres eventually times out idle connections (default 5–10 min via idle_in_transaction_session_timeout) — but during a deploy storm, you might hit max_connections before it catches up.
Recover
pg_terminate_backend() abandoned connections in an emergency. Restart the app pool with kubectl rollout restart.
Prevent
SIGTERM handler that explicitly closes connection pools · idle_in_transaction_session_timeout on the DB · connection pool with maxLifetime to age out connections proactively · grace period long enough for clean shutdown.

29.4.3 Incomplete transactions / data inconsistency

Signals
Orphaned rows (e.g., order created but payment never recorded) · stuck records in "processing" state · accounting reconciliation drift after deploys.
Root causes
Multi-step business operation interrupted mid-way: pod killed between step 2 and step 3 of "create order → charge payment → write fulfillment record." Without transactional boundaries spanning the operation, partial state persists.
Handle
Don't make this an in-process problem. Use one of: database transactions (atomic within one DB) · outbox pattern (commit business state + event in one transaction; relay processes the outbox to publish events) · saga pattern (each step has a compensating action).
Recover
Reconciliation jobs scan for stuck records and either complete them (if idempotent retry possible) or compensate (refund, mark failed, alert). For severe cases: manual data ops with care.
Prevent
Build operations as event-driven from day one — no multi-step in-process state machines that span network calls. Idempotent operations + idempotency keys. SIGTERM handler that finishes the current operation before exit, with a hard timeout.

29.4.4 Unflushed buffers (logs, metrics, events)

Signals
Last few log lines missing for terminated pods · last batch of metrics not visible · events emitted right before shutdown lost.
Root causes
Buffered writers (log libraries, OTel batch processors, Kafka producer) didn't flush before exit · stdout buffer not flushed · OTel collector batch lost when pod was killed.
Handle
For logs: write to stdout/stderr line-by-line, no app-level buffering — rely on the log shipper to do batching at the node level (where shutdown is independent of pod lifecycle).
Recover
Lost is lost. Best you can do is design for it.
Prevent
Explicit flush on SIGTERM: logger.flush(), tracer.shutdown(), producer.flush() · short batch intervals (1s) so worst-case loss is bounded · use otel-agent DaemonSet so the collector survives pod shutdowns.

29.4.5 SIGKILL before grace period — hard shutdown

Signals
Pod terminated with no graceful shutdown logs · Event FailedKillPod · grace period exhausted message in kubelet logs.
Root causes
App ignored SIGTERM (no handler installed) · graceful shutdown took longer than terminationGracePeriodSeconds · stuck on a long-running operation that won't unblock.
Handle
None — by definition the pod is gone.
Recover
Reconciliation logic catches abandoned work (see 29.4.3).
Prevent
Always handle SIGTERM · cap any in-flight operation to a duration shorter than your grace period · set terminationGracePeriodSeconds generous enough for the slowest legitimate request to complete (typically 30–90s).

29.4.6 Resource cleanup misses (locks, leases, file system)

Signals
Distributed locks held forever · leases not released (other pods can't acquire) · temp files accumulating on PVC · stale entries in service registries.
Root causes
Cleanup deferred until shutdown which never ran cleanly · no TTL on the lock/lease so it lives forever after the holder dies.
Handle
For Redis-style locks: TTL with periodic renewal — if the holder dies, the TTL eventually expires. For Zookeeper / etcd: ephemeral nodes that die with their session.
Recover
Manually clean up stuck locks/leases. Reconciliation jobs to detect and remove stale state.
Prevent
TTL on every distributed primitive · short renewal interval (e.g., lock for 30s, renew every 10s) · ephemeral semantics where the framework supports it · idempotent operations so a re-run is safe.

29.5 Infrastructure — Database

The most common source of major incidents. Databases are slow to scale, hard to replace, and a single point of failure for every service that depends on them.

29.5.1 Connection pool exhausted (client side)

Signals
db_pool_acquire_duration_seconds p99 climbing · "acquire connection timeout" log spam · request latency spike with no DB CPU spike (the bottleneck is the pool, not the DB).
Root causes
Slow queries holding connections (most common) · connection leak (queries not returning to pool) · pool size set too small for traffic · long transactions held open by app bug.
Handle
Pool waiters time out fast — return error rather than block forever. Circuit breaker on the DB if persistent. Bulkheaded pools per query category prevent one bad query from starving others.
Recover
Bump pool size only if DB has headroom. The real fix is upstream: the slow query (next subsection) or the connection leak.
Prevent
Per-query timeout (statement_timeout) so a slow query can't hold a connection forever · alert on pool wait time before exhaustion · profile pool usage in load tests · separate read pool / write pool / batch pool for bulkheading.

29.5.2 Too many connections (server side)

Signals
"FATAL: remaining connection slots reserved" errors · pg_stat_activity_count at max_connections · new clients rejected.
Root causes
Number of services × pods × pool size exceeds max_connections · leaked connections (29.4.2) accumulating · DDoS / spike of new clients · forgot to close sessions in batch jobs.
Handle
Connection pooler in front of the DB: PgBouncer (transaction-mode pooling) lets you have 1000 client connections multiplexed onto 50 actual Postgres connections.
Recover
Kill idle / abandoned sessions: SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid) FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state='idle' AND state_change < now() - interval '10 minutes';. Restart problem app pools.
Prevent
PgBouncer / RDS Proxy for shared multiplexing · capacity model: total potential connections must fit within max_connections with headroom · idle_in_transaction_session_timeout + statement_timeout on the DB · monitor connection count by application label.

29.5.3 Slow queries

Signals
pg_stat_statements shows queries with mean_exec_time > 100ms · app p99 latency tracks DB latency · sequential scans on large tables.
Root causes
Missing index (most common) · stale statistics (ANALYZE not run after data growth) · suboptimal query plan · N+1 queries (one for each item in a list) · table bloat without VACUUM · cross-joining without selectivity.
Handle
Per-statement statement_timeout kills queries that exceed budget. App-level query timeout via the driver. Read replica for analytical reads.
Recover
EXPLAIN ANALYZE the offender. CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY if missing index. VACUUM ANALYZE if statistics stale. Refactor N+1 to JOINs or batched queries.
Prevent
EXPLAIN in CI for new queries (block sequential scans on large tables) · slow-query log review weekly · auto_explain for slow queries in production · query reviews on PRs that touch hot paths · ORM lint rules to catch obvious N+1.

29.5.4 Database deadlocks

Signals
"deadlock detected" errors in app logs · pg_stat_database.deadlocks counter increments · transactions automatically aborted by Postgres.
Root causes
Two transactions acquiring the same row locks in opposite order · long-held row locks while waiting for external work · lock escalation due to large updates.
Handle
Postgres detects deadlocks and kills one transaction. App should retry the killed transaction — it's a transient error, not a bug.
Recover
Retry typically succeeds. If recurring, find the contended rows in pg_stat_activity.
Prevent
Always lock rows in a consistent order (e.g., by primary key ascending) · keep transactions short — never hold a lock across a network call · use SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED for queue-like patterns · advisory locks for application-level critical sections.

29.5.5 Database not available (primary down)

Signals
Connection refused errors · pg_isready fails · all writes failing across services · alert PostgresDown.
Root causes
Primary node hardware failure · OOM on the DB pod (StatefulSet) · disk full on the data volume · OS-level kernel panic · planned maintenance window.
Handle
Auto-failover via Patroni / RDS Multi-AZ / etc — a replica is promoted and DNS / virtual IP switches over within seconds. App connection pools should reconnect to the new primary automatically.
Recover
After failover, the old primary is repaired and rejoins as a replica. App pools may need restarts if the driver doesn't refresh DNS.
Prevent
Multi-AZ or multi-node HA topology · automated failover (Patroni, Stolon, RDS) · monitoring of replication lag (if lag is too high, failover loses data) · regular failover drills (game days) · backup + PITR.

29.5.6 Replication lag

Signals
pg_stat_replication.replay_lag growing · stale reads from replicas · "could not find row" errors in read-after-write flows.
Root causes
Heavy write traffic outpacing replica's WAL apply rate · long-running query on replica blocking WAL replay (hot_standby_feedback) · network bandwidth saturation between primary and replica.
Handle
Read-after-write consistency: route the immediate read after a write to the primary, not a replica. Or use synchronous replication for critical writes (slower but consistent).
Recover
Reduce write load · kill long-running queries on replica · scale up replica resources.
Prevent
Alert on lag > X seconds (depends on app tolerance) · capacity-model the write rate · separate analytical replicas from real-time read replicas · query patterns aware of eventual consistency.

29.5.7 Disk full on database

Signals
Disk usage > 90% on data PVC · "no space left on device" errors · WAL not archiving · DB stops accepting writes (Postgres) or fails over (some HA setups).
Root causes
Unbounded table growth without partitioning · WAL files not being archived · long-running transaction preventing VACUUM · old backups consuming space · forgotten audit log table.
Handle
App reads still work (Postgres) · writes fail with disk-full errors · readiness probe should reflect this if the app can't function in read-only mode.
Recover
Expand the PVC (online if your storage class supports it) · archive old WAL · drop or partition large tables · kill long transactions blocking VACUUM.
Prevent
Alert at 70% (ticket) and 85% (page) · automatic PVC expansion when supported · partition large tables by date · retention policies for logs/audit · capacity model that includes WAL + indexes + bloat factor.

29.6 Infrastructure — Queues & Messaging

Queues decouple producers from consumers — but they introduce their own failure modes that don't exist in synchronous architectures.

29.6.1 No consumers (producer with no reader)

Signals
Queue depth growing without bound · message age (oldest message timestamp) climbing · kube_deployment_status_replicas{deployment="consumer-svc"} = 0.
Root causes
Consumer Deployment crashed and didn't restart (CrashLoopBackOff with no Ready pods) · consumer scaled to zero by mistake · consumer subscribed to wrong queue / topic · authentication broken · namespace deleted.
Handle
Producer continues publishing as long as queue accepts. Eventually queue hits its size limit or disk fills (next subsection).
Recover
Fix the consumer — debug the crash loop, restore replica count, fix subscription. After consumer comes back, watch for catch-up storm: scale up consumers temporarily to drain the backlog.
Prevent
Alert on consumer count == 0 for any production queue · alert on queue depth growing for > 10 min · alert on oldest message age > X · KEDA-based autoscaling so consumers scale with backlog.

29.6.2 Disk full on broker

Signals
Broker disk usage approaching capacity · RabbitMQ flow control kicks in (publishers throttled) · Kafka log directory full alerts · disk pressure on broker pods.
Root causes
Backlog from no consumers (29.6.1) · retention period too long for traffic volume · large messages exceeding sizing assumptions · broker not configured to delete consumed messages (Kafka retention settings).
Handle
RabbitMQ flow control slows publishers — applications get back-pressure. Kafka stops accepting writes when log dir full. Producer should handle publish failures: retry, fall back to local buffering, or drop low-priority messages.
Recover
Expand PVC · reduce retention temporarily · drain consumers to clear backlog · purge low-priority queues (if business permits).
Prevent
Alert on broker disk > 70% · capacity model based on peak traffic × retention · message TTL on queues that don't need full retention · separate broker per workload tier so a noisy queue can't fill the disk.

29.6.3 Consumer lag (consumers can't keep up)

Signals
Kafka consumer lag growing · message age climbing · downstream effects (e.g., orders showing as "processing" for minutes).
Root causes
Producer rate exceeds consumer capacity · consumer is slow per-message (CPU bound, slow downstream call) · partition count too low to parallelize · poison message stalling progress.
Handle
Auto-scale consumers (KEDA on lag metric). For Kafka: scale consumer replicas up to but not beyond partition count.
Recover
Add consumer replicas · profile consumer for slow path · increase partition count for future-proofing (carefully — partitions can't be reduced) · skip ahead to current offset if you can tolerate dropping the backlog.
Prevent
Capacity-test consumer throughput · partitions ≥ max_consumer_replicas · idempotent consumer logic so retries are safe · async I/O in consumer to overlap network calls.

29.6.4 Poison messages

Signals
Same message processed and rejected over and over · consumer crash loop on the same message · lag stuck at one offset.
Root causes
Malformed payload that fails parsing · message references data that no longer exists · regression in consumer code that fails on legitimate messages · upstream bug producing invalid messages.
Handle
Dead-letter queue (DLQ): after N failed attempts, move the message to a DLQ and continue processing the rest. Human or batch process inspects the DLQ later.
Recover
If a regression: fix consumer + replay from DLQ. If a producer bug: fix producer, decide whether to compensate the affected entities.
Prevent
Always wire a DLQ from day one · max-retry count per message · alert on DLQ growth · contract tests on producer message schemas · schema registry (Avro / Protobuf) for compile-time compatibility.

29.6.5 Broker not available

Signals
"connection refused" / "broker unavailable" errors in producer + consumer logs · cluster controller down (Kafka) · clustering errors (RabbitMQ).
Root causes
Broker pod / VM crashed · network partition · cluster split-brain · disk full on all nodes simultaneously · planned upgrade gone wrong.
Handle
Producers should buffer locally for short outages, with a hard cap. For critical messages, persist to disk before acknowledging upstream — outbox pattern.
Recover
Multi-broker cluster with quorum survives node loss. Restore failed nodes; if data loss, restore from snapshots (rare with replication).
Prevent
Multi-node broker cluster with replication factor ≥ 3 · spread brokers across AZs · monitoring on quorum health · regular failover drills.

29.7 Infrastructure — External integrations

Anything outside your boundary: payment gateways, identity providers, CDNs, third-party APIs, DNS. You can't fix the root cause; you defend.

29.7.1 Third-party API returns 5xx

Signals
Per-dependency error rate (e.g., http_client_requests_total{provider="mada",status=~"5.."}) · vendor status page incident · customer reports "payment failed."
Root causes
Vendor outage · vendor regional issue · vendor rate-limited you · expired API credentials · payload contract change.
Handle
Retry with backoff and jitter for transient errors (idempotent only) · circuit breaker after sustained failures · fallback: try alternative provider (visa instead of mada) or fail gracefully with helpful message.
Recover
Once vendor recovers, circuit breaker auto-closes via half-open trial. If credentials expired: rotate. If contract change: hotfix client.
Prevent
Multi-vendor strategy where business permits · vendor-specific dashboards and SLOs · contract tests against vendor sandbox · subscription to vendor status feeds · regular failover drills with secondary vendor.

29.7.2 Third-party API slow / timing out

Signals
Per-dependency latency p99 elevated · upstream request latency tracks the slow vendor span · timeouts in the client.
Root causes
Vendor performance degradation · network path slow (geographic routing) · vendor capacity issues during peak.
Handle
Tight timeouts — fail fast rather than holding the request thread · hedge requests for low-cost reads (send to two backends, take fastest) · UX-level: show "this is taking longer, please wait" or offer alternatives.
Recover
Vendor returns to baseline. Your circuit breaker / timeout policy made the impact bounded.
Prevent
Vendor SLAs codified as your timeouts · vendor dashboards in your Grafana so you see them like a first-class signal · alternative vendor wired and tested.

29.7.3 DNS failure

Signals
"no such host" / NXDOMAIN errors · DNS resolution latency spike · CoreDNS pod errors in K8s.
Root causes
CoreDNS pod down or rate-limited · upstream DNS provider slow · DNS-record TTL expired with no refresh · network policy blocking egress to DNS · misconfigured resolv.conf.
Handle
App-level DNS cache with longer TTL than configured · NodeLocal DNS Cache (caches resolutions per-node) · graceful retry on resolve failure.
Recover
Restart CoreDNS / NodeLocal DNS · failover to alternative resolver · evict and reschedule problem CoreDNS pods.
Prevent
NodeLocal DNS Cache deployed by default · multiple CoreDNS replicas across zones · DNS query rate monitoring · longer TTLs for stable records · alert on DNS error rate.

29.7.4 Identity provider / auth service down

Signals
Login flow failing · token-refresh errors · IdP-specific status code (401/403) spike across the platform.
Root causes
External IdP outage (Okta, Auth0, Cognito, internal SSO) · expired signing certificate · clock skew on JWT validation.
Handle
Existing session tokens continue working until expiry · API-key fallback for service-to-service if IdP-issued · cached JWKS keys with long TTL so token validation survives short IdP outages.
Recover
Wait for IdP recovery. Session tokens for already-logged-in users buffer the impact.
Prevent
Cache JWKS keys with refresh on failure · long token lifetimes (with refresh) so brief IdP outage doesn't lock everyone out · NTP sync everywhere · regular cert renewal automation.

29.7.5 CDN / edge cache failure

Signals
Asset 404s / 5xx · sudden origin traffic spike (cache miss avalanche) · regional access errors.
Root causes
CDN provider regional outage · cache invalidation gone wrong · TLS cert expired at edge · misconfigured cache rules.
Handle
Origin must survive 100% cache miss for short periods (capacity headroom for the cache-bypass case).
Recover
Failover to secondary CDN if available · DNS swap to direct origin temporarily · purge bad cache entries.
Prevent
Origin sized for cache-miss capacity · multi-CDN strategy or DNS-based failover · cert renewal automation · staged cache rule rollouts.

29.7.6 Vendor rate limit hit

Signals
HTTP 429 from vendor · "rate limit exceeded" in vendor responses · vendor-specific retry-after headers.
Root causes
Traffic spike on your side · vendor reduced your quota · accidental retry storm · multiple environments sharing one quota.
Handle
Honor Retry-After header · client-side rate limit slightly below vendor limit so you never hit it · token bucket per vendor.
Recover
Wait out the window. Negotiate higher quota with vendor.
Prevent
Internal rate limiter aware of vendor quotas · separate API keys / quotas per environment · monitoring quota usage as a percentage so you alert before hitting it.

29.8 Cluster — Pod & Node failures

Failures of the K8s primitives themselves. K8s self-heals most of these, but you should understand what's happening so you can confirm the recovery worked — and intervene if it didn't.

29.8.1 Pod crash (container exits non-zero)

Signals
kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total increments · pod state CrashLoopBackOff when frequent · Event BackOff.
Root causes
Application bug (panic, unhandled exception) · OOMKilled (29.3.1) · liveness probe failure (29.3.5) · misconfiguration causing immediate exit.
Handle
kubelet auto-restarts the container. Other replicas serve traffic. maxUnavailable: 0 + multiple replicas mean no capacity loss.
Recover
kubectl logs --previous shows what the dying pod printed. Fix the root cause and redeploy, or rollback if regression.
Prevent
Universal exception/panic handlers · CI smoke tests catch crash-on-boot bugs · canary deploys catch crashes before full rollout · alert on restart rate (not single restarts).

29.8.2 Pod evicted

Signals
Pod state Evicted · Event with reason Evicted describing the resource pressure (memory, disk).
Root causes
Node memory or disk pressure · pod requested less than it actually uses (kubelet evicts BestEffort/Burstable pods first) · taint added to the node forcing eviction.
Handle
ReplicaSet creates a new pod elsewhere. Other replicas absorb traffic in the meantime.
Recover
Diagnose what caused node pressure. Fix the resource requests for affected pods so they're not first to be evicted next time.
Prevent
Set requests ≥ realistic usage (Guaranteed QoS class survives longest) · PodDisruptionBudget protects against voluntary evictions · monitor node pressure conditions · ensure cluster headroom.

29.8.3 Node not Ready

Signals
kube_node_status_condition{condition="Ready",status="true"} == 0 · kubelet not heartbeating · pods on the node may go to Unknown state.
Root causes
kubelet crashed · networking issue between node and control plane · node OS-level problems (disk, kernel) · cloud instance terminated.
Handle
After ~5 minutes (configurable), node controller marks pods on the node for eviction. ReplicaSet recreates them on healthy nodes. Anti-affinity / topology spread ensures no single node holds all replicas.
Recover
Replace the node. Cluster Autoscaler does this automatically for cloud-managed groups. Drain remaining workloads if the node is hung mid-state.
Prevent
Spread replicas across nodes (topologySpreadConstraints) and AZs · enough cluster headroom to absorb a node failure · automated node replacement (Karpenter, managed node groups) · monitor node Ready condition.

29.8.4 Node disk pressure / memory pressure

Signals
kube_node_status_condition{condition="DiskPressure"=true} or MemoryPressure · pods evicted from the affected node.
Root causes
Container images filling disk · pod logs not rotating · workloads using more memory than requested · multiple OOM-prone pods on one node.
Handle
kubelet starts evicting pods to relieve pressure (oldest / lowest QoS first).
Recover
Image GC kicks in automatically. Manual cleanup if needed: crictl rmi --prune. Replace node if persistent.
Prevent
Tune kubelet GC thresholds · log rotation policies · proper memory requests so kubelet's eviction signal triggers in time · larger nodes if pods are large.

29.9 Cluster & Cloud — platform failures

The control plane and cloud substrate. Rare but high-impact: when these fail, every service is potentially affected.

29.9.1 API server slow / overloaded

Signals
apiserver_request_duration_seconds p99 high · throttling errors in controller logs · kubectl commands slow.
Root causes
Misbehaving controller polling too aggressively · large list/watch operations · etcd slowdown propagating · DDoS from client tools.
Handle
API Priority & Fairness throttles low-priority clients while protecting critical ones (kubelet, scheduler).
Recover
Identify the noisy client and stop it. Scale apiserver replicas. Fix etcd if that's the bottleneck.
Prevent
Audit logging on apiserver requests · rate limits per service account · informer-based controllers (cached, not poll-based) · monitor apiserver request rate per client.

29.9.2 etcd slow / split-brain

Signals
etcd_disk_wal_fsync_duration_seconds p99 > 100ms · leader election storms · apiserver writes slow.
Root causes
Slow disk on etcd nodes · network partition between etcd nodes · oversized cluster state · too many clients watching.
Handle
etcd cluster maintains quorum (3 or 5 nodes). One node down = still healthy.
Recover
Move to faster disk · compact / defragment etcd · spread etcd nodes across AZs to reduce network correlation · scale etcd cluster.
Prevent
SSDs (NVMe) for etcd · monitor disk fsync latency · regular defrag · compaction policy tuned to workload · etcd cluster spread across failure domains.

29.9.3 Scheduler stuck / unable to place pods

Signals
Pods Pending despite nodes available · scheduler logs full of placement errors · kube_scheduler_pod_scheduling_attempts high.
Root causes
Over-constrained pod specs (affinity rules can't be satisfied) · scheduler bug · custom scheduler plugins misbehaving.
Handle
Existing pods continue running. Only new placements affected.
Recover
Restart scheduler · simplify constraints on stuck pods · roll back custom scheduler plugins.
Prevent
Validate constraints in CI · multiple scheduler replicas (active/standby) · monitor scheduler latency.

29.9.4 CNI / network plugin broken

Signals
Pod-to-pod connectivity failures · IP allocation failures (pods stuck ContainerCreating) · CNI DaemonSet pods crashing.
Root causes
CNI plugin upgrade gone wrong · IP pool exhausted · BGP issues (Calico) · IPAM corruption.
Handle
Existing pods may continue to function depending on the CNI failure mode. New pods can't be created.
Recover
Restart CNI DaemonSet pods · expand IP pool · revert CNI upgrade · vendor-specific recovery procedures.
Prevent
Stage CNI upgrades in non-prod · monitor IP pool usage · alert on CNI pod restarts · IPv6 dual-stack reduces v4 exhaustion risk.

29.9.5 Cluster Autoscaler / Karpenter unable to scale

Signals
Pods stuck Pending · autoscaler logs show errors · cloud quota / capacity errors.
Root causes
Cloud instance quota hit · IAM role missing permissions · spot instances unavailable · node group at maxNodes.
Handle
Existing pods unaffected. New pods queue.
Recover
Increase quota · fix IAM · raise maxNodes · fall back to on-demand if spot unavailable.
Prevent
Quota alerts well below limit · multi-AZ node groups · mixed spot/on-demand strategies · autoscaler observability dashboards.

29.9.6 Cloud region / AZ outage

Signals
Sudden node count drop · cloud provider status page incident · widespread connection failures · entire AZ unreachable.
Root causes
Cloud provider regional incident · networking issues at the cloud level · DNS / metadata service failures.
Handle
If multi-AZ: traffic shifts to surviving zones automatically (PodTopologySpread + anti-affinity ensure replicas exist in other zones). If single-AZ: total outage in the affected AZ.
Recover
Wait for provider to restore the AZ. If multi-region: failover DNS / global LB to healthy region.
Prevent
Multi-AZ as default for any production workload · multi-region for the most critical paths · regular failover drills · runbook for region-level failover · clear understanding of stateful service replication topology.

29.9.7 Control plane unreachable

Signals
kubectl commands time out · controllers can't reconcile · kube-system pods unhealthy · API server endpoint unreachable.
Root causes
Managed Kubernetes provider incident · network path between data plane and control plane broken · etcd majority loss.
Handle
Critical insight: data plane keeps running even if control plane is down. Existing pods continue to serve traffic. Services keep routing. You just can't deploy or auto-recover.
Recover
Fix or wait out the control plane issue. For self-managed clusters: restore etcd from backup if quorum is lost.
Prevent
Managed K8s with strong SLAs · multi-master setup with etcd quorum across failure domains · runbook for control-plane-down scenario emphasizing "don't panic — data plane is fine" · etcd backups tested regularly.
How to use this taxonomy. When designing a new service: walk through the relevant subsections and ask "do we have a Detect, Handle, Recover, Prevent for this?" Most teams discover their Detect coverage is decent and their Prevent coverage is thin — that gap is where production incidents are born. The next section turns this into an at-a-glance matrix.

§36 · Failure ReferenceType × Phase Matrix — the master reference

A grid that ties Part III together — one row per failure from §8, one column per phase from §27. Print it; tape it next to your monitor.

30.2 How to use the matrix

For any new service or feature, walk through the rows that apply and ask: "For each failure type, do we have signal? Alert? Handler? Recovery? Prevention?" Gaps in any column are work to do. Most service teams discover they have decent Detect coverage, weaker Handle coverage, and very little Prevent coverage — that's where to invest.

The deeper insight. The same failure can be tackled at any phase, but the cost rises and the user impact rises as you move right. Prevent is cheap and invisible. Detect+Handle means a brief blip. Recover means a real incident. Get good at the early phases and the late phases barely fire.

30.3 Closing — the connection back to observability

This whole part was about doing things to failures. But notice: every action in every phase depends on signals — metrics, logs, traces, events. You can't detect without signals. You can't handle without knowing dependency state. You can't recover without runbook context. You can't prevent without postmortem evidence.

That's why Parts I and II of this guide existed: observability is the substrate that makes failure management possible. Build the substrate first; then the discipline you build on top of it has somewhere to stand.

Part IV — Kubernetes

Putting it all together on K8s

Now apply everything from Parts I–III specifically to Kubernetes. K8s concepts (what changes vs traditional ops), each of the three pillars implemented in K8s, and operational practices (dashboards, full cluster architecture, troubleshooting playbook).

§37 · K8s ConceptsWhat changes when you run on K8s

Kubernetes solves real problems but it makes observability harder in five specific ways. Knowing them upfront saves weeks of debugging.

ProblemWhy it's hard on K8sWhat you need
