Pods are ephemeralA pod's IP and name change every restart. order-svc-7d8f4b-xy2 is dead in 5 min.Identity by label, not IP. Service discovery in your scraper.
Logs are stdout, not filesContainers write to stdout. The kubelet captures it to /var/log/pods/. If the pod dies, those log files get rotated/deleted.Node-level log forwarder (Fluent Bit, Vector) tails files before they vanish.
Many short-lived processesJobs, CronJobs, init containers run for seconds. A 30s scrape interval misses them entirely.Pushgateway for jobs, sidecar pattern, or short scrape intervals.
Multi-layer failuresWas it the app? container? pod? node? network? control plane? Lots of layers can fail.Telemetry from every layer: app, kubelet, node-exporter, kube-state-metrics, control plane.
Cardinality explosionEvery pod has a unique name. If you label metrics by pod name, your TSDB cardinality explodes on every deploy.Use stable labels (deployment, service, namespace) — drop pod-instance labels at ingestion.
The pod-name trap

A common mistake: scraping pods with the auto-generated pod name (order-svc-7d8f4b-xy2) as a label. Every rolling deploy creates new pods with new names → cardinality grows linearly with deploys → after a month your TSDB is on fire. Relabel in the Prometheus config to drop pod hashes.

§38 · K8s ConceptsThe observability stack inside a cluster

Before any YAML, here is the topology — what runs where, and what talks to what. Internalize this picture and the rest of Part II is just filling in details.

A complete K8s observability stack. Three classes of components: DaemonSets (one per node), backends (in monitoring namespace), and your application pods.

Why DaemonSets for collectors

You see node-exporter, fluent-bit, and otel-collector deployed as DaemonSets — exactly one replica per node. This is by design:

  • node-exporter reads /proc and /sys — these are per-node, so you need one collector per node.
  • fluent-bit tails /var/log/pods/ — log files live on the node, one collector per node reaches them via hostPath mount.
  • otel-collector receives traces over the loopback interface from app pods on the same node — keeps inter-node traffic minimal.

§39 · Availability & Resilience in K8sAvailability in Kubernetes

Quick Reference · Availability in K8s

K8s gives you the primitives for HA out of the box: Deployments for replicating stateless workloads, topology spread constraints for AZ distribution, PodDisruptionBudgets to survive voluntary disruptions, and Services for load-balanced traffic. The art is configuring them deliberately. See §9 for the SRE theory of availability; this section is the K8s implementation.

K8s primitives that deliver Availability

Deployment
replicas: N+M
Declarative replication for stateless workloads
Topology spread
across AZs / nodes
Survive zone failure
PodDisruptionBudget
minAvailable: N
Bound voluntary disruptions
Service
load-balanced
Stable VIP routing to healthy pods

Patterns & practices

Deployment with replicas ≥ 3 topologySpreadConstraints PDB with minAvailable Multi-AZ node pools Anti-affinity rules Service-level LB
Validate spread before deploy Monitor pod distribution Test node drain / cordon Track availability per service Plan for control-plane failures

Real scenario · Why your "3-replica" service was actually a single point of failure

3 replicas, 1 AZ, 1 node — the silent SPOF
order-svc has replicas: 3. Looks safe. The scheduler placed all 3 on the same node (no spread constraints, the cluster had 1 underutilized node). The node experiences a kernel panic. All 3 pods die simultaneously. Service unavailable for 2 minutes while pods reschedule. Fix: add topologySpreadConstraints with maxSkew: 1 on topology.kubernetes.io/zone AND on kubernetes.io/hostname — forcing distribution across both zones AND nodes.

K8s makes high availability achievable, but not automatic. You must configure it. The defaults (replicas:1, no spread, no PDB) are the bare minimum for development — and a guaranteed SEV-1 in production. This section covers the K8s primitives that deliver availability, how to wire them together, and how to verify they actually work.

39.1 The K8s availability stack — what each layer contributes

LayerPrimitiveWhat it provides
ProcessPod restart policyCrashed container auto-restarts in same pod
PodDeployment / StatefulSetCrashed pod auto-replaced by controller
NodeTopology spread + anti-affinityPods distributed across nodes; node failure ≠ outage
ZoneTopology spread on zonePods across AZs; zone failure ≠ outage
RegionMulti-cluster federation / DNS failoverRegion failure handled by §48 DR
DisruptionPodDisruptionBudgetVoluntary disruptions (drains, upgrades) bounded
TrafficService + readiness probesTraffic routes only to ready pods

Each layer protects against a different scale of failure. Skipping any of them creates a class of failure that's not handled.

39.2 The full HA Deployment manifest

This is the canonical pattern for any production stateless service:

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: order-svc
  namespace: jahez-prod
  labels:
    app: order-svc
    tier: critical
spec:
  replicas: 5                              # N=3 + M=2 (survives single failure during deploy)
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxUnavailable: 1                      # at most 1 pod down at any time
      maxSurge: 1                            # at most 1 extra during rollout
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: order-svc }
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: order-svc
        version: v2.15.0
    spec:
      topologySpreadConstraints:
      - maxSkew: 1                            # spread evenly across zones
        topologyKey: topology.kubernetes.io/zone
        whenUnsatisfiable: DoNotSchedule
        labelSelector:
          matchLabels: { app: order-svc }
      - maxSkew: 1                            # also spread across nodes
        topologyKey: kubernetes.io/hostname
        whenUnsatisfiable: ScheduleAnyway        # softer constraint
        labelSelector:
          matchLabels: { app: order-svc }
      containers:
      - name: app
        image: ghcr.io/jahez/order-svc:v2.15.0
        ports: [{ containerPort: 8080 }]
        readinessProbe:                          # traffic only when ready
          httpGet: { path: /readyz, port: 8080 }
          periodSeconds: 5
          failureThreshold: 2
        livenessProbe:                            # restart if hung
          httpGet: { path: /healthz, port: 8080 }
          periodSeconds: 10
          failureThreshold: 3
        resources:
          requests: { cpu: "500m", memory: "512Mi" }
          limits:   { cpu: "1000m", memory: "1Gi" }
---
apiVersion: policy/v1
kind: PodDisruptionBudget
metadata:
  name: order-svc
  namespace: jahez-prod
spec:
  minAvailable: 3                            # N=3 must always be up
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: order-svc }
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata: { name: order-svc, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  selector: { app: order-svc }
  ports: [{ port: 80, targetPort: 8080 }]
  type: ClusterIP                              # L4 LB; pair with Ingress for L7

Every line in this manifest exists for a reason. maxUnavailable: 1 means rollouts never take more than one pod out at once. topologySpreadConstraints with DoNotSchedule guarantees zone distribution even if it means refusing to schedule. minAvailable: 3 in the PDB means a node drain blocks if it would take you below 3 healthy pods.

39.3 Probes — the gate between Service and traffic

The Service routes to pods labeled "Ready." Readiness is determined by the readiness probe:

ProbePurposeWhat happens on failure
readinessProbe"Should I receive traffic?"Removed from Service endpoints (no traffic)
livenessProbe"Am I still alive?"Container killed and restarted
startupProbe"Have I finished starting?"Container killed if startup never completes

The most common K8s availability bug: readiness probe that's too aggressive. If /readyz returns 503 during a brief downstream blip, your pod gets removed from the Service, traffic goes to fewer pods, they overload, more pods fail readiness, cascade. Set probes to fail only on conditions where the pod genuinely cannot serve traffic.

39.4 Multi-AZ topology — what good distribution looks like

Run kubectl get pods -l app=order-svc -o wide and verify pods are spread across nodes AND zones. The classic check:

$ kubectl get pods -l app=order-svc -o wide \
    --no-headers | awk '{print $7}' | sort | uniq -c
   2 ip-10-0-1-22.ec2.internal     # zone us-east-1a — 2 pods
   2 ip-10-0-2-15.ec2.internal     # zone us-east-1b — 2 pods
   1 ip-10-0-3-8.ec2.internal      # zone us-east-1c — 1 pod

# Zone distribution:
   2 us-east-1a
   2 us-east-1b
   1 us-east-1c        # maxSkew: 1 satisfied (max - min = 1)

If pods cluster in one zone, your spread constraints aren't working — investigate node labels or scheduler taints.

39.5 Voluntary vs involuntary disruptions

TypeExamplesBounded by
VoluntaryNode drain, cluster upgrade, deployPodDisruptionBudget
InvoluntaryHardware failure, kernel panic, OOMTopology spread + replica count

PDBs only protect against voluntary disruptions. Hardware failure ignores PDBs entirely. You need both — replicas spread across zones for involuntary, plus PDB for voluntary.

39.6 Verifying availability — drain test

The single most useful test of K8s availability: drain a node and watch what happens.

# In a non-prod cluster — pick a node hosting order-svc pods
$ kubectl drain ip-10-0-1-22.ec2.internal \
    --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data

# During the drain, in another terminal:
$ watch 'kubectl get pods -l app=order-svc -o wide'
$ watch 'curl -w "%{http_code}\n" -o /dev/null -s http://order-svc/healthz'

# Expected: pods reschedule to other nodes; 200 responses continue uninterrupted.
# Failure mode: drain blocked by PDB → fix PDB; OR brief 5xx → readiness probe too lenient.

39.7 The ceiling — what K8s alone cannot give you

K8s primitives realistically deliver 99.9% – 99.95% availability for well-configured stateless services. Past that requires:

  • 99.99%+ — multi-region active-active (§48), and very careful capacity planning to avoid cascading failures during failover
  • True FT — synchronous cross-region replication for the data tier; consensus systems (etcd, CockroachDB) for state
  • Control-plane HA — most managed K8s services give 99.95% control-plane SLA; if you need higher, you may need data-plane independence from control-plane

39.8 Connection

This section covers the K8s implementation. For the SRE theory of availability — the 9s, MTBF/MTTR, composite chain math, the cost curve — see §9. For the resilience patterns that make individual pods survive their own failures, see §40 (Resilience in K8s) next.

§40 · Availability & Resilience in K8sResilience / Fault Tolerance in Kubernetes

Quick Reference · Resilience in K8s

K8s gives you self-healing by default: crashed processes restart, failed pods get replaced, unhealthy nodes drain. Beyond that, service mesh (Istio, Linkerd) adds circuit breakers, retries, timeouts, and bulkheads at the network layer — without app code changes. See §10 for the resilience theory; this is the K8s implementation.

K8s primitives that deliver Resilience

RestartPolicy
Always (default)
Crashed containers auto-restart
ReplicaSet controller
Auto-replace pods
Lost pod → new pod scheduled
Liveness probes
Detect & restart hung
Process alive but stuck → restart
Service mesh
L7 resilience patterns
Retry, circuit-break, timeout — no app code

Patterns & practices

Liveness probe + auto-restart Init containers for warmup Sidecar circuit breaker (Istio) Retry budget (mesh policy) Timeout per call (mesh) Outlier detection Pod anti-affinity
Tune probe thresholds carefully Set retry budgets on mesh Chaos engineering (Litmus, Chaos Mesh) Monitor restart count per pod Test outlier detection thresholds

Real scenario · Liveness probe gone wrong

Liveness probe = readiness probe → restart loop
A team copy-pasted the readiness probe as the liveness probe. The pod's /readyz returns 503 if the database is briefly unreachable (correct behavior). Now the LIVENESS probe ALSO returns 503 → kubelet kills the pod → ReplicaSet schedules new pod → new pod can't reach DB either → killed → cascade. The fix: liveness should only fail when the process is truly broken (e.g., deadlocked, OOMing). Database connectivity is a readiness concern, not liveness.

K8s self-healing handles most pod-level failures automatically — but only if probes and restart policies are configured correctly. For higher-level resilience (circuit breakers, retries, bulkheads), the modern answer is a service mesh: same patterns as in §10, but injected at the sidecar layer instead of in application code.

40.1 K8s self-healing — the layers

LayerFailureDetectionRecovery
ContainerProcess crashExit code != 0kubelet restarts container (~seconds)
ContainerHung processLiveness probe failskubelet kills + restarts container
PodPod evicted (resource pressure)kubelet evictionReplicaSet schedules new pod (~10s)
PodNode lostNode controller after grace periodPods rescheduled to healthy nodes (~5 min)
ServicePod unhealthyReadiness probe failsRemoved from Service endpoints (no traffic)

40.2 The probe trinity — get them right

spec:
  containers:
  - name: app
    image: ghcr.io/jahez/order-svc:v2.15.0

    # StartupProbe — gives slow-starting apps time before liveness kicks in
    startupProbe:
      httpGet: { path: /healthz, port: 8080 }
      failureThreshold: 30                 # 30 × 10s = 5 min to start
      periodSeconds: 10

    # LivenessProbe — only for "process is dead/hung" — restart triggers reschedule
    livenessProbe:
      httpGet: { path: /healthz, port: 8080 }   # shallow check only
      periodSeconds: 10
      timeoutSeconds: 3
      failureThreshold: 3                  # 30s of failures before restart

    # ReadinessProbe — "ready to serve traffic" — controls Service membership
    readinessProbe:
      httpGet: { path: /readyz, port: 8080 }   # can include dep checks
      periodSeconds: 5
      failureThreshold: 2

The three probes serve different purposes

  • Liveness = "should kubelet kill me?" — for deadlocks, OOMs, hung threads. Should NOT depend on downstream services.
  • Readiness = "should I receive traffic?" — can include shallow downstream checks (DB pool healthy?). Failing temporarily is fine; pod stays alive, traffic just routes elsewhere.
  • Startup = "have I finished starting?" — protects slow-starters from liveness. Once it passes once, the other two take over.

40.3 Service mesh — circuit breaker / retry / timeout without app code

For higher-level resilience patterns from §10, the cleanest implementation is at the mesh layer. With Istio:

# Outlier detection — eject failing pods from the LB pool (circuit breaker)
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: DestinationRule
metadata: { name: payment-svc, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  host: payment-svc
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      tcp: { maxConnections: 100 }
      http:
        http1MaxPendingRequests: 50      # bulkhead
        http2MaxRequests: 200
    outlierDetection:                    # circuit breaker
      consecutive5xxErrors: 5
      interval: 30s
      baseEjectionTime: 30s             # eject failing pod for 30s
      maxEjectionPercent: 50             # never eject > 50% of pods

---
# Retry policy + timeout — at the call site
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata: { name: payment-svc, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  hosts: [payment-svc]
  http:
  - timeout: 2s                          # bound the call (deadline)
    retries:
      attempts: 3
      perTryTimeout: 500ms
      retryOn: 5xx,reset,connect-failure   # don't retry 4xx
    route:
    - destination: { host: payment-svc }

The big win: app code stays simple — just http.Post(...), no retry/timeout/CB logic. The mesh enforces them uniformly across all services with consistent observability.

40.4 Bulkheads via namespaces and resource quotas

Pattern §10 (bulkheads) at the cluster level: separate failure domains so one team's mistake can't take down another team's services.

apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata: { name: orders-quota, namespace: jahez-orders }
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "40"                 # 40 CPU max for entire namespace
    requests.memory: "80Gi"
    limits.cpu: "60"
    limits.memory: "120Gi"
    pods: "100"                          # max 100 pods
    persistentvolumeclaims: "20"
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: LimitRange
metadata: { name: orders-defaults, namespace: jahez-orders }
spec:
  limits:
  - type: Container
    defaultRequest: { cpu: "100m", memory: "128Mi" }
    default:        { cpu: "500m", memory: "512Mi" }
    max:            { cpu: "4",    memory: "4Gi" }

40.5 Chaos engineering on K8s

The discipline: introduce failures deliberately to verify resilience. Tools:

  • Chaos Mesh — CNCF project; pod kill, network delay, IO chaos, kernel chaos
  • Litmus — CNCF; structured experiment-based
  • kube-monkey — simpler; randomly kills pods labeled for chaos
  • Manual — kubectl delete pod in production (after warming up in staging)

The minimum useful chaos discipline: quarterly game day where the team kills a random pod, drains a random node, and verifies the system stays healthy. Most teams discover their PDBs are wrong, their probes are mistuned, or their replica counts are too low — exactly the things you want to find before customers do.

40.6 The Jahez calibration

ServiceRestartLivenessReadinessMesh CB
order-svcAlwaysShallow /healthzDeep /readyz (DB pool)5 errors / 30s
payment-svcAlwaysShallow /healthzDeep /readyz (gateway reachable)5 errors / 30s, eject 30s
recommendation-svcAlwaysShallow /healthzAlways ready (best-effort)10 errors / 60s
kitchen-dispatchAlwaysShallow /healthzDeep (Kafka producer ready)5 errors / 30s

40.7 Connection

For the resilience theory (resilience vs FT, redundancy ladder, full pattern catalog), see §10. For the catastrophic-event recovery that resilience cannot handle (region failures, ransomware), see §41 next.

§41 · Availability & Resilience in K8sRecoverability / Disaster Recovery in Kubernetes

Quick Reference · DR in K8s

K8s DR has three tiers: cluster-level (etcd backups, Velero for resources + volumes), data-tier (database cross-region replication, S3 cross-region replication), and application-level (multi-cluster federation with global LB). The K8s control plane itself is recoverable through GitOps — your cluster state lives in Git, restoration is "apply manifests to a fresh cluster." See §11 for the SRE theory of DR; this is the K8s implementation.

Three layers of K8s DR

Cluster state
etcd backups + GitOps
Manifests in Git → reapply to fresh cluster
Workload state
Velero + PV snapshots
Resources + volumes → backup & restore
Data tier
DB cross-region repl.
Postgres streaming · Kafka MirrorMaker
Multi-cluster
Global LB + DNS failover
Active-passive or active-active across regions

Patterns & practices

GitOps (Argo CD / Flux) Velero scheduled backups CSI volume snapshots Cross-region etcd backup DB streaming replication Route53 DNS failover Pilot Light DR cluster
Quarterly DR drills Test restore (not just backup) Track real RTO from drills Document runbook Backup encryption key escrow All cluster config in Git

Real scenario · The "GitOps saved us" cluster rebuild

Cluster destroyed by accidental terraform apply — recovered in 90 minutes
A junior engineer runs terraform destroy against the wrong workspace. Production EKS cluster gone. Everything is gone EXCEPT: cluster manifests in Git, Velero backups in S3 cross-region bucket, and a Postgres replica in the DR region. Recovery: (1) terraform create fresh cluster (~20 min), (2) bootstrap Argo CD pointing at the manifests repo (~5 min), (3) Argo CD applies all manifests to fresh cluster (~10 min), (4) Velero restores volumes (~30 min), (5) promote DR Postgres to primary, point apps at it (~10 min), (6) verify, switch traffic (~15 min). Total: ~90 minutes. The Git repo is your real backup. Treat it accordingly.

DR in K8s is fundamentally different from DR in traditional systems because K8s state is declarative — your cluster is the result of applying manifests. If those manifests live in Git, the cluster itself is largely reproducible. The hard parts are the data tier (which is NOT in Git) and the orchestration of failover. This section covers the three-tier K8s DR model and how to actually execute it.

41.1 What needs to survive a disaster

ComponentState?Backup mechanism
Deployments / Services / ConfigMapsDeclarative (in Git)Git repo + Argo CD
SecretsSensitive — NOT in GitExternal Secrets Operator + cloud KMS
etcd (control plane)Source of truth for K8sCloud-managed (EKS/GKE) OR scheduled snapshots
PersistentVolumesApp data on volumesVelero + CSI snapshots
Stateful databases (Postgres pods)App data inside DBDB-native replication, NOT volume snapshots
External cloud DBs (RDS)App dataProvider-native cross-region replicas + backups
Object storage (S3)App dataS3 cross-region replication
Container imagesBuild artifactsRegistry replication (ECR/GCR cross-region)

The pattern: infrastructure and configuration in Git, ephemeral state in K8s, persistent state in dedicated storage with its own replication strategy. Each tier has a different backup approach.

41.2 GitOps — your real backup

If every cluster resource is created by Argo CD or Flux from Git manifests, then losing the cluster is recoverable by pointing a fresh cluster at the same Git repo. The Git history IS your audit trail and rollback target.

# Argo CD Application — declares "make this namespace match Git"
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Application
metadata:
  name: jahez-prod-services
  namespace: argocd
spec:
  project: jahez
  source:
    repoURL: https://github.com/jahez/k8s-manifests
    targetRevision: main
    path: prod/services
  destination:
    server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
    namespace: jahez-prod
  syncPolicy:
    automated:
      prune: true                       # delete resources removed from Git
      selfHeal: true                    # revert manual edits
    syncOptions:
    - CreateNamespace=true

The "selfHeal: true" is important — it means anyone who manually edits a Deployment in production has their change reverted within minutes. The cluster IS the Git repo, in continuously-applied form.

41.3 Velero — backing up what's NOT in Git

Velero handles the gap: PersistentVolume contents, secrets that are dynamically generated, dynamically-created resources. It backs up resource definitions to S3 + takes CSI snapshots of volumes.

# Velero scheduled backup — daily snapshot of jahez-prod namespace
apiVersion: velero.io/v1
kind: Schedule
metadata:
  name: jahez-prod-daily
  namespace: velero
spec:
  schedule: "0 2 * * *"                  # 02:00 daily
  template:
    includedNamespaces: [jahez-prod]
    snapshotVolumes: true
    ttl: "720h"                            # 30-day retention
    storageLocation: aws-cross-region        # bucket in DR region
    volumeSnapshotLocations: [aws-csi]

Volume snapshots are NOT a substitute for database-level backups. A snapshot of a Postgres data directory taken mid-write may be corrupt. For databases, use the database's own backup mechanism (pg_dump, WAL archiving, RDS snapshots) — Velero is for stateless app data.

41.4 The data-tier replication patterns

Per §11.4 (Pilot Light topology), the data tier replicates continuously to the DR region while app pods are scaled to zero there.

Data typePatternRPO achievable
Postgres (StatefulSet or RDS)Streaming replication to DR replicaSeconds (replication lag)
Postgres (point-in-time)WAL archiving to S3Configurable, ~1 minute
KafkaMirrorMaker 2 to DR cluster~minutes
RedisReplication for failover; RDB snapshots for recoverySeconds for failover; minutes for restore
S3Cross-region replication enabled~15 min typical
etcd (DIY clusters)Snapshot every 30 min, ship to S330 min

41.5 Multi-cluster active-active — the highest tier

For systems requiring true zero-downtime regional failover, run two (or more) clusters serving live traffic with a global LB:

# Route53 weighted routing with health checks
jahez.com     →   weighted records
                  ├── 50% → me-central-1 ALB (cluster A)  [health-check]
                  └── 50% → me-central-2 ALB (cluster B)  [health-check]

# Each ALB front-ends an EKS cluster running the full stack
# DB tier: CockroachDB or Spanner-style multi-region (both write-active)
#       OR primary in one region + sync replica in other (one writer at a time)
# A failed health check removes that region from the LB pool within seconds.

The hard part is the data tier: active-active app tiers are easy; active-active data tiers require either a multi-region database (Spanner, CockroachDB, DynamoDB Global Tables) or careful sharding by user/region. Most "active-active" K8s deployments are actually active-passive at the data tier with fast failover.

41.6 The DR runbook for K8s

For a Pilot Light topology (cluster pre-provisioned in DR region with workloads scaled to zero):

  1. Detect & declare — confirm primary cluster genuinely down, declare DR via §34
  2. Promote data tier — promote Postgres replica, confirm WAL position, check Kafka cluster health
  3. Scale up app tier — Argo CD already deployed manifests; scale Deployment replicas from 0 → production count via overlay or ApplicationSet
  4. Wait for readiness — readiness probes confirm pods can reach DB
  5. Switch traffic — update Route53 weighted records (or fail health check on primary) to direct traffic to DR cluster
  6. Verify business correctness — synthetic checkout, validate orders persist
  7. Communicate — status page, executive briefing
  8. Failback planning — when primary returns, controlled cutback in maintenance window

41.7 The Jahez K8s DR posture

CLUSTER STATE
  All manifests in github.com/jahez/k8s-manifests
  Argo CD with selfHeal=true in both regions
  Cluster bootstrap: terraform + 1 helm chart (Argo CD)

APP STATE (Velero)
  Daily backups, 30-day retention
  Cross-region S3 storage location
  Quarterly restore drill in DR region

DATA TIER
  RDS Postgres in me-central-1 with cross-region replica in me-central-2
    streaming replication, lag < 30s typical
    daily backups, PITR for 7 days
  Kafka with MirrorMaker 2 mirroring core topics
  S3 buckets with cross-region replication enabled

FAILOVER
  Pilot Light pattern: DR cluster runs at 0 replicas in normal operation
  Route53 health check on primary ALB; auto-failover < 60s
  RTO: 45 minutes (measured in Q3 drill)
  RPO: ~30 seconds (replication lag)

41.8 Connection

For DR theory (RTO/RPO, the four site types, backup strategies), see §11. The K8s availability/resilience story (§39, §40) handles all the failures DR doesn't have to handle — the goal of HA + resilience is to invoke DR as rarely as possible, because DR is always more disruptive than failover within a healthy cluster.

§42 · Performance in K8sPerformance / Latency in Kubernetes

Quick Reference · Performance in K8s

K8s itself rarely IS the latency problem — but it provides the levers that determine how much of your latency budget the platform consumes. The big ones: resource requests (right-sized = no CPU throttling), QoS classes (Guaranteed = stable), node affinity (data locality), and avoiding cross-AZ hops. See §12 for the latency theory; this is the K8s implementation.

K8s primitives that influence Performance

Resource requests/limits
CPU + Memory
Wrong values = throttling = latency spikes
QoS class
Guaranteed / Burstable / BestEffort
Eviction order under pressure
Node affinity
Pod placement
Co-locate with deps to avoid network hops
Topology-aware routing
Service endpoints
Prefer same-zone endpoints (cuts cross-AZ latency)

Patterns & practices

Right-size requests = limits (Guaranteed) Topology-aware Services Node affinity for data locality Pod anti-affinity for spread Init containers for warmup Pre-stop hooks for graceful drain
Profile real workloads (don't guess) Watch CPU throttling metric Set p99 latency SLO per service Test connection-pool sizing Monitor cross-AZ traffic costs & latency

Real scenario · The CPU-throttling mystery

"CPU is at 40% but p99 is 800ms — why?"
order-svc shows container_cpu_usage_seconds_total at 40% of the limit. p99 climbing during traffic peaks. The hidden metric: container_cpu_cfs_throttled_periods_total shows the kernel CFS scheduler is throttling 28% of CPU periods. The pod is bursting above its CPU request frequently and getting throttled even though average usage is "low." Fix: raise the CPU request to match peak burst (or remove the limit entirely for trusted workloads). Latency drops to 80ms.

Latency in a K8s service is the sum of: process service time (your code), platform overhead (cgroup throttling, scheduler, network plugin), and inter-service hops (mesh sidecars, DNS resolution, cross-AZ routing). K8s is rarely the largest term — but if it's mistuned, it can dominate. This section covers the K8s knobs that affect latency directly.

42.1 Resource requests & limits — the latency lever

The single most consequential performance setting in K8s. The cgroup constraints control how much CPU/memory the kernel will let your process use:

SettingEffectLatency impact
requests.cpuGuaranteed minimum allocationUsed by scheduler. Too low → pod placed on packed node → competes for CPU.
limits.cpuHard cap (CFS throttling)The killer. Too low → kernel throttles your process, latency climbs even at low avg CPU%.
requests.memoryScheduler reservationUsed for placement. Memory isn't compressible — request matters most.
limits.memoryOOM kill thresholdHit the limit → OOMKilled. No throttling for memory; just death.

CPU throttling — the silent latency killer

The most common K8s latency bug: pods set with low CPU limits get throttled by the kernel's CFS scheduler. Average CPU may show 30%, but p99 latency is terrible because every 100ms period the process gets paused mid-request.

# The metric to watch — throttled periods as % of total
sum(rate(container_cpu_cfs_throttled_periods_total{
    container="app", pod=~"order-svc-.*"
}[5m]))
/
sum(rate(container_cpu_cfs_periods_total{
    container="app", pod=~"order-svc-.*"
}[5m]))

# > 5% throttling = real latency impact. Raise the limit or remove it.

42.2 QoS classes — eviction order under pressure

K8s assigns each pod one of three QoS classes based on its requests/limits. This determines who gets killed first when a node runs out of memory:

QoS classHow to get itBehavior
Guaranteedrequests = limits, both setLast to be evicted. Stable for latency-critical.
Burstablerequests < limits, or only requests setEvicted before Guaranteed. Most pods.
BestEffortNo requests or limitsFirst to be evicted. Use only for batch.

For latency-critical services: use Guaranteed. Set requests = limits for both CPU and memory. The pod gets a stable allocation, isn't subject to neighbor-noise, and is last to be evicted.

resources:
  requests:
    cpu: "1000m"          # requests = limits → QoS: Guaranteed
    memory: "1Gi"
  limits:
    cpu: "1000m"
    memory: "1Gi"

42.3 Topology-aware routing — cut cross-AZ latency

Default K8s Service routing is random across all healthy endpoints — including endpoints in other AZs. Cross-AZ traffic adds 1-3ms of latency per hop AND costs egress fees. Modern K8s offers topology-aware hints:

apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: order-svc
  namespace: jahez-prod
  annotations:
    service.kubernetes.io/topology-mode: Auto   # prefer same-zone endpoints
spec:
  selector: { app: order-svc }
  ports: [{ port: 80, targetPort: 8080 }]
  internalTrafficPolicy: Local                   # or "Cluster" (default, all zones)

Local traffic policy combined with topology hints means a pod in zone a calls only zone a endpoints first — same-zone traffic is faster (~0.5ms) than cross-AZ (~2-3ms). At scale, this adds up to meaningful latency reduction AND big network egress savings.

42.4 Service mesh latency overhead

Service mesh sidecars (§40, §22) add 1-3ms per hop. In a 5-hop request chain that's 5-15ms of pure proxy overhead — not free. Mitigations:

  • Skip the mesh for hot internal paths. Use sidecar.istio.io/inject: "false" annotation on pods where mesh observability isn't worth the latency.
  • Enable HTTP/2 between sidecars. Multiplexes; cuts connection overhead.
  • Use eBPF-based meshes (Cilium service mesh). Skips the sidecar entirely; ~zero per-hop overhead.
  • Increase sidecar resources. A throttled sidecar adds latency to every request through the pod.

42.5 The pod-level latency budget — example

Total checkout p99 = 500ms. Where does it go in K8s?

# Where 500ms goes for a Jahez checkout request
Mobile network RTT to ALB:           50ms
ALB → Ingress controller:             1ms
Ingress → istio-ingressgateway:       2ms
ingressgateway → order-svc sidecar:   1ms (same node usually)
order-svc sidecar → app container:    0.3ms (localhost)
order-svc app processing:             80ms   // your code
order-svc → payment-svc (sidecar):    3ms (cross-AZ + mTLS)
payment-svc app processing:           250ms  // external mada call
order-svc → DB (in-cluster):          1ms (same AZ)
DB query + WAL flush:                 30ms
Response back through chain:          5ms (combined)
                                      ─────
                              Total: ~423ms (77ms safety margin)

The K8s overhead in this budget: ~12ms (mesh sidecars + cross-AZ hops). If you're on a tight budget, audit those — same-zone routing + selective sidecar opt-out can recover 5-10ms.

42.6 Connection pool sizing inside K8s

From §13 (Little's Law): connection pool size = throughput × latency. In K8s:

  • Per-pod pools sum across replicas. 10 pods × 30 connections = 300 connections to the DB. The DB has a maxConnections setting — make sure your sum doesn't exceed it.
  • HPA scale-up multiplies pools. 10 pods → 50 pods = 5× the connection demand. The DB might not be sized for that.
  • Use connection poolers (PgBouncer for Postgres). The poolerruns as a separate deployment; apps connect to it; it multiplexes onto a smaller real-DB pool. Decouples pod count from DB connections.

42.7 Pod startup as latency — readinessGate & pre-warming

From §14 (cold-start problem): newly-scheduled pods serve traffic before they're warm — JIT not done, connection pools empty, cache empty. Two K8s mechanisms:

# Startup probe — keeps pod from being marked ready until truly warm
startupProbe:
  httpGet: { path: /readyz/warm, port: 8080 }
  failureThreshold: 60          # up to 5 minutes before giving up
  periodSeconds: 5

# PostStart hook — drive synthetic warm-up traffic
lifecycle:
  postStart:
    exec:
      command:
      - sh
      - -c
      - "./bin/warmup --requests=200 --concurrency=10"

The /readyz/warm endpoint can check internal indicators: are connection pools full? Has the JIT seen N requests? Are caches loaded? Only return 200 when all of those are true.

42.8 The Jahez calibration

order-svc — latency-critical
  QoS:                Guaranteed (requests = limits)
  CPU request/limit:  500m / 500m   # profiled at peak
  Memory:             512Mi / 512Mi
  Throttling alert:   > 2% throttled periods

recommendation-svc — best-effort
  QoS:                Burstable (requests < limits)
  CPU:                100m request, 500m limit  # OK to throttle if loaded
  Memory:             256Mi / 512Mi

batch-export-job — not user-facing
  QoS:                BestEffort (no limits)
  Runs on spot nodes; first to be evicted under pressure

42.9 Connection

For latency theory (Little's Law, percentiles, hockey stick, latency budgets), see §12. K8s implements the levers; the theory tells you which to pull and when. The next section (§43 Scalability) covers how K8s lets you scale capacity to keep latency stable as load grows.

§43 · Performance in K8sScalability in Kubernetes

Quick Reference · Scalability in K8s

K8s is purpose-built for horizontal scalability: Deployments and StatefulSets let you scale stateless and stateful workloads with a number. The hard parts are at the edges — sharding, sticky sessions, and shared bottlenecks (DB, distributed locks). See §13 for scalability theory; this section covers how K8s primitives map to its patterns.

K8s primitives that deliver Scalability

Deployment
Stateless workloads
Trivial: change replicas number
StatefulSet
Stateful, ordered
Stable identities, ordered start/stop
DaemonSet
One per node
Scales with cluster, not load
Job / CronJob
Batch parallelism
parallelism + completions for fan-out work

Patterns & practices

Stateless services on Deployment Sharded data via StatefulSet Read replicas behind Service External Redis / DB (not in cluster) JWT auth (no sticky sessions) Connection pooler (PgBouncer)
Identify the next bottleneck Load-test at projected scale Capacity plan per shared resource Don't run heavy DBs in-cluster Make services stateless

Real scenario · "We added more pods but throughput didn't go up"

Adding pods doesn't help when the bottleneck is downstream
order-svc scales 10 → 30 pods to handle Friday peak. Throughput barely moves. Investigation: the Postgres primary is at 95% CPU. Adding more pods just adds more clients hitting the same overwhelmed DB. The fix isn't K8s — it's database-side: PgBouncer to multiplex connections, then read replicas to offload analytics queries, eventually sharding by city. K8s scales the app tier; you have to know whether the app tier is your actual bottleneck.

K8s makes horizontal scaling easy for stateless workloads — change a number, the controller does the rest. The interesting questions are: when is your service actually stateless? what's the next bottleneck after the app tier? and how do you scale the parts that aren't trivially replicable (databases, queues, distributed locks)?

43.1 Deployment vs StatefulSet — choose deliberately

 DeploymentStatefulSet
Pod identityRandom hash suffix (interchangeable)Ordinal (pod-0, pod-1, pod-2…)
StorageShared / ephemeral / externalPer-pod PVC (each pod has dedicated volume)
Startup orderParallelSequential (0 → 1 → 2)
Network identityService load-balancesHeadless Service: pod-0.svc, pod-1.svc…
Use caseWeb apps, APIs, workers — most thingsPostgres, Kafka, Cassandra, leader-elected services
ScalingTrivial — change replicasPossible but careful (resharding, leader election)

The general rule: start with Deployment; use StatefulSet only when you genuinely need stable identity AND per-pod storage. Most applications you think need StatefulSet actually don't — they need an external database (RDS, etc.) and a stateless app tier on Deployment.

43.2 The stateless requirement — patterns to get there

For Deployment to scale, the service must be stateless — any pod can serve any request. Patterns from §13:

Anti-patternK8s-native fix
Server-side sessionsJWT (token carries identity) OR external Redis session store
Local file uploadsS3 / object storage — pods just generate signed URLs
In-memory cacheExternal Redis / Memcached — Deployment in cluster or managed service
Sticky sessionsRestructure protocol; use JWT; OR if truly needed, use Service annotations for affinity
Local SQLite / file DBExternal database (RDS, Cloud SQL, etc.)
WebSocket session stateExternal pub/sub backplane (Redis pub/sub) so any pod can serve any connection

43.3 Sharding via StatefulSet — when one bottleneck splits into N

Sharding pattern from §13.6: split data by key, each shard handled by a different replica. K8s gives you the substrate for this:

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata: { name: order-shard, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  serviceName: order-shard-headless
  replicas: 8                              # 8 shards
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: order-shard } }
  template:
    metadata: { labels: { app: order-shard } }
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: app
        image: ghcr.io/jahez/order-shard:v1.4.0
        env:
        - name: SHARD_INDEX
          valueFrom:
            fieldRef: { fieldPath: metadata.labels['apps.kubernetes.io/pod-index'] }
        - name: TOTAL_SHARDS
          value: "8"
        volumeMounts:
        - { name: data, mountPath: /var/lib/shard }
  volumeClaimTemplates:                       # each pod gets own PVC
  - metadata: { name: data }
    spec:
      accessModes: [ReadWriteOnce]
      resources: { requests: { storage: 100Gi } }

Each pod (order-shard-0, order-shard-1, …, order-shard-7) handles a fraction of users. Routing decision is in the app tier ahead of it: shard_id = hash(user_id) % 8; route to order-shard-{shard_id}.order-shard-headless.jahez-prod.svc.

43.4 Read replica pattern — scaling reads

For data-heavy services, the cleanest path is one primary + N read replicas. K8s pattern: separate Services for read and write traffic.

# Headless service for primary (writes)
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata: { name: postgres-primary, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  selector: { app: postgres, role: primary }
  ports: [{ port: 5432 }]

---
# LB service for replicas (reads)
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata: { name: postgres-replicas, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  selector: { app: postgres, role: replica }
  ports: [{ port: 5432 }]

App connection strings: writes use postgres-primary, reads use postgres-replicas. Read traffic spreads across all replicas; primary stays unloaded for writes. Most apps can scale a long way on read replicas before sharding becomes necessary.

43.5 Don't run heavy databases in-cluster (usually)

K8s CAN run Postgres, MySQL, Cassandra. But running mission-critical state in the cluster has serious operational complexity:

  • Backup/restore is YOUR problem (Velero CSI snapshots can be inconsistent for active DBs)
  • Failover is YOUR problem (Patroni, etcd quorum, careful StatefulSet config)
  • Upgrades are YOUR problem (major version migrations are intricate)
  • Performance tuning is YOUR problem (storage class IOPS, kernel parameters, NUMA)

For most teams, managed cloud databases (RDS, Aurora, Cloud SQL) are the right choice. They handle replication, failover, and backups out of the box. Use K8s for the stateless app tier; let the cloud provider run the database.

Exceptions where running DB in K8s makes sense: very specific operational requirements (data residency, custom configs), self-hosted by necessity, or smaller workloads where managed-DB cost outweighs the operational complexity.

43.6 Scaling shared-resource bottlenecks

Per §13.5: app pods can scale freely, but shared resources cap throughput. Common bottlenecks in K8s and how to scale past them:

BottleneckHow to scale
Single Postgres primaryRead replicas → Connection pooler → Sharding (StatefulSet) → CockroachDB-style multi-master
Single RedisRedis Cluster (sharded) — deploy as StatefulSet OR managed (ElastiCache)
Distributed locksShard the lock space; use lease-based locks (etcd, Consul) instead of mutex
Kafka partition limitsAdd partitions (forward-only); rebalance consumer groups
External API rate limitToken-bucket caching layer in K8s; batch requests
DNS lookupsNodeLocal DNSCache DaemonSet — caches DNS per node
Service mesh control planeMultiple replicas of istiod; or sharded mesh

43.7 Cluster-level scalability limits

K8s itself has scaling limits per cluster. From the official scalability targets:

  • ~5,000 nodes per cluster (managed offerings vary; EKS officially supports up to 5,000)
  • ~150,000 pods per cluster
  • ~300,000 containers
  • ~100 pods per node (kubelet practical limit, varies)

If you're approaching these, the answer isn't "make K8s bigger" — it's to shard at the cluster level: multiple clusters, federated or with multi-cluster service discovery (Cilium ClusterMesh, Istio multi-cluster). Each cluster stays comfortably below scale limits.

43.8 The Jahez calibration

CURRENT (Q4 2025)
  Cluster:      80 nodes, 1,200 pods, 1 region
  order-svc:    Deployment, 25 replicas, stateless
  payment-svc:  Deployment, 12 replicas, stateless + idempotent
  Postgres:     RDS (managed) — primary + 3 read replicas
  Redis:        ElastiCache cluster mode, 6 shards
  Kafka:        MSK with 12 brokers, 24 partitions on hot topics

PROJECTED (Q4 2026, +50% YoY)
  Cluster:      ~120 nodes
  order-svc:    Will need ~38 replicas at peak
  Postgres:     Read replicas to 5; consider read-only Aurora cluster for analytics
  Bottleneck:   Postgres write throughput at +80% growth → plan to shard by city
                  This is the next major scalability milestone (Q3 2026)

43.9 Connection

For scalability theory (vertical vs horizontal, USL, sharding strategies, the shared-bottleneck problem), see §13. K8s gives you the substrate; the theory guides which patterns to apply. The next section (§44 Elasticity) covers how K8s makes scaling automatic in response to load.

§44 · Performance in K8sElasticity in Kubernetes

Quick Reference · Elasticity in K8s

K8s elasticity has three layers: HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler) scales replicas, VPA (Vertical Pod Autoscaler) right-sizes pod resources, and Cluster Autoscaler / Karpenter provisions nodes. A complete elastic system uses all three. The big practical wins come from KEDA (event-driven scaling) and Karpenter (cost-aware node provisioning). See §14 for the elasticity theory.

K8s primitives that deliver Elasticity

HorizontalPodAutoscaler
Scale replicas
CPU, memory, custom, external metrics
VerticalPodAutoscaler
Right-size pods
Recommends or auto-applies CPU/memory requests
Karpenter
Node provisioning
Faster, cost-aware than Cluster Autoscaler
KEDA
Event-driven scaling
Kafka lag, queue depth, cron, etc.

Patterns & practices

HPA on real-load metric Scale up fast, down slow Scheduled pre-warming (KEDA cron) Spot + on-demand mix (Karpenter) PDB + Karpenter respect Pod priority classes
Right metric to scale on Test scaling under realistic load Don't fight HPA with VPA on same metric Buffer for cold-start gap Watch HPA event log for flapping

Real scenario · Lunch peak that auto-scaler couldn't catch

5 → 30 pods needed in 60 seconds — HPA caught up at minute 4
12:55 PM: lunch traffic begins to climb. HPA target: 70% CPU. By 12:58, CPU is at 95%, latency p99 is 2 seconds, users are abandoning. HPA reacts at minute 1 (scale 5→10), pods schedule (~30s), but Cluster Autoscaler also has to provision 2 new nodes (~90s). HPA reacts again at minute 2 (10→20), more nodes needed. By minute 4, system stable at 30 pods. Lunch peak = 4 minutes of degraded experience. Fix: scheduled scaling via KEDA cron — pre-warm to 25 replicas at 11:45 AM. Reactive HPA still handles the rest, but the floor is no longer 5.

Reactive auto-scaling alone is not enough for predictable load patterns. The mature pattern combines: scheduled scaling for known peaks (cron-based), reactive HPA for the rest, custom metrics where CPU isn't the right signal (Kafka lag, queue depth), and Karpenter for fast cost-aware node provisioning. This section walks through the K8s elasticity stack as it actually works in production.

44.1 The HPA on the right metric

From §14.3: scaling on CPU is the default but often wrong. The K8s implementations:

Metric sourceHow to use
CPU/memoryBuilt-in: type: Resource in HPA spec
Pod metrics (RPS, etc.)Prometheus Adapter exposes via custom metrics API
External (Kafka lag, SQS)KEDA scalers (best path); or Prometheus Adapter + external metrics
Object metrics (Ingress RPS)type: Object in HPA spec

KEDA — the modern way to do custom scaling

KEDA (Kubernetes Event-Driven Autoscaling) wraps HPA and adds 60+ built-in scalers. Especially useful for queue/event-driven workloads where CPU is a lagging indicator:

apiVersion: keda.sh/v1alpha1
kind: ScaledObject
metadata: { name: order-events-consumer, namespace: jahez-prod }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef: { name: order-events-consumer }
  minReplicaCount: 3
  maxReplicaCount: 30
  cooldownPeriod: 300            # 5 min after scaling down
  pollingInterval: 15           # check every 15s
  triggers:
  - type: kafka
    metadata:
      bootstrapServers: kafka.jahez-prod.svc:9092
      consumerGroup: order-processors
      topic: order.events
      lagThreshold: "1000"             # scale up if lag > 1000 msgs/consumer
  - type: cron                          # also pre-warm at peak
    metadata:
      timezone: Asia/Riyadh
      start: "45 11 * * *"
      end:   "30 14 * * *"
      desiredReplicas: "15"

44.2 The asymmetric scaling rule — fast up, slow down

From §14.2. K8s implementation via HPA's behavior field:

behavior:
  scaleUp:
    stabilizationWindowSeconds: 0         # no delay — go fast
    policies:
    - type: Percent
      value: 100                       # up to 100% increase per period
      periodSeconds: 30
    - type: Pods
      value: 5                         # or +5 pods, whichever is more
      periodSeconds: 30
    selectPolicy: Max                      # pick the more aggressive option
  scaleDown:
    stabilizationWindowSeconds: 300       # 5-min cooldown — be careful
    policies:
    - type: Percent
      value: 10                        # max 10% reduction per period
      periodSeconds: 60

44.3 VerticalPodAutoscaler — right-size, don't fight HPA

VPA recommends or auto-applies request/limit changes based on observed usage. Critical rule from §14.4: don't run VPA and HPA on the same metric. They will fight.

The clean combination: HPA on RPS or CPU, VPA on memory (recommendation mode):

apiVersion: autoscaling.k8s.io/v1
kind: VerticalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: order-svc-vpa }
spec:
  targetRef: { apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment, name: order-svc }
  updatePolicy:
    updateMode: "Off"           # start in recommend-only; review weekly
  resourcePolicy:
    containerPolicies:
    - containerName: "*"
      controlledResources: [memory]   # memory only — HPA owns CPU
      minAllowed:  { memory: "256Mi" }
      maxAllowed:  { memory: "4Gi" }

Run VPA in updateMode: "Off" initially. It generates recommendations you review during capacity planning (§49). Most teams find at least 30% over-provisioning that they can recover.

44.4 Cluster Autoscaler vs Karpenter

 Cluster Autoscaler (legacy)Karpenter (modern)
Provisioning modelPre-defined node groups (ASGs)Direct EC2 / GCE — picks instance type per pod set
Provisioning speed3-5 minutes30-60 seconds typical
Instance type flexibilityLimited to ASG typesWide — any instance type that fits
Spot integrationSeparate ASGNative; can mix spot + on-demand
Cost optimizationLimited bin-packingStrong — picks cheapest fit; consolidates
Where it runsMost cloudsAWS native; Azure beta; GKE has equivalent

For new clusters on AWS, Karpenter is the right choice. Significantly faster scale-up, 30-50% lower compute spend through smarter packing.

44.5 Karpenter NodePool example

apiVersion: karpenter.k8s.aws/v1
kind: NodePool
metadata: { name: stateless-spot }
spec:
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        workload-type: stateless-spot
    spec:
      requirements:
      - key: karpenter.sh/capacity-type
        operator: In
        values: [spot]                        # prefer spot
      - key: kubernetes.io/arch
        operator: In
        values: [amd64, arm64]                # either; cheaper at any moment
      - key: node.kubernetes.io/instance-type
        operator: In
        values: [m5.large, m5.xlarge, m6i.large, m6i.xlarge]
      expireAfter: 720h                          # 30d — replace nodes for security
  disruption:
    consolidationPolicy: WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized
    consolidateAfter: 30s                       # aggressive consolidation
  limits:
    cpu: "1000"                                  # max 1000 vCPU in this pool
---
# Pin stateless workloads to spot pool
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      nodeSelector:
        workload-type: stateless-spot
      tolerations:
      - { key: spot, operator: Exists, effect: NoSchedule }

44.6 Pod Priority Classes — who scales at the cost of who?

When the cluster is full, K8s decides which pods get scheduled and which get evicted. PriorityClasses give you control:

apiVersion: scheduling.k8s.io/v1
kind: PriorityClass
metadata: { name: jahez-critical }
value: 100000
globalDefault: false
description: "Critical user-facing services. Preempt batch jobs to schedule."
---
apiVersion: scheduling.k8s.io/v1
kind: PriorityClass
metadata: { name: jahez-batch }
value: 1000
globalDefault: true
description: "Default. Evictable to make room for higher priority."
---
# Critical services reference the priority class
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      priorityClassName: jahez-critical

Now if a node is full and a critical pod can't schedule, K8s evicts a batch pod first to make room. Combined with PDBs, this creates a clean priority hierarchy.

44.7 The cold-start mitigation pattern

From §14.6: new pods serve traffic at degraded performance. K8s mitigations:

  • Higher minReplicas — keep enough warm capacity that scale-out is additive, not the difference between working and broken
  • Scheduled pre-warming via KEDA cron — pre-scale before known peaks
  • Startup probe + warmup hook (§46.7) — pod doesn't enter Service rotation until truly warm
  • Service mesh slow-ramp — Istio's traffic-shifting can route 1% → 10% → 100% to a new pod over a minute

44.8 The Jahez calibration

order-svc — user-facing, predictable peak
  HPA:           min 8, max 50, target 70% CPU
  Schedule:      KEDA cron, 25 replicas during 11:45-14:30 daily
  Priority:      jahez-critical (preempts batch)
  Node pool:     50% spot, 50% on-demand mix

order-events-consumer — event-driven
  KEDA scaler:   Kafka lag > 1000 msgs/consumer
  Min/max:       3 / 30
  Priority:      jahez-standard
  Node pool:     spot only

batch-export-job — opportunistic
  Job spec:      parallelism 20, completions 1000
  Priority:      jahez-batch (lowest)
  Node pool:     spot only — happy to be preempted

44.9 Connection

For elasticity theory (HPA/VPA fundamentals, asymmetric scaling, the cold-start problem, predictive vs reactive), see §14. The next section (§45 Capacity Planning) covers how to know what your maxReplicas should actually be — and where the next bottleneck will appear.

§45 · Performance in K8sCapacity Planning in Kubernetes

Quick Reference · Capacity Planning in K8s

K8s gives you the levers to enforce capacity policies (ResourceQuotas, LimitRanges) and visibility into actual consumption. The discipline is using them: knowing your knee, sizing for headroom (the 70% rule), and identifying the NEXT bottleneck before it becomes a SEV-1. See §15 for capacity planning theory.

K8s primitives that enforce Capacity

ResourceQuota
Per-namespace caps
Total CPU/memory/pods/PVCs limit
LimitRange
Per-container defaults
Default + max requests/limits
PriorityClass + Preemption
Bin-packing under pressure
Higher-priority pods evict lower
Descheduler
Re-balance over time
Evicts pods from over-loaded nodes

Patterns & practices

ResourceQuota per namespace LimitRange defaults Load test → find knee 70% headroom rule N+M for deploy + failure Bound every queue
Quarterly capacity review Forecast 6-12 months out Track utilization per resource Identify bottleneck per service Plan migration before hitting wall

Real scenario · The bottleneck wasn't where the dashboard said

CPU at 65%, latency exploding, scaling pods doesn't help
order-svc shows healthy CPU around 65%. Friday night latency starts climbing. HPA scales 15 → 30 pods. Latency gets WORSE. Investigation: app exposes db_pool_in_use_total metric — pool size is 30, all in use, queue building. Each pod has its own pool of 30; 30 pods × 30 = 900 connections, but Postgres maxConnections = 200. New pods can't even open connections. The bottleneck is pool exhaustion at the DB, not pod CPU. Fix: deploy PgBouncer in front, set maxConnections high there, app pools point at PgBouncer. Real lesson: capacity isn't one number — measure every constrained resource.

Capacity planning in K8s is the discipline of measuring what your cluster can actually handle, enforcing limits so noisy neighbors don't blow it up, and forecasting when you'll hit the next wall. The hardest part is identifying the next bottleneck — by definition the one your current dashboards aren't watching.

45.1 The four questions, K8s edition

From §15.1, applied to K8s:

  1. What's our current capacity per service? — At what RPS does each service start to degrade? Need load tests, not guesses.
  2. What's our headroom? — How far is current peak from the knee? The 70% rule says you want to stay below 70% of the knee.
  3. How fast is demand growing? — Use predict_linear() on your RPS metrics. Forecast 6-12 months.
  4. Where's the next bottleneck? — When you scale past current limits, what runs out first? CPU, memory, DB connections, network egress, or quota?

45.2 ResourceQuota — bin the namespace

Without ResourceQuotas, one team's runaway can take down everyone else's services. The defensive posture is per-namespace caps:

apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata: { name: jahez-orders-quota, namespace: jahez-orders }
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "60"
    requests.memory: "120Gi"
    limits.cpu: "100"
    limits.memory: "200Gi"
    pods: "150"
    persistentvolumeclaims: "30"
    services.loadbalancers: "5"
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: LimitRange
metadata: { name: jahez-orders-limits, namespace: jahez-orders }
spec:
  limits:
  - type: Container
    defaultRequest: { cpu: "100m", memory: "128Mi" }
    default:        { cpu: "500m", memory: "512Mi" }
    max:            { cpu: "4",    memory: "4Gi" }
    min:            { cpu: "50m",  memory: "64Mi" }

The LimitRange ensures pods without explicit resource specs get sensible defaults, and the ResourceQuota caps the entire namespace. Together they bound the blast radius if a single deployment goes wild.

45.3 Finding the knee — load testing in K8s

From §15.3: theory says 70% utilization, but YOUR system's knee needs measurement. The K8s-native protocol:

  1. Spin up a load-test environment in K8s itself (use Argo Workflows or a simple Job)
  2. Use k6, Locust, or Vegeta in a Deployment to drive load against a target service in the same namespace
  3. Ramp RPS from low to high over 5-10 minutes; don't ramp too fast — queues need time to settle
  4. Plot RPS vs p99 latency. The knee is where p99 detaches from the trend
  5. Set HPA maxReplicas at 70% of the knee × pod-throughput
  6. Re-test quarterly; the knee shifts
# k6 load test as a K8s Job
apiVersion: batch/v1
kind: Job
metadata: { name: load-test-order-svc, namespace: jahez-loadtest }
spec:
  parallelism: 5                            # 5 generator pods in parallel
  template:
    spec:
      restartPolicy: Never
      containers:
      - name: k6
        image: grafana/k6:latest
        command: ["k6", "run", "-q", "/tests/checkout-ramp.js"]
        env:
        - { name: TARGET, value: "http://order-svc.jahez-prod.svc:80" }
        - { name: VUS_START, value: "50" }
        - { name: VUS_PEAK,  value: "5000" }
        - { name: DURATION,  value: "15m" }
        volumeMounts:
        - { name: tests, mountPath: /tests }

45.4 The per-resource view — what runs out first?

From §15.4: capacity is the minimum across all resources. Per-resource bottlenecks in K8s:

ResourceSymptomK8s metric to watch
CPUp99 climbs; throttling visiblecontainer_cpu_cfs_throttled_periods_total
MemoryOOMKilled; restartscontainer_memory_working_set_bytes approaches limit
Pods (per namespace)Quota exceeded errorskube_resourcequota usage vs hard
NodesPods Pending; no fitkube_pod_status_phase{phase="Pending"}
PVCs / IOPSSlow disk; iowaitkubelet_volume_stats_* + storage class IOPS
Network egressCross-AZ traffic spikes; costCloud provider network metrics
Service mesh sidecarLatency rises uniformlySidecar CPU usage; dropped connections
DB connection pool"pool exhausted" errorsApp-emitted metric — not in K8s
Etcd (cluster scale)API latency climbsetcd_disk_*, apiserver_request_duration

The cardinal rule: measure utilization of every constrained resource, not just CPU. If you're only watching CPU, the bottleneck will be elsewhere.

45.5 Cluster-level capacity signals

K8s itself has capacity signals you should monitor:

# Pods stuck Pending — usually scheduling issue (not enough room)
sum(kube_pod_status_phase{phase="Pending"}) by (namespace)

# Node CPU/memory utilization across the cluster
sum(rate(node_cpu_seconds_total{mode!="idle"}[5m])) by (node)
/
count(node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="idle"}) by (node)

# Namespace using % of its quota
kube_resourcequota{type="used"} / kube_resourcequota{type="hard"}

# OOMKilled count — capacity-related red flag
sum by (pod, namespace) (rate(kube_pod_container_status_terminated_reason{reason="OOMKilled"}[1h]))

45.6 The N+M rule for K8s

From §15.8: peak load + survive failure during deploy. In K8s terms:

  • Set HPA minReplicas = N+M — never let auto-scaler take you below the safe floor
  • PDB minAvailable = N — no voluntary disruption can take you below peak capacity
  • Topology spread maxSkew = 1 — survive zone failure (loses ~1/3 of N+M; remaining must still cover N)

For a service needing N=10 pods at peak: set minReplicas=12, maxReplicas=50, PDB minAvailable=10, spread across 3 AZs. During deploy with maxUnavailable=1, you have 11 of 12 always serving — and N=10 is met even if a zone fails simultaneously.

45.7 Capacity forecasting in K8s

The Prometheus pattern from §15.7:

# Peak RPS forecast 30 days out, by service
predict_linear(
  max_over_time(
    sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m])) by (service)
  [1d:5m]),
  30 * 86400
)

# When will namespace memory quota be exceeded?
predict_linear(
  kube_resourcequota{namespace="jahez-orders", resource="memory", type="used"}[14d],
  90 * 86400
) > on(namespace, resource)
kube_resourcequota{type="hard"}

The rule: forecast every constrained resource, not just one. Different bottlenecks reach saturation at different rates.

45.8 The Jahez quarterly capacity review

The mature SRE practice — every quarter, the team writes:

JAHEZ Q4 2025 CAPACITY REVIEW

CURRENT STATE
  Cluster size:        80 nodes (m5.2xlarge mix), ~1,200 pods
  Cluster CPU avg:     45% (peak 72% at lunch)
  Cluster memory avg:  58% (peak 81% at lunch)

SERVICE-LEVEL HEADROOM
  order-svc:    peak 4,200 RPS / knee 5,800 RPS  → 72% (pressure zone)
  payment-svc:  peak 1,100 RPS / knee 2,500 RPS  → 44% (healthy)
  kitchen-svc:  peak   800 RPS / knee 1,800 RPS  → 44% (healthy)

BOTTLENECKS IDENTIFIED
  order-svc → DB pool exhaustion at +15% growth
  Cluster nodes → 5,000-node EKS limit not relevant for 12+ months
  ECR image pulls → bottleneck during fleet-wide deploy (8 min lag)

FORECAST (Q4 2026, +50% YoY)
  order-svc would exceed knee → scale via PgBouncer + read replica expansion
  Cluster CPU peak ~95% if no node growth → grow node count to ~120

ACTION ITEMS BY Q1 2026
  1. Deploy PgBouncer + raise app pool size (2 sprints)
  2. Add 2 RDS read replicas for analytics (1 sprint)
  3. Increase ResourceQuota for jahez-orders namespace (this week)
  4. Re-run order-svc load test post-PgBouncer deploy
  5. Investigate ECR pull-through cache for fleet-wide deploy speed

45.9 Connection

For capacity planning theory (the four questions, 70% rule, knee detection, bound-every-queue, asymmetric capacity for cascade recovery), see §15. The K8s primitives covered here let you ENFORCE your plan; the discipline of measuring, forecasting, and acting on it is yours. The next stage covers the Data family — durability and consistency on K8s.

§46 · Pillars in K8sMetrics inside Kubernetes

A real cluster has metrics flowing from at least five distinct sources. You need all of them — confusing one for another is how outages get missed.

Five distinct metric sources in any Kubernetes cluster.

The five sources, source by source

K8s Metric Sources
1/5

1. node-exporter — node-level Linux metrics

A DaemonSet running one pod per node. It reads /proc and /sys on the host and exposes Linux-level metrics: CPU per core, memory, disk I/O, network bytes, filesystem space, load average.

Why a DaemonSet: /proc and /sys are per-node — you need one exporter per host with hostPath mounts.

Sample metrics:

node_cpu_seconds_total{cpu="0",mode="idle"}
node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes
node_filesystem_avail_bytes{mountpoint="/"}
node_network_receive_bytes_total{device="eth0"}
node_load1  # 1-min load average

2. kubelet / cAdvisor — per-container metrics

Every kubelet has a built-in cAdvisor (Container Advisor) that reads cgroup data and exposes per-container CPU, memory, network, and filesystem usage. No installation needed — comes with kubelet.

Endpoint: https://<node-ip>:10250/metrics/cadvisor (TLS, requires service account auth).

container_cpu_usage_seconds_total{pod="order-svc-7d8",container="app"}
container_memory_working_set_bytes{pod="order-svc-7d8"}
container_network_receive_bytes_total{pod="order-svc-7d8"}
container_fs_writes_bytes_total

This is what HPA uses. When you set CPU-based autoscaling, the metrics-server reads cAdvisor metrics through the kubelet.

3. kube-state-metrics — K8s object state

A standalone Deployment that talks to the K8s API and exports metrics about Kubernetes objects themselves — not container resource usage, but object state. Are deployments at the desired replica count? Are pods Pending? Are PVCs bound?

kube_deployment_status_replicas_available{deployment="order-svc"}
kube_deployment_status_replicas_unavailable{deployment="order-svc"}
kube_pod_status_phase{pod="order-svc-7d8",phase="Pending"}
kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total
kube_persistentvolumeclaim_status_phase
kube_horizontalpodautoscaler_status_current_replicas

Critical for alerting on K8s-level problems: "deployment has 0 ready replicas," "PVC stuck Pending," "pod restart loop."

4. Control-plane metrics

Every control-plane component exposes /metrics:

  • kube-apiserver — request rate, latency, errors per resource type. Critical: if this is slow, the entire cluster is slow.
  • etcd — read/write latency, leader changes, DB size. The most fragile component.
  • kube-controller-manager — controller queue depths.
  • kube-scheduler — pods scheduled per second, scheduling latency.
apiserver_request_duration_seconds_bucket
etcd_disk_wal_fsync_duration_seconds
etcd_server_leader_changes_seen_total
scheduler_pod_scheduling_duration_seconds

On managed K8s (EKS, GKE, AKS) some of these are exposed via the cloud provider's metrics API rather than directly scrapable.

5. Application metrics — RED + business KPIs

Your code instruments itself with a Prometheus client library or OTel SDK and exposes /metrics on a port. Prometheus discovers these pods via K8s service discovery and scrapes them.

# RED metrics from your service:
http_requests_total{route="/api/orders",method="POST",status="200"}
http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{route="/api/orders"}

# Business metrics:
jahez_orders_placed_total{city="riyadh",restaurant_tier="gold"}
jahez_payment_failures_total{provider="mada",reason="timeout"}
jahez_active_drivers{city="jeddah"}

Discovery happens via annotations on your Pod or Service: prometheus.io/scrape: "true".

Service discovery — how Prometheus finds your pods

Prometheus uses the K8s API as a service-discovery source. You declare what to look for in scrape_configs using kubernetes_sd_configs, and Prometheus continuously rebuilds its target list from the API.

# prometheus.yml — scrape config that finds pods with annotation
scrape_configs:
- job_name: 'kubernetes-pods'
  kubernetes_sd_configs:
  - role: pod          # also: service, endpoints, node, ingress

  relabel_configs:
  # Only scrape pods with prometheus.io/scrape: "true"
  - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape]
    action: keep
    regex: true

  # Use the pod's annotated port
  - source_labels: [__address__, __meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_port]
    action: replace
    regex: ([^:]+)(?::\d+)?;(\d+)
    replacement: $1:$2
    target_label: __address__

  # Inject namespace, pod, deployment as labels (low cardinality only)
  - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_namespace]
    target_label: namespace
  - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app_kubernetes_io_name]
    target_label: app
  
  # Drop the pod name (changes every deploy → cardinality bomb)
  - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_name]
    action: replace
    target_label: pod_instance   # keep but don't aggregate on it

Full example — instrumenting a Jahez service

This is a complete kubectl apply-ready Deployment for an app that exposes Prometheus metrics, plus the ServiceMonitor that tells the Prometheus Operator to scrape it.

# =====================================
# order-svc — Deployment with /metrics
# =====================================
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: order-svc
  namespace: jahez-prod
  labels:
    app.kubernetes.io/name: order-svc
    app.kubernetes.io/version: v2.14.3
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app.kubernetes.io/name: order-svc
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app.kubernetes.io/name: order-svc
        app.kubernetes.io/version: v2.14.3
      annotations:
        # Prometheus auto-discovery (if not using ServiceMonitor)
        prometheus.io/scrape: "true"
        prometheus.io/port:   "8080"
        prometheus.io/path:   "/metrics"
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: app
        image: ghcr.io/jahez/order-svc:v2.14.3
        ports:
        - name: http
          containerPort: 8080
        - name: metrics       # named port — referenced by ServiceMonitor
          containerPort: 8080
        env:
        - name: OTEL_SERVICE_NAME
          value: "order-svc"
        - name: OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES
          value: "deployment.environment=prod,region=ksa"
        resources:
          requests: { cpu: "200m", memory: "256Mi" }
          limits:   { cpu: "1000m", memory: "512Mi" }
        readinessProbe:
          httpGet: { path: /readyz, port: 8080 }
          periodSeconds: 5
        livenessProbe:
          httpGet: { path: /healthz, port: 8080 }
          periodSeconds: 10
---
# =====================================
# Service in front of the pods
# =====================================
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: order-svc
  namespace: jahez-prod
  labels:
    app.kubernetes.io/name: order-svc
spec:
  selector:
    app.kubernetes.io/name: order-svc
  ports:
  - name: http
    port: 80
    targetPort: 8080
  - name: metrics
    port: 8080
    targetPort: metrics
---
# =====================================
# ServiceMonitor — tells Prometheus Operator to scrape
# (only if you've installed kube-prometheus-stack)
# =====================================
apiVersion: monitoring.coreos.com/v1
kind: ServiceMonitor
metadata:
  name: order-svc
  namespace: jahez-prod
  labels:
    release: kube-prometheus-stack   # must match Operator selector
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app.kubernetes.io/name: order-svc
  namespaceSelector:
    matchNames: [jahez-prod]
  endpoints:
  - port: metrics
    interval: 15s
    path: /metrics
    relabelings:
    - sourceLabels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app_kubernetes_io_version]
      targetLabel: version

HPA — when metrics drive autoscaling

Once you have metrics, the Horizontal Pod Autoscaler can scale based on them. There are three flavors:

SourceUse forBacked by
Resource metricsCPU%, memory% (built-in)metrics-server (cAdvisor)
Custom metricsApp-specific Prometheus metricsprometheus-adapter
External metricsOutside the cluster (SQS depth, Pub/Sub)KEDA
 
# HPA scaling on a custom Prometheus metric (RPS per pod)
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: order-svc
  namespace: jahez-prod
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: order-svc
  minReplicas: 3
  maxReplicas: 30
  metrics:
  - type: Pods
    pods:
      metric:
        name: http_requests_per_second   # exposed via prometheus-adapter
      target:
        type: AverageValue
        averageValue: "100"             # 100 RPS per pod target
  behavior:
    scaleUp:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 30
    scaleDown:
      stabilizationWindowSeconds: 300   # 5 min cooldown

§47 · Pillars in K8sLogs in Kubernetes logs

In Kubernetes, your app does one thing: write JSON to stdout. Everything else — collection, enrichment with pod metadata, parsing, shipping, indexing — is the platform's job.

16.1 The journey of a log line

Five-stage K8s log pipeline. The app's responsibility ends at stdout.

16.2 Why DaemonSet, not sidecar?

DaemonSet (recommended)

  • One agent per node — collects logs for every pod on that node
  • Resource cost: ~50–100 MB RAM per node, regardless of pod count
  • Reads /var/log/pods/ via hostPath mount
  • Pod metadata enrichment via K8s API (cached)
  • Workload doesn't care — apps just write to stdout

Sidecar (rare cases only)

  • One agent per pod — duplicated everywhere
  • Cost: ~50 MB × number of pods (huge at scale)
  • Use only when: app writes to a file (not stdout) and you can't change it, or you need per-pod custom parsing
  • Each sidecar adds startup time, failure surface, cost

16.3 Fluent-bit DaemonSet — full manifest

This is the real thing. Apply it and your cluster has logs.

# fluent-bit ConfigMap — defines the pipeline
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: fluent-bit-config
  namespace: monitoring
data:
  fluent-bit.conf: |
    [SERVICE]
        Daemon          Off
        Flush           5
        Log_Level       info
        Parsers_File    parsers.conf
        HTTP_Server     On
        HTTP_Listen     0.0.0.0
        HTTP_Port       2020

    [INPUT]
        Name              tail
        Tag               kube.*
        Path              /var/log/containers/*.log
        Parser            cri
        DB                /var/log/flb_kube.db
        Mem_Buf_Limit     50MB
        Skip_Long_Lines   On
        Refresh_Interval  5

    [FILTER]
        Name                kubernetes
        Match               kube.*
        Kube_URL            https://kubernetes.default.svc:443
        Kube_CA_File        /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/ca.crt
        Kube_Token_File     /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token
        Merge_Log           On
        Merge_Log_Key       app_log
        K8S-Logging.Parser  On
        K8S-Logging.Exclude On
        Annotations         Off
        Labels              On

    # Drop noisy health-check logs to save storage
    [FILTER]
        Name    grep
        Match   kube.*
        Exclude $app_log['path'] ^/healthz$

    # Add cluster-wide labels
    [FILTER]
        Name    modify
        Match   kube.*
        Add     cluster jahez-prod-ksa
        Add     region  me-central-1

    [OUTPUT]
        Name                   loki
        Match                  kube.*
        Host                   loki.monitoring.svc.cluster.local
        Port                   3100
        Labels                 cluster=$cluster, namespace=$kubernetes['namespace_name'], app=$kubernetes['labels']['app'], pod=$kubernetes['pod_name']
        Label_Keys             $level, $kubernetes['container_name']
        Line_Format            json
        Auto_Kubernetes_Labels Off
        Tenant_ID              jahez

  parsers.conf: |
    [PARSER]
        Name        cri
        Format      regex
        Regex       ^(?<time>[^ ]+) (?<stream>stdout|stderr) (?<logtag>[^ ]*) (?<message>.*)$
        Time_Key    time
        Time_Format %Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S.%L%z
---
# DaemonSet — one fluent-bit pod per node
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: DaemonSet
metadata:
  name: fluent-bit
  namespace: monitoring
  labels: { app: fluent-bit }
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: fluent-bit }
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: { app: fluent-bit }
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: fluent-bit
      tolerations:                          # run on every node, including masters
      - operator: Exists
      containers:
      - name: fluent-bit
        image: fluent/fluent-bit:2.2.0
        imagePullPolicy: IfNotPresent
        resources:
          requests: { cpu: "100m", memory: "128Mi" }
          limits:   { cpu: "500m", memory: "512Mi" }
        ports:
        - containerPort: 2020
          name: http
        volumeMounts:
        - name: varlog
          mountPath: /var/log
          readOnly: true
        - name: varlibdockercontainers
          mountPath: /var/lib/docker/containers
          readOnly: true
        - name: config
          mountPath: /fluent-bit/etc/
      volumes:
      - name: varlog
        hostPath: { path: /var/log }     # THE key bit — read host's container logs
      - name: varlibdockercontainers
        hostPath: { path: /var/lib/docker/containers }
      - name: config
        configMap: { name: fluent-bit-config }
---
# RBAC — fluent-bit needs to read pod metadata
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata: { name: fluent-bit, namespace: monitoring }
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: ClusterRole
metadata: { name: fluent-bit-read }
rules:
- apiGroups: [""]
  resources: ["namespaces", "pods"]
  verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: ClusterRoleBinding
metadata: { name: fluent-bit-read }
roleRef:
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
  kind: ClusterRole
  name: fluent-bit-read
subjects:
- kind: ServiceAccount
  name: fluent-bit
  namespace: monitoring
What just happened? Fluent-bit pods now run on every node, tail every container's stdout file, parse the CRI format, ask the K8s API "who owns this pod?", attach namespace, app, pod labels, and ship to Loki. Zero app changes required.

16.4 Structured logging in your app — the only thing you should care about

The platform handles transport. You are responsible for what gets written. Three rules:

  1. JSON, not plain text. Loki can parse JSON fields and filter on them.
  2. Always include trace_id if a request is in flight — that's how you correlate logs to traces.
  3. Levels matter — DEBUG/INFO/WARN/ERROR. WARN+ should be alertable.
// order-svc — Node.js with pino
import pino from 'pino';
const log = pino({
  level: process.env.LOG_LEVEL || 'info',
  formatters: {
    level: (label) => ({ level: label })   // "level":"info" not "level":30
  },
  timestamp: pino.stdTimeFunctions.isoTime
});

// In a request handler:
log.info({
  trace_id: ctx.traceId,            // from OTel
  span_id:  ctx.spanId,
  user_id:  user.id,
  order_id: order.id,
  city:     order.city,             // "riyadh" — useful filter
  amount:   order.totalSar,
  payment:  'mada',
  event:    'order.placed'
}, 'order placed successfully');

// Result on stdout:
// {"level":"info","time":"2026-04-28T10:14:22.331Z","trace_id":"4bf92f...",
//  "user_id":"u_8821","order_id":"ord_551","city":"riyadh","amount":87.5,
//  "payment":"mada","event":"order.placed","msg":"order placed successfully"}

16.5 LogQL — querying logs like Prometheus queries metrics

LogQL has two halves: label selectors (indexed, fast) and filter expressions (full-text scan, slower).

QueryWhat it does
{namespace="jahez-prod", app="order-svc"}All logs from order-svc — the cheapest query
{app="order-svc"} |= "ERROR"Filter to lines containing "ERROR"
{app="order-svc"} | json | level="error"Parse JSON, filter where level field = error
{app="order-svc"} | json | city="riyadh" | amount > 200Riyadh orders over 200 SAR
sum by (app) (rate({namespace="jahez-prod"} |= "ERROR" [5m]))Error-rate per service — yes, metrics from logs
{app="order-svc"} | json | trace_id="4bf92f43..."Every log line for a single request — correlation
